Agricultural Development;  
b) No-objection letter (s) issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal 
point(s);  
c) Environmental and Social report(s) disclosure ;  
d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel ’s assessment;

asse ssment; and  
f) Gender documentation  of the funding proposal . 
These documents are presented as submitted by the accredited entity and the national designated 
authority(ies) or focal point(s), respectively. Pursuant to the Comprehensive Information Disclosure P olicy 
of the Fund, the funding proposal titled “ Africa Rural Climate Adaptation Finance Mechanism (ARCAFIM) 
for East Africa region ” submitted by International Fund for Agricultural Development  is being circulated on 
a limited distribution basis only to B oard Members and Alternate Board Members  to ensure confidentiality 
of certain proprietary, legally privileged,  or commercially sensitive  information of the entity.

Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template  
• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross -
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.  
• Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annex es must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60 . Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the  usual 
service standard time.  
• The rec ommended font is Arial, size 11.  
• Under  the GCF Information Disclosure Policy , project and programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. 
Accredited Entities are asked to fill o ut information on disclosure in section G.4 .

fundingproposal@gcfund.org

“FP-[Accredited Entity Short Name] -[Country/Region] -[YYYY/MM/DD ]”

 A.21. Executive summary  
A progressive but rapid shift in agricultural practices in Africa will be critical as harsh climatic conditions will increase i n 
most countries of the region  and new climatic threats for crop production and livestock keeping are projected to emerge. 
By 2030  - 2040, new and innovative agricultural systems need to be implemented in many regions where the climate 
will soon be no longer suitable for current practi ces. The lack of adequate and appropriate private sector financing for 
Climate Change Adaptation ( CCA ) investments jeopardizes  the materialization of this transformation process in Sub -
Saharan Africa.  Currently, there is a gap of  well-functioning and repli cable, significant scale private sector CCA finance 
programmes  across  the Africa n region s, although some new interventions are emerging. The CCA finance efforts led 
by public sector , on the other hand, are often  grant -based  and have limited capacity to leverage sustainable long term 
market driven financing . 
With the Africa Rural Climate Adaptation Finance Mechanism (ARCAFIM) , IFAD’s  aim is to introduce a practical, widely 
implementable model for achieving  a paradigm shift towards systematic large -scale use of private financial resources 
for rural CCA investment s, the impact of which would go well beyond the direct impacts and time frame of the 
Programme. The key implementing/executing partners of IFAD are the  regional ARCAFIM “ Host Banks”, which are 
leading commercial banks in the regions and which co -finance the programme investments. IFAD, as a GCF Accredited 
Entity, will on-lend the funds provided for ARCAFIM by GCF and other international financiers and be  the lender of 
record of the ARCAFIM funds to the Host Banks. IFAD will disburse the funds for ARCAFIM on -lending to the regional 
Host Banks.  The IFAD ARCAFIM loans will be matched 1:1 by the Host Banks, thus doubling the ARCAFIM financing 
resources. Suppo rted by a ppropriate  tracking and monitoring arrangement , IFAD will monitor and oversee that Host 
Banks will use the loan proceeds exclusively for on -lending under the ARCAFIM programme to the targeted 
beneficiaries based on agreements and definitions of th e Loan Agreements between IFAD and the Host Banks.  
Operating with a projected total budget of US$ 600 million , the ARCAFIM  overall development goal is “ Wider  use of 
commercial resources for Climate Change Adaptation  in the region, enhancing  the well-being of rural communities 
through climate resilient livelihoods ”. To achieve this, the two programme Outcomes are: (i) ARCAFIM PFIs expand 
and mainstream CCA finance in the region resulting in increased CC resilience and improved food security levels  of 
rural communities ; and (ii) Improved capacity of PFIs and agriculture value chain participants to adapt to risks imposed 
by climatic changes . These Outcomes are achieved by a set of activities that include deployment of tailored financial 
products and offer of dedicated technical assistance to all stakeholders in the targeted African countries. .  
With this application  to GCF , IFAD seeks from the Green Climate Fund the approval for GCF financing share of 
US$ 55 million (US$ 45 million loan, US$  10 millio n TA grant) for the ARCAFIM Phase 1 operations in the East Africa 
Region . The planned, phased expansion of ARCAFIM and the progress in programme preparations in the four regions 
is described in the table below.  IFAD also seek s a confirmation on the GCF’s interest in co -financing this systematic 
large -scale promotion of private sector CCA finance with the next phases of ARCAFIM in the four African regions (East, 
South, North and West/Central).

 Under the ARCAFIM framework, the same model, programme structure and key activities will be used in each region 
to achieve the programme targets, with adjustments to the local climatical, economic and financial sector environments. 
This model is scalable a nd replicable with a vision to become a leading co -investment platform  for CCA finance targeting 
small producers and rural poo r. The key objective is to achieve a demonstration effect and paradigm shift by designing 
approaches and methodologies for private  sector CCA lending targeting small producers and rural poo r, and proof of 
concept for implementation at scale.  
For Phase 1 to be implemented in the East Africa Region  with the total budget of US$  200 million (GCF share 
US$ 55 million) , the target ARCAFIM Phase 1 countries are Kenya, Tanzania, Uganda  and Rwanda, selected for the 
reasons that (i) they are either Low Income Countries or Lower Middle Income Countries; (ii) all are under severe 
climate change stress, higher compared to average in develo ping countries as stated by the IPCC 2022 report on 
Climate Change Impacts, Adaptation and Vulnerability; and finally (iii) in the target countries, there is a substantial 
market of small -scale agriculture production with high potential for rural CCA finan cing.  The Host Bank for the ARCAFIM  
Phase 1 operations will be Equity Bank (Kenya ) Limited  (EBK), subsidiary of Equity Group Holdings PLC  (EGH) . EBK 
is the Borrower as well as the Executing Entity for ARCAFIM for East Africa . EBK will on -lend the loan received from 
IFAD to Equity Bank Uganda  Limited (EBUL) , Equity Bank Rwanda  PLC (EBR) , and Equity Bank (Tanzania ) Limited  
(EBTL)  through sub -loan agreements to be executed between EBK and each of the three country subsidiaries  of EGH . 
EGH  is the largest ba nking group in the region  with a high level of ESG integration in its operations, a strategic focus 
to increase its rural and  agricultural portfolio,  track -record of micro lending in the agriculture sector,  a strong balance 
sheet  to match the international capital investments , and ability  to hedge funding in US$ and disburse loans to low -
income borrowers in local currencies.   
To achieve the ARCAFIM obj ectives, the programme activities in East African and later in other regions are structured 
into two mutually reinforcing components: (a) Component  1: Effective Financing for Rural Adaptation Targeting Small 
Producers and Rural MSMEs; and (b) Component 2:  Innovations and Capacity for Rural Adaptation  (collectively, the 
Funded Activities) .  
In East Africa, Component 1  will provide catalytic international financial resources of US$ 90 million (50%  - US$ 45 
million - from the GCF, 50%  - US$ 45 million - from the IFAD -organized  co-financiers) to the Host Bank for on -lending 
for CCA purposes in the programme target countries. The total ARCAFIM on -lending resources  in this region will  be 
US$ 180 million, which includes a matching amount o f US$ 90 million financed by the Host Bank. The delivery of the 
CCA loans to the final target groups will take place either directly through the Host Bank branch/agency networks or 
through different types of rural on -lending financial intermediaries. To su pport Component  1 implementation, ARCAFIM 
will provide a Taxonomy of  investment categories which are justified and eligible for CCA finance end -use in this private 
sector operation. The developed CCA Taxonomy will be critical for the later mainstreaming and replication of the 
ARCAFIM CCA finance model. To further encourage the active use of ARCAFIM lending capital, t he proposed financial 
structure of the programme includes credit risk sharing between  IFAD, financially supported by  international partners , 
including GCF  and the Host Bank.  
Component 2 , with grant -based funding of US$ 20 million  in East Africa , will promote critical innovations and address 
non-financial barriers currently preventing greater CCA financing in the ARCAFIM target regions. Firstly , it will 
strengthen the awareness and capacity of Participating Financial Institutions (PFIs) to offer CCA fina ncial services  to 
the small prod ucer and MSME markets in the climate -vulnerable rural areas . Secondly , it will develop the CCA 
investment capacities and financial literacy skills of small -scale producers and rural MSMEs. Thirdly , ARCAFIM will 
improve climate finance enabling environment through CCA finance policy dialogues.  Gender and social inclusion 
(GESI) dimensions will be mainstreamed throughout this process,  To promote wider  application of the ARCAFIM model 
outside the direct ARCAFIM stakeholders, IFAD, the other co -financiers , and the Host Bank will proactively share 
knowledge and information of the ARCAFIM performance and achieved results through various  regio nal and 
international networks.  Successful implementation of the programme is expected to encourage African financial 
institutions and international financiers to re -visit their policies on climate finance and seek new rural business 
opportunities based on climate smart agricultural practices and modern CC adaptation technologies , designed and 
delivered with a GESI lens . 
The overall Programme description presented below provides a framework for the ARCAFIM Mechanism 
implementation in all the four AFRICAN regions. The presentation also includes the climate context, detailed budgets, 
implementation targets and expected programme results for the Phase 1 operations in East Africa, for which GCF co -
financing is sought under this application.

2. In East Africa,  the climate zones covering the programme’s target countries (Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania and Uganda) 
are diverse, ranging from arid to temperate to tropical. Temperatures are high and characterized by little seasonal 
variation across much of the region. Temperatur es are lower in the highland regions of Kenya and Tanzania. The 
highest temperatures are experienced in the drier interior regions towards the north and east, where average 
summer temperatures exceed 30°C3, cooling to around 26°C in coastal areas . There are three main rainfall regimes 
— in equatorial regions (much of the area described), there are two rainy seasons with peaks in October and April; 
in the north, the primary rainy season is June to August; and in the south the primary rainy season is  December to 
February. These seasons are linked to the annual north –south movement of the precipitation -inducing Inter Tropical 
Convergence Zone (ITCZ), which shifts toward warmer air bringing rainy seasons in the respective warm months 
of areas north and south of the equator. Additionally, precipitation is periodically influenced (every two to seven 
years) by the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO), leading to alternate periods of above - and below -average 
rainfall. Annual rainfall averages from nearly 0 mm  in parts of northwest Kenya to over 2,000 mm in highland areas 
of the region. There is substantial interannual and interdecadal rainfall variability, and both droughts and floods are 
common4.

B.1.b. Climate change problem  
 
Observed Climate Change  
 
3. There has been a clear trend of increasing average temperatures across East Africa  since the 1960s, while 
precipitation trends have been weak and variable. Observed trends for the regions with a focus on the project’s 
target countries is presented in  Table 2 .  
 
Table 2 Summary of observed climate change trends for East  Africa 10, 11, 12 
Climate variable  Observed trends  
Temperature  • Increased average temperatures of 1°C to 1.5°C since the 1960s, with the greatest warming in the 
region’s north and centre and least warming along coastal Kenya .  
• In Rwanda ’s south -west and eastern regions, temperatures increased by between 1.4°C and 2.6°C 
from 1971 to 2016.  
• Between 1960 and 2006, average temperatures in Tanzania  have increased by 1 ºC. 
• Average annual temperatures in Uganda  have increased by as much as 1.3°C since the 1950s.  
Rainfall  • Precipitation trends are weak and variable across th e region; however, evidence suggests a decrease 
in precipitation in the March –May period since the late 1970s in some areas (i.e., Central Kenya , and 
Uganda ). 
• Across the region, increased frequency, duration and intensity of heavy rainfall events.  
Drought and dry spells  • Increased frequency and severity of droughts since the 1990s (most notably in Kenya, Tanzania  and 
Uganda ). 
Glaciers  • Glacial surface area loss of 80% across Mt. Kenya (0.5 km2 to 0.1 km2), Mt. Kilimanjaro (6 km2 to ~1 
km2) and the Ru wenzori Mountains (~10.6 km2 to ~1.7 km2) from 1900 to 2010 . 
Sea-level rise  • From 1984 to 2016, observed average rise in sea level of 1.58 mm per year for Tanzania  (Zanzibar).  
• From 1986 to 2016, observed average rise in sea level of 3.46 mm per year for Kenya  (Mombasa) . 
 
Projected Climate Change   
4. In East Africa, climate models  indicate continued increases in average temperatures (and heat waves), as well as 
the frequency and intensity of heavy rainfall events. The future of overall precipitation changes is less certain, with 
most models showing increased precipitation for much of the region (with the exception of southern Tanzania) but 
with shifts in seasonality. Despite potential precipitation increases shown in some models, droughts are expected 
to intensify in the 21st century due to longer dry spells combined with increased evaporation and a greater proportion 
of precipitation coming in heavy rainfall events.

by 2050, particularly during the northeast monsoon period (except for southern Tanzania ) and with 
the greatest potential increases for Kenya . Under a mid -emissions scenario (SSP2 4.5), near term 
(2021 –2040) total precipitation is expected to increase by between 1.4% and 9.9% in Kenya  and 
Rwanda  and between 0.7% and 10.4%, in Tanzania  and Uganda  relative to a baseline period of 
1981 to 2010, as modelled for the IPCC’s AR6.  
• Shifts in rainfall seasonality for much of the region, including increased precipitation during October –
December (the short rainy season north of the equator) and de creased March –May precipitation (the 
long rainy season north of the equator).  
• An increase in interseasonal and interannual rainfall variability.  
Heavy rainfall  • Increased frequency and intensity of heavy precipitation events. Models project that maximum one -
day precipitation in Kenya under the SSP2 4.5 mid -emissions scenario, in the near term (2021 –
2040), will increase by 4.2% –16.5%, while in Rwanda, Tanzania  and Uganda  it will increase by 
2.6% –16.4%.  
• There is high confidence that extreme precipitation events will intensify across East Africa by 205015. 
Drought and dry spells  • Intensified drought occurrences.  
• Drought area in East Africa is likely to increase at the  end of the 21st century by 16%, 36%, and 54% 
under RCP 2.6, 4.5, and 8.5, respectively, with the areas affected by extreme drought increasing 
more rapidly than severe and moderate droughts.  
• Spatially, drought event, duration, frequency and intensity would increase in Tanzania, but generally 
decrease in Kenya, Uganda, and Rwanda16 
• There is high confidence that glaciers will disappear from East Africa by 2050.  
Glaciers  • There is high confidence that glaciers will disappear from East Africa by 2050.  
Sea-level rise  • There is high confidence that sea levels along the East African coastline will rise by 205017, with 
estimations varying between 16 cm to 46 cm ( Kenya  and Tanzania ) 
*The confidence relating to the Climatic Impact Drivers (CID) future changes summarized in this table, refers to the regional synthesis compiled by 
IPCC WGI. CIDs and confidence trends refer to a 20 -to-30-year period centred around 2050 and/or consistent with 2°C global warming, compared 
to a similar pe riod within 1960 -2014 or 1850 -190018. 
 
Climate change vulnerabilities and impacts  
6. Climate variability and change pose major obstacles to resilience in East Africa, where increasing temperatures and 
shifting rainfall patterns are already affecting economic growth, small producer livelihoods, food security, health, 
and ecosystems. The region is characterized by rapid urbanization at 4.0% –6.2% annually for much of the region, 
higher than sub -Saharan Africa as a whole, which is one of the world’s most rapidly urbanizing areas. More than 
70% of the population, however, still live in rural areas where population growth continues and people rely heavily 
on climate -sensitive rainfed agriculture  and pastoralism , especially small producers. Overall, agricultu re exceeds 
24% of GDP in all the region’s countries. Projections indicate that reduced crop productivity in much of the region 
is expected to increase poverty and food insecurity among populations, particularly among the already low -income 
small producers.  The combination of urbanization and declining agricultural productivity  and pastoralism  will 
adversely affect livelihoods, health, infrastructure, and food security. In the Lake Victoria Basin, home to 
approximately 45 million people across areas of Kenya , Rwanda, Tanzania, and Uganda, drought in recent years 
has already pushed more than 10% of the population toward chronic food insecurity. At the same time climate -
sensitive diseases like malaria are poised to expand into the warming East African highlands  and Lake Victoria 
region19.  
 
7. Crop Production:  Warming temperatures and changing precipitation patterns are expected to decrease subsistence 
and cash crop production, which remain a critical source of small producer livelihoods and economic activity acros s 
the region. Small producer farms account for up to 90% of crop production across the region and the main source 
of employment for the region’s 297 million rural residents. The dominant crops are a cereal –legume mix that 
includes maize, millet, sorghum, w heat, and beans; major cash crops include coffee and tea. The already low yields

22 USAID. 2020. Climate risk profile: East Africa. Online available: 
https://www.climatelinks.org/sites/default/files/asset/document/2020_USAID_ATLAS_CRP -East-Africa -Regional.pdf  
23 Available at: https://www.climatelinks.org/sites/default/files/asset/document/ 2017_USAID -PREPARED -TetraTech_Vulnerability -Impacts -
Adaptation -Assessment -East-Africa -Food -Security -Agriculture.pdf   resulting from low -input rainfed production with limited processing, storage, and transportation infrastructure make 
the vast majority of crop production highly vulnerable to changes in temperature and rainfall. Heat and unreliable 
rainfall  are expected to reduce maize, bean, banana, and plantain yields at lower elevations by the 2030s to 2050s. 
However, in higher elevations, temperature increases are projected to expand the areas for crop production 
potential. This expanded production poten tial could increase to cultivation pressure on mountain slopes and in 
protected areas , with implications for ecosystem services and biodiversity.  
  
8. Maize, which accounts for nearly half of calories consumed in the region, is expected to experience yield re ductions 
of 8% –37% across East Africa by the 2090s.  An average of between 600 mm and 1,200 mm of rainfall annually is 
generally sufficient for maize or cassava cultivation. Yet, with the projected increased irregularities in rain patterns , 
these crops are expected to be highly vulnerable to climate change20. Lowland bean production, vital for nutritional 
security, is expected to become unviable due to rising temperatures sometime after mid -century, particularly 
affecting Tanzania and Uganda. In drier areas of East Africa, even drought -resilient millet, sorghum, and cassava 
yields may become increasingly marginal. Wetter rainy seasons could prevent staple crops (e.g., maize, sweet 
potatoes) from maturing, although increased rainfall would be favourable for ot her crops (e.g., perennials and a 
range of fruits and vegetables). A warmer, wetter climate may also raise crop and postharvest losses due to 
heightened risk of mildew, leaf spot, and bacterial stem and root rot and increased fungus growth in stored seeds.  
Increasing heat and pest stress (e.g., coffee berry borer) are expected to diminish the area suitable for coffee and 
tea production by 20% -40% across countries including Kenya, Rwanda and Uganda by 2100. However, the areas 
suitable for coffee production a re expected to expand into higher elevations as well, which may have substantial 
negative environmental implications (e.g., deforestation, reduced water availability, nutrient loading in streams). For 
East Africa’s water -intensive cut flower industry, heat  and water stress, heavy rainfall resulting in soil degradation, 
and growing tensions over access to limited water resources threaten production. Along the coast, sea level rise is 
expected to increase water logging, salinization, and inundation of agricul tural lands with particular risk for mango, 
cashew, and coconut harvests21. 
 
9. Livestock : Rising temperatures, shifting rainfall patterns, droughts, and increased erratic heavy rainfall events are the 
most significant stressors facing the region’s main lives tock species: cattle, sheep, goats, camels, and poultry. East 
Africa’s livestock are integral for the region’s culture, as well as food security and livelihoods, particularly in arid and 
semi -arid lands, and contribute significantly to GDP. Increasingly un reliable rainfall patterns and frequently occurring 
droughts have had a considerable impact on grazing availability, fodder quality and livestock production in these 
regions. As climate variability and change, including heat and water stress, make crop pro duction more tenuous, 
livestock production is expected to replace areas of mixed crop –livestock systems in some coastal and mid -
elevation areas by the 2050s. While typically more resilient to climate variability and shocks than crops, livestock 
are suscept ible to heat stress, and their viability is affected by the productivity and availability of pasture, feed 
production, water availability, and pest and disease dynamics. Recurrent climate -related shocks are already leading 
to critical livestock losses.  Rising temperatures and flooding could also increase the incidence of diseases, such as 
anthrax and Rift Valley Fever, and expand the range of ticks carrying diseases such as East Coast Fever. Increased 
seasonal and drought -related water stress, alongside warming trends, have already impacted pastures, fodder 
crops, and water availability in some areas, including the Lake Victoria Basin. For pastoralist communities, climate -
related changes in pasture and water availability could alter pastoral mobility, exa cerbating the already on -going 
tensions over land and water resources in the region22. 
 
10. Fisheries:  Climate change will affect fisheries in the ARCAFIM target areas in diverse ways. The key variables 
affecting marine and inland fisheries in East Africa inc lude changes in water temperature, precipitation, salinity, river 
flow, nutrient levels, lake levels, storm frequency and intensity, and flooding. Aquaculture, increasingly important to 
food security in the region, is highly sensitive to climate change var iables because cultured fish are less able to 
adapt to changes by migrating or by changing their ranges to occupy more favourable conditions23. The continuing 
reliance on marine resources has placed strain on the fisheries productivity along the western In dian Ocean, with

temperatures, rising sea levels and intensified tropical storms have been of notable concerns for artisanal fishing

sector in the targ eted East African countries that simultaneously face major challenges in feeding their fast -growing 
populations with the products from the crop cultivation  and livestock sub -sectors.  
 
11. Rural lives:  Climate change is not only one of the greatest environmental and development challenges facing the 
world today, but also one that will have critical impacts on human rights and inequalities, including gender inequality. 
Women and girls are impacted dispro portionately from the effects of climate change due to factors such as 
entrenched discriminatory social and cultural norms, a lack of entitlements to land and other credit assets and 
unequal access to land, water and productive assets, compounded by limite d mobility and decision -making power 
in many contexts. Their mortality from climate -related disasters is higher than that of men and climate -induced 
floods and droughts exacerbate women’s poverty and unpaid domestic and care work burdens.  Yet in many Afri can 
countries, women are recognized as agents of change who make valuable contributions to climate change 
mitigation and adaptation efforts.  
 
Micro, Small and Medium Sized Enterprises in Food Systems  
12. The majority of businesses involved in food systems in East Africa are Micro, Small and Medium Size Enterprises 
(MSMEs) and perform various functions in the food value chains . According to a recent study by AGRA24, trading , 
logistics  and processing constitute about 40% of the total gross value of the agri -food system in the region, equal 
to the share coming from farms , while  retailers constitute the remaining 20% . Increased weather unpredictability 
and frequency of extreme events will continue to have an acute impact on the se critical  MSME operations in the 
food system, especially when  they are  involved with small primary producers across the crop production, livestock 
and fisheries sectors. For the MSMEs in particular, the impacts include disruptions in raw material supply chain 
planning, increased prices of pr imary producer outputs, and destabilized markets for agriculture services including 
mechanization, logistics, storage and trade.25 As temperatures rise and extreme weather events come more 
frequent due to climate change, the already -insufficient transporta tion infrastructure will become more vulnerable 
and further complicate the operations of rural MSMEs.  The ability of MSMEs in food value chains to adapt and 
respond to climate change will be critically important considering their importance to the Eastern African econom ies 
in their roles of providers of services and employment in rural communities26.  
 
Demand and Supply of Rural and Agriculture Finance for Climate Adaptation  
13. In order to estimate the CCA finance demand in the targeted countries,  a demand side analysis has been conducted 
through  both top-down macroeconomic modelling and a comprehensive and extensive stakeholder consultation in 
each country. The below summary of  the top-down macroeconomic modelling shows that the potential  for CCA 
investments in agriculture is at a significantly higher level compared to resources made available by ARCAFIM :.

A supply -side analysis  was also conducted, as part of the same study,  to identify the gap between supply and 
demand in the targeted countries. Overall, the landscape for agricultural finance in East Africa is nascent. Although 
an increasing number financial institutions (FIs) (including commercial banks, microfinance instit utions and 
SACCOs) are offering agricultural lending products,  the conditions for accessing these loans including high -interest 
rates, collateral requirements, and short repayment periods often excluded smallholder farmers, and indicated a 
reluctance by FI s to lend to this segment of the market. High interest rates and short repayment periods were both 
also commonly cited as barriers by smallholder farmer associations in the four East African countries. Data collected 
through interviews with financial insti tutions indicated that there are currently very limited financial products targeted 
to climate change adaptation. The interest rates and loan tenors given by FIs and those perceived by farmers’ 
association are different, with farmers often perceiving them to be higher and shorter than FI’s information. Interest 
rates noted by respondents in the region vary widely,  but in most cases, stated annual interest rates are between

B.1.c. Proposed solution : Africa Rural Climate Adaptation Finance Mechanism (ARCAFIM)  
 
14. A progressive but rapid shift in agricultural practices in East Africa will be critical as harsh climatic conditions will 
increase in most countries of the region and new climatic threats for crop production, livestock keeping, and fisheries 
are projected t o emerge , as described in the above sections B.1.a and B.1.b . By 2030  - 2040, new and innovative 
agricultural systems need to be implemented in many regions where the climate will soon be no longer suitable for 
current practices. The lack of adequate and a ppropriate private sector financing for CCA investments is a serious 
threat for the materialization of this transformation process.  
 
15. Building on l essons learned and best practices from  ongoing climate change adaptation programming27, mainly 
through the pub lic sector, IFAD proposes a new private sector led  Africa Rural Climate Adaptation Finance 
Mechanism (ARCAFIM) , with the  aim is to introduce a practical, widely implementable model for achieving a 
paradigm shift towards systematic large -scale use of privat e financial resources for rural CCA investments . Through 
the projected paradigm shift, t he impact of ARCAFIM  would go well beyond the direct impacts and time frame of 
the Programme. In the next Section, t he identified underlying overall key barriers for increasing the volumes of CCA 
financing and investments in the target area are described, leading to the prese ntation of the ARCAFIM Theory of 
Change (TOC) and the Programme’s overreaching impact logic. This is followed by the detailed descriptions of the 
ARCAFIM targets, its key components and activities, implementation approach and resource use, as well as longe r 
term strategies in reaching the wider paradigm change objectives.   
 
16. The overall Programme description presented below provides a  framework for the ARCAFIM  Mechanism  
implementation across the AF RICAN regions . The presentation also includes detailed budgets, 
implementation targets and expected programme results for the Phase 1 operations in East Africa, for 
which GCF co -financing sought under this application .   
 
 
 
B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram  
17. Only very limited financing is currently allocated to small producers’ CC adaptation investments28, despite urgent 
need for increased invest ment volumes  in low -income and lower middle -income countries in the Eastern Africa , 
facing high climate stress. Particularly, financing by private sector financial institutions (FIs)  rarely includes loans 
for rural adaptation projects of  small producers. This leads  to a low overall level of CC adaptation investments 
especially in the food systems and rural value chains .  In addition, the international community pledged to support 
indigenous peoples’ efforts as stewards of mother earth and lessening interlinked factors of vulnerability.  Underlying  
key barriers for increasing the volumes of CCA financing and investments  are identified and presented in Table 4 
below , together with solutions  that ARCAFIM programme can provide .

18. ARCAFIM will offer such solutions by supporting the project Host Bank and Partner Financial Institutions (PFIs) with 
financial and technical capacities to deploy tailored CCA financial products; offering targeted TA to Host Banks, 
PFIs (supply) and smallholder farmers and MSMEs (demand) to scale up both the availability and uptake of CCA 
finance; and facilitating improvements in the enabling environment for CCA finance in the p rogramme countries. 
These activities are expected to increase the availability of appropriate and gender - responsive   CCA finance in the 
region, improve the capacity of PFIs to deliver and report on CCA finance, and improve the capacity of male and 
female smallholder farmers and MSMEs to access and manage CCA finance. As a result, smallholder farmers and 
MSMEs will use CCA finance to implement effective climate change adaptation and resilience practices. These 
practices are expected to increase the resilien cy of rural communities to climate change, which in turn will increase 
agricultural productivity and thus food and water security, incomes and employment; all of which will enhance the 
well-being of rural communities. In addition, by strengthening the opti mal use of natural, social and human assets, 
biodiversity benefits will be achieved; and by improving the efficiency of resource use and renewable energy options 
in the agriculture sector, the project will ensure that GHG emissions will not be increased, h owever to ensure focus 
on adaptation benefits there will not be comprehensive Climate Change Mitigation objectives and measurement 
structure in the programme. ARCAFIM will also facilitate an enabling environment for ARCAFIM’s PFIs and other 
financial insti tutions to design, deliver and report of CCA finance; will share best practices in CCA finance learned


---
*Page 17*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 13 
 
   from the program’s partners; and engage in continuous dialogue with policymakers and influencers to address 
emerging challenges or risks identified durin g program implementation. These activities are expected result in an 
expansion of commercial CCA finance models for low -income rural populations.   The ARCAFIM programme will 
use the twin -track approach to mainstream gender empowerment and social inclusion considerations into its 
operations, complemented by targeted interventions to overcome any specific barriers as necessary. This approach 
will enable programme opportunities to be expanded to women, youth and persons with disabilities, while also 
addressing  the main gaps and barriers they face to inclusion.  
19. As result of the ARCAFIM interventions , the two key outcomes of the programme  include (i) ARCAFIM PFIs expand 
and mainstream CCA finance in the region resulting in increased CC resilience and improved food security levels 
of rural communities ; and (ii) Improved capacity of PFIs and agriculture value chain participants to adapt to risks 
imposed by climatic changes . Closely related to these, the important co -benefits from successful ARCAFIM 
imple mentation include increased rural household incomes, employment generation , improved biodiversity and 
climate change mitigation in the targeted communities.  The outcomes  for women and youth as a result of accessing 
CCA finance will be securing climate chan ge-resilient livelihoods with the benefits of food and water security 
(including reduced workloads associated with household tasks); improved business and employment opportunities 
and incomes; gains in voice and decision -making over the use of assets and o utputs, including income generated; 
and reductions in harmful practices and domestic violence.   
20. Diagram 1 below presents the ARCAFIM Theory of Change (TOC) and overreaching logic. In summary,  the logic 
underlying the programme ’s overall goal, impact and paradigm change function s as follows: IF adequate climate 
adaptation financial services are offered and the capacity of both small producers and rural MSMEs active in food 
systems (demand) and financial institutions (supply) are built, demonstrating a viab le CCA finance ecosystem, 
THEN  there will be a shift towards wider use of commercial resources for CCA in the region, enhancing the well -
being of rural communities through climate resilient livelihoods BECAUSE  smallholder farmers  and MSMEs will use 
CCA fin ance to adopt appropriate and effective climate change adaptation and resilience practices, increasing 
incomes, improving food security and creating and preserving jobs . In medium - to long term, the creation of 
ARCAFIM’s viable CCA finance ecosystem and it s demonstration effect will lead to a paradigm shift towards 
significantly wider use of commercial resources for CCA investments in the target regions.  
21. The Theory of Change link s ARCAFIM closely to GCF ’s key Adaptation Result Areas (ARAs) and Core Indicators. 
The ARCAFIM operations are directly aligned with GCF’s ARA1 (most vulnerable people and communities) and 
ARA2 (health, well -being, food and water security). Regarding the GCF Core Indicators , the TOC  together with the 
ARCAFIM design documents show the way the programme will contribute to climate resilient technology 
development, dissemination and innovation (Core Indicator 6), to market development/transformation at the local, 
sectoral and national le vel (Core Indicator 7), and to effective CCA knowledge generation and learning processes, 
and the use of good CCA practices, methodologies and standards (Core Indicator  8). These links to GCF ARAs 
and Core Indicators form a key element when presenting  the comprehensive Logical Framework in Section E, to 
guide the ARCAFIM operations and the monitoring of its results.  
22. There is a need to identify and address social norms that perpetuate gender empowerment and social inclusion 
(GESI) inequalities and exacerbate  barriers on both the supply and demand sides of CCA financial inclusion.  Unless 
negative norms and behaviours are addressed, women still face challenges in accessing formal financial products 
even when customized financial products and services are avail able.  GESI activities are present in programme 
component 2 (which addresses the non -financial barriers to CCA financing) and programme implementation 
arrangements. Early programme implementation arrangements will ensure there is sufficient capacity and ski lls to 
deliver and track GESI performance through the recruitment of specialists, selection of implementing partners, 
strengthening capacity of those responsible for programme delivery, and the monitoring, evaluation and learning 
system.

B.3. Project/programme description  
ARCAFIM Overview  
 
1. Overall ARCAFIM Approach.  Rural small producers are responsible for estimated average of 80% of food 
production in Sub -Saharan African  countries30, while  MSMEs involved in food systems provide services and 
valuable jobs and income opportunities to rural low -income population. Due to environmental, technical, 
economic, social and political factors, these small producers and MSMEs disproportionately experience the 
effects of climate change and external shocks on their livelihoods. To maintain and improve their levels of 
productivity,  they urgently need dedicated investment resources to build stronger resilience to climate change , 
but have been struggling so far to attract sufficient financing  for the purpose . While the exact share of climate 
finance for adaptation activities by small producers in ARCAFIM target countries is  unknown , evidence 
suggests that it is very low, in view of the limited share (1.7%) of small -scale agriculture producers’ share of 
the global CC financing  which includes both adaptation and mitigation resource s 31.  
 
2. As discussed in B.1 above, various barriers, including both demand and supply side challenges, restrict the 
expansion of CCA financing across all markets in Africa. The Africa Rural Climate Adaptation Finance 
Mechanism (ARCAFIM)  will address these barriers causing limited CCA financing from formal financial sector 
targeting small producers in Africa, by deploying catalytic financial instruments  (i.e. risk sharing facility)  into the 
mainstream  private  financial sector in each participating country co mbined with well targeted technical support 
interventions that address both supply and demand side non-financial barriers to greater CCA financing. By 
doing so, ARCAFIM seeks to achieve a demonstration effect and paradigm shift towards large -scale 
implementation of financing models for increased climate change resilience among the economically active 
low-income rural populations and small producers, notably women and youth.


---
*Page 20*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 16 
 
  
32 https://www.ipcc.ch/report/sixth -assessment -report -working -group -ii/   
3. IFAD, as a GCF Accredited Entity and a global leading organisation w ith over 40 years of successful track record 
investing in rural poor and small producers, will act as the manager of ARCAFIM ensuring overall programme 
oversight and coordination. The ARCAFIM is designed to be scalable and replicable, with the ambition is to roll 
it out to other African regions following a gradual phased approach starting with Phase 1 implementation in  
East Africa .  
4. Country Selection.  The ARCAFIM target countries under Phase 1 in East Africa region are Kenya, Tanzania, 
Uganda  and Rwanda, selected for the reasons that (i) they are either Low Income Countries or Lower Middle 
Income Countries; (ii) all are under severe climate change stress, higher compared to average in developing 
countries as stated by the IPCC 2022 report on Clima te Change  Impacts, Adaptation and Vulnerability32; (iii) in 
the target countries, there is a substantial market of small -scale agriculture production with high potential for 
rural CCA financing , and (iv) the Host Bank has significant operations and suitabl e delivery networks to reach 
the intended ARCAFIM target groups with CCA finance products . 
5. The regional approach, based on strong regional Host Bank , will allow to achieve economies of scale , efficiency 
and significant demonstration effect by leveraging on  the strengths of a large -scale regional partner 
organization with a high level of ESG integration in their operations, a strong balance sheet , appropriate 
operational capacities , and ability  to hedge funding in US$ and offer loans to low -income borrowers in local 
currencies. The phased approach will also enable learning by testing the implementability of the model with 
strong partner organisations in East Africa  and integrate lessons learnt into effective messages on how to 
promote CCA finance on a wider scale over the African continent.  
6. Host Banks . ARCAFIM’s main Partner Financial  Institutions, “ Host Banks ”, will at regional level manage both the 
financial and non -financial ARCAFIM services to the programme target groups based on ARCAFIM eligibility 
criteria and other terms and conditions, agreed upon in the programme design and included in the financing  
agreements between IFAD and the Host Banks. Financial resources provided by  IFAD, financially supported 
by its international partners,  to the Host Banks will be exclusively on -lent to small producers and those rural 
MSMEs that provide services  and jobs to rural low-income people  in the food systems , directly from the Host 
Bank subsidiaries or indirectly through intermediary financial institutions . The CCA lending methods developed 
and used under ARCAFIM will follow each  Host Bank’s operational structure and network, rural and agricult ure 
finance strategy, and their digital tools development approach.  
7. Based on IFAD’s extensive rural finance experience in Africa and reviews of financial markets in the targeted 
countries as well as interviews with several candidate  banks , the Equity Group  was selected as ARCAFIM the 
Host Bank  for East Africa . The criteria for Host Bank identification included: (a) status of a leading commercial 
bank in the targeted countries, (b) solid institutional and financial conditions, (c) strong interest in agr iculture 
and rural finance operations as reflected in the size of portfolio or foreseen strategies to scale up lending to the 
sector, (d) corporate objective to develop and scale -up the bank’s climate finance and particularly climate 
adaptation finance ope rations, (e) a regional branch or agency network to operate in at least 4-5 countries 
relevant for ARCAFIM, (f) strong expressed interest to manage and co -finance the ARCAFIM programme, and 
(g) established governance, social and environmental management st ructures.  
8. Expected Outreach, Results and Targeting . ARCAFIM will achieve its goal and outcomes, described in the 
Theory of Change above, by deploying tailored financial products and targeted TA to scale up CCA finance 
through the local private financial se ctor in the targeted countries. For East Africa, through the 12 years of 
implementation, the projected volume of increased small producer and MSME access to CCA lending is 
US$ 266 million through four -five revolved investment cycles for small producers and  three investment cycles 
for MSMEs of the total capital base of US$ 180 million with expected 6% average loss and 20% share of new 
clients per cycle . 
9. The expected outreach to households, through the revolved investment capital, is based on the expected level 
of agreement of ARCAFIM investment channels and the climate finance taxonomy of investment types 
described at Section B3, Component 1. Investment v alues at small scale producer level will range between 
US$ 50 – US$ 7,500 per farm and the MSME investment values will range between US$ 1,000 – US$ 300,000. 
The average investment per client is projected at US$ 716 for small producers and US$ 150,000 for the 
MSMEs. With the minimum of 70% of small producer investments (and maximum 30% of MSME investments) 
of the total ARCAFIM investments, the expected number of loans will be about 260,000 for small producers 
and 500 for MSMEs.


---
*Page 21*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 17 
 
 10. Therefore, as result of ARCA FIM by programme completion, about 260,000 small producers at Africa will invest 
in improved or new climate resilient production systems and directly access the ARCAFIM adaptation benefits. 
Through the 500 MSME loans, an additional 60,000 small producer ho useholds will benefit indirectly (assumed 
average 120 per MSME) through their linkages in the value chains, and 15,000 rural people will benefit from 
job creation (assumed average 30 per MSME). By project completion the total outreach is estimated at about  
335,000 households with 1.494 million people. The expected outreach will be continuously monitored through 
the ARCAFIM M&E system including the MIS quarterly financial reports, Host Bank annual reports, and through 
the household surveys at project years 3 , 6, 9 and 12 . 
11. The ARCAFIM ultimate beneficiaries are low -income, economically active rural households and small 
producers, with specific target on women and youth. IFAD has strong capacities in targeting investments to 
rural small producers and the linked  MSMEs, as well as in measurement of achieved development impact. 
ARCAFIM’s core target groups, small -scale agriculture producers and rural MSMEs, are aligned  with the 
targeting strategies of IFAD and its ongoing projects in the target countries . In summar y, the ARCAFIM 
targeting and beneficiary profiles include small scale producers, rural microenterprises and agribusiness -SMEs 
which are critical to the development of sustainable food systems and agriculture value chains:  
• Small scale producers and rural microenterprises  in the project targeted rural areas with exposure to 
climate change hazards. This target group is the final beneficiary of all ARCAFIM activities and 
investments. These producers and micro -entrepreneurs have limited access to CCA fin ancial services and 
products. The final composition of this core target group is specified by demand for the ARCAFIM micro - 
and small financing packages, including willingness to implement CCA finance products.  
 
• Agribusiness -SMEs  that have viable and small holder -inclusive business models  such as post -harvest 
processing, local service and input material provision, logistics, storage and distribution, with potential to 
create climate resilient production opportunities and stable jobs for small -scale agricultu re producers. Due 
to risks inherent in the agriculture sector, as well as insufficient business planning capacities, agro -
businesses have limited access to finance to expand their outreach in smallholder communities and 
employment creation in the VCs. All ARCAFIM supported agri -businesses are involved in and promote 
inclusive value chains development, establish market opportunities for small producers, and provide 
employment for under -employed  rural men, women and youth  in the targeted rural areas. Their ro le is 
critical in the development of rural value chains and food systems, vital for sustainable small producer 
livelihoods.  
 
ARCAFIM Components  
12. To achieve the ARCAFIM objectives, the programme activities are structured into two mutually reinforcing 
components: (a) Component  1: Effective Financing for Rural Adaptation Targeting Small Producers and Rural 
MSMEs ; and (b) Component 2:  Innovations and Capacity for Rural Adaptation  Investments . The Component 
2 capacity building activities are al igned as a required element for the CCA investments under Component 1.  
Component 1: Effective Financing for Rural Adaptation Targeting Small Producers and Rural MSMEs  
13. Under Component 1 in East Africa Region, ARCAFIM will provide catalytic international fin ancial resources of 
US$ 90 million (50% from the GCF, 50% from the IFAD -organized co-financiers) to the selected Host Bank  
(Equity Group)  for on -lending for CCA purposes in the programme target countries. The total ARCAFIM on -
lending resources, which are dedicated for CCA loans targeting small producers and rural MSMEs active in 
food systems, are US$ 180 million, which includes a matching a mount of US$ 90 million financed by the Host 
Bank.  
14. The delivery of the CCA loans to the final target groups will take place either directly through the Host Bank 
branch /agency  network or through different types of rural financial intermediaries . The loan delivery channels 
at the start-up of the operations (to be adjusted during implementation) are categorized under Outputs 1.1 and 
1.2, and will include:  
Output 1.1:  Regional Host Bank accesses resources to provide CCA loans to smallholders and 
MSMEs  
• Direct micro -lending through  Host Bank branches  and agent networks . Target: small-scale crop , livestock 
and aquaculture producers. 3 -12 month s’ seasonal input loans  and 6 - 60 months’  long-term investment 
loans  of US$ 50 – US$ 7,500.

15. Selection criteria for intermediaries.  The organizations to operate wholesale lending resources will be 
selected by the Host Bank in consultation with IFAD. The selection criteria will include:  (a) sound  institutional 
and financial condition, ( b) focus on agriculture and /or rural finance opera tions  that includes collaboration with 
small scale producers ,  with a proven commitment to poverty reduction and gender equality, (c) objective to 
develop and scale -up climate finance and particularly climate adaptation finance operations  and the capacity 
to develop and appraise a pipeline of CCA investments , (d) sufficient network to operate in rural areas with 
climate change effected small producers , (e) strong expressed interest to participate in the  ARCAFIM 
programme, ( f) appropriate quality  governance,  social and environmental management structures , and (g) 
registration under the appropriate national legal framework and supervision structures . In addition to screening 
against ARCAFIM criteria, the Host Bank will perform its regular credit risk assessmen t, and the full method 
will be documented in the ARCAFIM Operations Manual.  
16. For all loan delivery channels , the lending methods to effectively reach the final target group of ARCAFIM 
will vary between the target countries . For the East Africa region  overall , the survey -based projected shares  as 
measured in US$ volume  are (a) direct micro -loans by the Host Bank: 4 0%, (b) direct SME loans by the Host 
Bank  and their subsidiaries : 30%, and (c) wholesale loans through  MFIs, SACCOs  and value chain companies : 
30%.End-use of CCA Finance . One of the main barriers to mobilization of private sector financing into CCA 
investments is that there is no clear mutual  understanding and agreement on what types of investments can 
be considered for the end -use of climate adaptation finance  that is like ly to be commercially viable for farmers . 
Without such agreement, it has remained challenging for private sector to mobilize CCA funds and especially 
challenging to implement projects and report back on CCA results.  
17. To overcome this barrier , ARCAFIM introd uces a new operational framework with the title: “ Climate Change 
Adaptation Finance Taxonomy for Sub-Saharan African  Food Systems ”. The draft Ta xonomy  includ es firstly 
an overall presentation  and justification  for eligible CCA investment types for the PFIs, and secondly, a localized 
financial and technical viability assessment for a subset of CCA solutions that are recommended for PFI 
investment s at country and agroecological zone level. The Taxonomy documentation  has been developed 
following an analyti cal process : (i) categorization of SSA CC risks in rural and agriculture sector, (ii) mapping 
of CCA models as adverse impact reduction measures for the CC risk categories, (iii) mapping of CCA models 
with private sector accountability and ownership, (iv) assessment of viability by private sector (farms, MSMEs) 
to implement CCA models, (v) categorization of FI -viable CCA models per CC risk,  and (vii) at country level, 
categorization of the taxonomy by agronomic geographic al zone s and crops for technically a nd financially 
viable CCA models .  The framework will adopt a holistic livelihood approach and will include technologies and 
practices that make not only the productive activities more climate change resilient but also the domestic 
aspects of rural livelihoods, such as water collection/harvesting, storage and use; fuel collection and use; food 
processing and preparation; and energy.  
18. Table 5 below summarizes  the categorization of eligible CCA investment types for each potential borrower 
group and risk  type.  This Table will be included in legal agreements to be signed between IFAD and the Host 
Bank, defining the general categories of eligible end -borrower adaptation investments that can be financed 
with ARCAFIM support.  The Taxonomy should be considered  a living document that can be revised and 
expanded through consensus between ARCAFIM stakeholders. The operationalization and broad distribution

 of the T axonomy development will continue during  the ARCAFIM implementation as a joint exercise between

PFIs. The wider objective is that the Taxonomy will be applied as a mainstream ed tool by ARCAFIM 
participating financial institutions, as well as by other financial institutions and international financiers 
supporting CC adaptation in Africa (see more in Component 2).

20. ARCAFIM r isk management solution . The ARCAFIM financial structure is based on a blended finance risk 
sharing structure that unlocks international capital (including from GCF as the key international financier ) and 
crowds in regional capital for the ARCAFIM CCA financing operations at scale. Th e favourable financing terms 
of the international ARCAFIM loans to the Host Bank, together with non -financial support will make it possible 
to provide competitive financing for CCA investments at affordable terms to the target groups, including 
adequate re payment and grace periods.  
Component 2: Innovations and Capacity for Rural Adaptation Investments  
21. As a required element to support the Component 1 investments , Component 2 will address non -financial 
barriers currently preventing greater CCA financing  in the ARCAFIM target regions . With a total technical 
assistance budget  of US$ 20 million34, firstly, ARCAFIM  will strengthen the awareness and capacity of 
Participating Financial Institutions to offer rural CCA finance services  and secondly , the project  will develop the  
CCA investment capacities and financial literacy skills of small -scale producers and rural MSMEs  that will apply 
for ARCAFIM financing . Thirdly , ARCAFIM will improve climate finance enabling environment through CCA 
finance policy d ialogues and the development of a Taxonomy for CCA Rural Finance that can be implemented 
in East Africa  and more widely , in all regions of  Africa.

*Page 29*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 25 
 
 22. Output  2.1: Improved readiness and capacities of all PFIs to make appropriate climate finance investments . 
ARCAFIM will provide global top end expertise to the regional Host Bank – Equity Bank Group – and its network 
of subsidiaries  and branches  in the four target countries,  as well as an estimated 20 other PFIs that will perform 
as local intermediary financial service providers (see Component 1 on the respective lending  roles and 
selection criteria  for the intermediaries ). The capacity building service will ensure that the provided credit will 
deliver real, effective and transformati onal adaptation impact and is channelled to the intended purposes and 
beneficiaries. The Host Ban k with IFAD support will recruit  systematic support to establish the ARCAFIM 
programme management systems, to coordinate the programme implementation and repor t on the 
performance. All ARCAFIM PFIs will be sensitized to the importance of CCA and receive a demand -driven 
package of technical support and innovation services, with the aim to develop and offer appropriate CCA rural 
financial services, appropriate ris k management practices in CCA financing, and reduce transaction costs  by 
strengthened delivery channels . Initiation of the Technical Assistance to PFIs is a condition to access to 
ARCAFIM financing for on -lending.  
23. The Output will be managed by the Host Bank . The implementation will be  through competitively selected 
external service providers and through the Equity Bank Foundation , which is dedicated for social transformation 
through inclusive finance  and has a strong network and track record on the same . The external  service provider 
selection process is conducted jointly by the Host Bank  and IFAD to ensure mobilization of global top end 
capacities for the task.  The Host Bank and its Equity Bank Foundation will share 50% of the costs of the 
activities  implemented through the Foundation .  
24. Participatin g FIs (Host Bank ’s branch network as well as  local intermediaries) involved in ARCAFIM financing 
activities will receive technical support packages tailored for their specific needs. A preliminary demand 
analysis for the capacity building services was conducted during the ARCAFIM  design , confirming that in the 
financial sector there is limited understanding of CCA finance for agriculture value chains  however a high 
interest to initiate climate finance portfolios with available appropriate support . The ARCAFIM capacity building  
to PFIs will be launche d at project start up to support the Host Bank  Group and  branches and subsequently to 
other PFIs against their expressions of demand to participate in the ARCAFIM implementation . Technical 
support areas will include: (i) development of CCA finance strategy  and costing of its implementation, (ii) 
development or strengthening of CCA lending products that comply with the eligibility criteria under ARCAFIM 
Taxonomy, that are reported as a separate category in the Host Bank’s management information system, and 
that target small producers, rural poor and MSMEs active in food systems, (iii) support rural outre ach and social 
inclusion strategies, including CCA finance advisory capacities, (iv) strengthen Digital Finance Services by 
inclusion of functions for climate, rural and agricultural finance, to enable data driven, affordable and effective 
risk management and support functions for CCA lending, (v) support improved Management Information 
Systems (MIS), (vi) develop agriculture and climate insurance products and solutions such as bundled service 
packages, and (vii) support strengthened ESG standards and manag ement systems including CC risk 
management  structures  for each PFI to comply with IFAD , GCF and other co -financier safeguards.  
Furthermore, ARCAFIM will work with the PFIs to develop technical assistance packages delivered to small 
producers and MSMEs unde r Output 2.2. 
25. Tech nology -enabled innovative solutions . Technical support to PFIs will also be provided to identify and pilot 
digital technologies which reduce transaction costs, and/or improve the credit assessment and risk 
management of small CCA loans. The solutions to be implemented directly under the ARCA FIM Host Bank 
delivery channels or by intermediary financial service providers, including Fintechs, can include but are not 
limited  to: (i) location services for farmer plots and GIS -based information of the agriculture models and 
expected yields contribut ing to client risk assessments in a cost -effective manner, (ii) digital payments/mobile 
wallet transaction histor ies of small producers, contributing to the cost-effective client risk assessment, (iii) data 
analytics capabilities of PFIs to include new typ es of data like farm  boundaries and soil health incorporated into 
the credit assessment to improve accessibility of potential borrowers including women and youth who typically 
lack credit histories and collateral, (iv) cost -effective transactions of small -scale financing to small producers 
by utilization of mobile money systems including savings, credit and payment services, (v) tracking of the 
utilization of ARCAFIM investment resources through mobile payment systems, and (vi) integration of technical 
advisory packages and weather information into the mobile banking financial service interface. Details of 
selected solutions will be developed and contracts with digital service providers launched in the project initiation 
period  and during Programme Year 1.  
26. GESI. The CCA finance strategy (including CCA financial advisory capacities) and rural outreach and social 
inclusion strategies will specifically address how the CCA products and services will developed and 
disseminated to women, youth and people with disa bilities. Methods for community engagement and outreach 
will be inclusive and gender responsive . CCA lending products and services (including climate risk insurance ,

35 https://www.safinetwork.org/   digital technologies and advisory services ) will be tailored to the needs, resources and capacities of women 
and youth who are small producers or micro/small entrepreneurs in the food system.   
 
27. Output  2.2: Improved CCA investment capacities of smallholder farmers and MSMEs . Recognizing the urgent 
need to stimulate CCA finance demand among rural small producers and MSMEs  involved in food systems , 
ARCAFIM will offer targeted and practical technical assistance to the se target groups.  The pipeline generated 
through the capacity building directly builds to the investment outreach of the PFIs, ensuring effective and 
appropriate utilisation of the ARCAFIM Component 1 financial resources.  In the four East African target 
countries, the Output activities will reach an estimated 325,000  small producers and 625 agribusinesses. The 
selection criteria and identification process for the target groups is explained above under Component 1.  
28. This Output will also be managed by the Host Bank. Its execution will be mainly through the Equity Bank 
Foundation that has extensive experience in implementation of technical assistance operations to prospective 
financial service clients. For the  activities implemented through the Foundation , 50% of the costs  will be 
covered by the Foundation.  To bridge gaps in the Foundation’s technical capacity related to Climate Change 
Adaptation as well as the Foundation’s outreach potential in areas with thinner footprint, external technical 
assistance partners will be co mpetitively recruited by the Host Bank with IFAD support and oversight.  
29. Small -Scale Agriculture Producer CCA Investment Promotion and Financial Literacy Services.  An estimated 
16,000 rural producer groups will be trained, with average 20 participants in ea ch group. The activity will 
increase knowledge of viable CCA options and financial services among small producers, and enhance the 
financial literacy skills and savings culture among the supported households, leading to increased volumes and 
sustainability  of CCA investments. Capacity building topics will include: CCA investment options, basic banking 
skills, “Saving for a Purpose”, managing resources, borrowing with special focus on the medium to long -term 
perspective required in the CCA loans, interest ra tes, collateral, bank transactions and payments, and the use 
of mobile and digital banking services. Furthermore, a targeted module for women empowerment in the use of 
financial services will be developed . 
30. Agribusiness CCA Business Development Services (BD S). The aim of the CCA BDS is to strengthen the 
knowledge of rural enterprises  of viable CCA options , benefits  and related financial services, and to develop 
their business planning , investment start -up and financial management capacity,  thereby  increasing the 
demand for CCA finance and  lower ing the investment risk for the PFIs. Furthermore, bankability assessments 
can be completed for agribusinesses, helping to inform the PFIs of the  real investment risks and opportunities. 
The BDS services to the targeted 625 agribusinesses will be organized as training of small groups and as 
individual consulting service, depending on demand,  with the aim to ensure that ARCAFIM financing will target  
well planned productive and screened investments in the rural agricultural sectors, to ensure highest possible 
positive impact on climate change resilience.  During BDS initiation, MSME participation fees will be piloted and 
progressively introduced with t he objective of 25% cost coverage.  
31. GESI. Under output 2.2, the gender action plan will ensure that GESI considerations are mainstreamed into the 
provision of targeted and practical technical assistance to stimulate the demand for CCA finance. Participatory  
engagement with the community, will ensure CCA products and services meet the priority needs and resources 
of different groups within the food system, including women, youth and people with disabilities.  
32. Output  2.3: Improved enabling environment of CCA through application of taxonomy and best practices by 
climate finance ecosystem . Policy dialogue on rural CCA finance  enabling environment , especially to create 
functioning private sector investment mechanisms, will be supported by the A RCAFIM team, implemented  by 
IFAD  as the  Executing Entity for this Output . The deliverables will include: (i) continuous technical backstopping 
by IFAD to support the ARCAFIM Host Bank and PFIs in the climate adaptation finance operations, (ii) semi -
annual  technical working papers on emerging topics on climate finance in East Africa region, and on the same 
topic s, semi -annual high -level regional policy workshops , and (iii) regular updates, dissemination and 
operationalization of the ARCAFIM Taxonomy framewor k as a key governance tool for CCA finance.  
33. For this work, IFAD will closely engage with the GCF -led network of National Designated Authorities and with 
regional intergovernmental organizations to ensure that the developed best practises will become broadly 
known and utilized for climate change adaptation programming in the region. Furthe rmore, to enable effective 
replication, the ARCAFIM core implementing group will capitalize IFAD’s knowledge and networks in  
international privat e and public sector stakeholder groups and platforms , including the IFAD -hosted 
Smallholder and Agri -SME Financ e and Investment Network (SAFIN)35, Public Development Banks Platform

37 https://www.rfilc.org/   for Green and Inclusive Food Systems36, the Rural Finance and Investment Learning Centre (RFILC -
CABFIN)37, as well as the IFAD -supported Rural Finance Investment Projects at country leve l and the ongoing 
COMESA –EAC–SADC Climate Change Program.  The objective is to ensure continued enabling environment 
for climate finance and dialogues to adjust policy frameworks in case of emerging challenges or risks that may 
prevent ARCAFIM effective imp lementation and scaling up of its best practices.  
34. In particular, d uring the ARCAFIM  start-up period IFAD will, together with the key implementing partners, lead 
the operationalization  of the framework for private sector CCA financing applicable across the Sub -Saharan 
region  (“Climate Change Adaptation Finance Taxonomy for Sub-Saharan African  Food Systems  ”) and support 
the related policy dialogue on rural CCA finance enabling environment . The developed Taxonomy  will 
immediately serve as the core  framework for end-borrower investment eligibility under ARCAFIM, but more 
broadly, help the climate finance ecosystem at international, regional, national and local levels to justify, monitor 
and report back on private sector  CCA investments.  In addition to the overall eligibility framework , IFAD has 
prepared technical and financial assessments of a subset of eligible investment options and provides 
investment recommendations to the PFIs for each country and agroecological zon e. The steps in the 
development of t he methodology are described under Component 1 above.  Following  the methodology 
development and  technical groundwork, a series of regional workshops, both virtual and physical, will be 
organised  to complete, agree upon, and disseminate the agreed  version of the Taxonomy , as well as 
continuously update the CCA investment option viability recommendations at local levels . IFAD will lead this 
effort, joined by the ARCAFIM financiers, including international financing partners , the regional Host Banks, 
local FIs, local value chain stakeholders as well as the relevant national policy makers and authorities. 
Technical synergies will be sought with other GCF -financed IFAD projects in Africa with similar types of 
activities , including in particular the secon d phase of IGREENFIN programme , and also with climate change 
adaptation programmes by other development partners.  
35. GESI. The policy dialogue on CCA rural finance presents a unique opportunity to engage govern ment and 
policy makers with GESI challenges and opportunities linked to inclusive CCA finance.  Engagement will be 
broaden ed to encourage collaboration and exchange between government, NGOs and civil society on the 
nexus between GESI, climate change and fi nancial services.  
B.4. Implementation arrangements  
36. Overall Implementation Structure.  Diagram 2 shows  the overall ARCAFIM implementation structure.  Below 
in this Section, the key implementation roles and approaches of IFAD and the Host Bank are summarised, as 
well as the core elements of the M&E arrangements and the implementation model of the TA services. The 
Section is closed with the description o f the key elements and practices in the  ARCAFIM financial structure 
and its legal framework .

37. Role of IFAD.  In additio n to the co -EE role for output 2.3, IFAD , as a GCF PSF Accredited Entity,  will manage 
the funds provided for ARCAFIM by GCF and other international financiers and be the lender of record of the 
ARCAFIM funds to the Host Bank. IFAD will disburse the funds for ARCAFIM on -lending to the regional Host 
Bank subject to satisfaction o f the agreed Conditions Precedents for the  drawdown.  The IFAD ARCAFIM loans 
will be matched 1:1 by the Host Bank, thus doubling the ARCAFIM financing resources.   The IFAD loans  to the 
Host Bank serve as risk protection for the underlying Qualifying Loans i n the blended finance structure. IFAD 
will monitor and oversee that the Host Bank will use the loan proceeds exclusively for on -lending under the 
ARCAFIM programme to the targeted beneficiaries based on agreements and definitions of the Loan 
Agreements bet ween IFAD and the Host Bank.  
38. Based on monitoring and reporting procedures to be included in the loan agreement between IFAD and the 
Host Bank , IFAD will provide regular progress reports to GCF and other co -financiers to keep them informed 
of the ARCAFIM  performance including both financial aspects and development results as well as social, 
environmental and governance safeguards  developed for th e programme . IFAD will undertake regular 
supervision s and commission periodical evaluation studies  on ARCAFIM development impact . IFAD will also 
coordinate the development and implementation of the CCA finance taxonomy and policy dialogues under 
Output 2.3. These functions will be performed by dedicated IFAD staff for the ARCAFIM operation as well as 
by recruited part -time consultants, all financed with the management fees earned on the loans and grants  
provided by GCF and other international financiers to I FAD for ARCAFIM.  
39. Role and Approach of Host Bank.  The regional Host Bank , in East Africa the Equity Group,  will manage the 
investment of the ARCAFIM resources and services (financial and non -financial) to the programme target 
groups, based on ARCAFIM eligibility criteria and other terms and conditions, agreed upon at the programme 
design and included in the loan agreement between IFAD and the Host Bank. The  Host Bank  will also perform 
the role of the GCF Executing Entities for ARCAFIM. The Host Bank  will ensure utilization  of ARCAFIM 
resources exclusively to agreed targets , detailed monitoring of the investment s, and periodic reporting of the 
ARCAFIM progress against agreed key indicators. The Host Bank  will also coordinate the ARCAFIM -financed 
technical assistance support to the end target groups to improve their capabilities to participate in CCA finance 
opera tions. There are three layers of project management activities under the ARCAFIM program me – i.e.
