40. As described under Component 1, the Host Bank  can utilise various alternative channels to effectively reach 
the ARCAFIM target group with the CCA financing services. In the East Africa Region, the Equity Group 
operates in ARCAFIM target countries with a wide rural branch network supported by a large number of banking 
agent s. In total, the Group has over 330 branch offices and over 53,000 agen ts in the ARCAFIM target 
countries . With this significant outreach and its history as an active lender to small -scale farmers, the Equity 
Group is well equipped t o provide CCA loans direct to smallholders and rural MSMEs. Therefore, direct loans 
to end borrowers are expected to have a large share of  the ARCAFIM portfolio in East Africa . There will , 
however,  also be loans issued through the wholesale lenders such as  rural Savings and Credit Cooperatives 
(SACCOs ) and Microfinance Institutions ( MFIs ), which operate with large rural clienteles in this region.   
41. ARCAFIM Technical Advisory Services . The implementation and management modality of the ARCAFIM 
technical support services under Component 2 will be tailored  for each  type and recipient of these services. 
For technical support to the Host Bank and to the intermediary PFIs, to develop their  CCA  lending  operations , 
IFAD will, jointly with the Host Bank, select the external service providers/consultants to provide the required 
services.  The Host Bank will manage the recruitment of these service providers while  IFAD  no objection will be 
required to  oversee that the services provided meet the expectations  agreed in the ARCAFIM design . 
42. Regarding the TA services to the end borrowers, the Hos t Bank will directly manage the service delivery, 
utilizing either the service units  under Equity Bank Foundation,  with ARCAFIM support , and recruiting external 
technical service providers  when needed . This is a preferred solution t o ensure full alignment between the 
financing  and capacity building aspects  of ARCAFIM  and thereby increase the effectiveness of capacity 
building in increased access to finance results . To further emphasise ARCAFIM capacity building, all e xternal 
service provider contracts will be outcome -based . IFAD , as the programme manager , will leverage its


---
*Page 34*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 30 
 
 experience and knowledge of relevant farmer organization networks , NGOs  and other service providers to 
identify the most appropriate capacity buildi ng partners  for a CCA financing programme of the ARCAFIM type.  
43. For the services and development tasks related to policy dialogue and the CCA taxonomy, IFAD has the key 
implementation responsibility. The tasks in this important area  will require close co -operation between IFAD 
and the financial institutions implementing ARCAFIM, who are also direct beneficiaries and immediate users 
of the  developed  taxonomy in their portfolio operations with the rural clientele.  
44. Preliminary Implementation Activities Leading to ARCAFIM Launch.   During the period leading to 
ARCAFIM effectiveness and official launch, the ARCAFIM -related staff at IFAD and the Host Bank  will closely 
co-operate to ensure a smooth take -off of the programme activities. In East Africa, t o support and co -ordinate 
these activities, a CCA Finance Specialist will be recruited at the banking group level to Equity Group , with 
funding from the ARCA FIM design grants. The expert  will (a) support the design of appropriate CCA loan 
products for the bank to be launched under ARCAFIM, based on the developed CCA finance taxonomy, (b) 
co-ordinate the development of the lending mechanism appropriate for these CCA loan products, and (c) 
support the planning and organising of appropriate in -house training programmes and training/marketing 
materials for the Host Bank on ARCAFIM -financed lending. At the same time IFAD and the Host Bank w ill 
finalise the preparations for the selection and training of appropriate local wholesale on -lending institutions to 
take part in the ARCAFIM lending and develop the framework and action plans for capacity building of small 
producers, their organisations, and the borrowing MSMEs in the CCA operations.   Under the guidance of a 
GESI expert appointed to the PMU, gender and social norms diagnostic studies will be undertaken in t he four 
participating countries  during early implementation and the findings will guide the development of the detailed 
GESI action plan.   
45. Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E).   The overall M&E approach for ARCAFIM is built around its Theory of 
Change and Log  Frame and builds in adaptive management so that the program me can regularly assess, learn 
and adapt to improve  its climate adaptation  outcomes. Details  on the Monitoring , Reporting and  Evaluation 
System are proposed in Section E .7, and the system will be  further refined during the inception phase with the 
necessary inputs of stakeholders in the M&E process . While IFAD will lead the M&E  reporting to GCF  for the 
program me, the Host Bank , as key implementer of the programme, will have a prominent  role in data collection 
and analysi s. 
    
46. ARCAFIM Contractual Arrangement.  ARCAFIM has two level of contractual arrangements – upstream and 
downstream. In the upstream arrangement, the IFAD intends to receive financial resources from the 
international financiers, including GCF, based on bilateral financin g agreements to be entered into between the 
international financiers and IFAD. In the downstream arrangement, the Host Bank (Equity Bank (Kenya ) 
Limited ) will receive the loan from IFAD and make a contribution  equal to the amount of the loan that the Host 
Bank received from IFAD, to be on -lent to the final beneficiaries or financial intermediaries through Host Bank  
or other country subsidiaries ( EBUL , EBR, and EBTL ). 
Upstream:  
1. Limited recourse f unding agreement s with the international financiers (including the FAA with GCF).  
2. Grant agreements with GCF and international financiers.  
3. An inter -creditor agreement between the international financiers and IFAD (if agreed with all other 
international financiers).  
 
Downstream:  
1. IFAD loan agreement with the Host Bank  (i.e. Equity Bank (Kenya ) Limited ), including the risk-sharing 
arrangement between IFAD and the Host Bank  (the “ Subsidiary  Loan Agreement”) . 
2. IFAD technical assistance and grant agreements with the Host Bank . 
Upstream ARCAFIM Contractual Arrangement  with GCF . IFAD will enter into a FAA with GCF under which IFAD, 
in its capacity as Accredited Entity, shall administer the releva nt GCF Proceeds to be used for the financing of ARCAFIM 
and channel such GCF Proceeds to Host Bank , in accordance with the AMA and the terms and conditions to be agreed 
in the FAA.  The requested GCF Loan amount is USD 45 million, which consists of USD 21 million to be utilized for the 
1st loss tranche and USD 24 million for mezzanine tranche under the risk sharing arrangement in the loan agreement 
between IFAD and Host Bank . IFAD’s repayment obligation to GCF is limited to obligations to principal, interest , fees 
and any other costs related to the GC F Loans  (the “Reflowed Funds”) received from Host Bank  by way of repayment


---
*Page 35*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 31 
 
  
38 CPI, Landscape of Climate Finance in Africa, September 2022.  under the Subsidiary  Loan Agreement  (limited recourse) , and such repayment obligations  will be subject to the 
operation of the risk sharing mechanism.  
Downstream ARCAFIM Contractual Arrangement  with Equity Bank (Kenya ) Limited (EBKL) .  Equity Bank 
(Kenya ) Limited is the Borrower as well as the Executing Entity for ARCAFIM for East Africa. EBKL  will on -lend the 
loan received from IFAD to Equity Bank Uganda, Equity Bank Rwanda , and Equity Bank Tanzania  through sub -loan 
agreements to be executed between EBKL  and each of the three country subsidiaries.  The country allocation among 
four countries is expected as follows – Kenya: 55%, Uganda: 25%, Rwanda: 10%, and Tanzania: 10%.   The Subsidiary  
Loan Agreement will provide for  the risk sharing arrangement between IFAD and Host Bank. The risk sharing 
mechanism is further described in the next section  and the risk -sharing agreement may equally be a stand -alone 
agreement for the convenience purposes .  
47. Risk Sharing Mechanism under the Subsidiary  Loan Agreement. In order for the Host Bank to recognize 
the credit protection, a risk-sharing application  process  will be  introduced and stipulated  in the Subsidiary  Loan 
Agreement  (or in a separate stand -alone agreement if advised by legal counsel) . It operates as a reduction of 
the Host Bank’s loan repayment obligation to IFAD, and the process is similar to the claim s process in unfunded 
guarantee products.  The loan repayment obli gation from the Host Bank to IFAD changes through the risk-
sharing application  requested by the Host Bank and approved by IFAD, based on the materialized loss of the 
underlying loans through the pre -agreed request/approval process .  
48. Disbursement / Recyclin g / Repayment process  with EBKL . During the availability period of the first 5 years, 
the Host Bank will make a disbursement request to IFAD based on the disbursement projection every six 
months. Each participating  country subsidiar y will be able to make a disbursement for the Loans qualifying 
under the ARCAFIM eligibility criteria and CCA taxonomy (the “Qualifying Loans”) from the fund s that received 
from IFAD, and its own contribution equal to the fund.  Once the Qualifying Loan is d isbursed, the Host Bank 
should submit the inclusion request to IFAD to notify IFAD of the Qualifying Loan, so that IFAD can monitor the 
portfolio.  During the investment period of the first ten years, the Host Bank is allowed to recycle the principal 
repayments of the Qualifying Loans, as a revolving facility. During the repayment period of the last 5 years , the 
Host Bank will repay the principal amount to IFAD in accordance with the repayment schedule under the 
Subsidiary  Loan Agreement .  
B.5. Justification for GCF funding request  
49. Requirement of GCF Funding: Limited Alternative  Options for CCA Financing.  As discussed above in 
detail, ARCAFIM, with financing from the GCF and the programme co -financiers, will provide resources for the 
financing o f the type of CCA adaptation investments, for which very limited alternative finance is available in 
the target countries.  Low Income Countries only received 8% of all climate financing compared to 70% invested 
in Middle Income Countries (UNEP 2022 Adaptat ion Gap Report). Furthermore , according to the Climate Policy 
Initiative 38, climate financing in Africa is US $ 29.5 billion annually , far short of the  US$ 277 billion needed 
annually for countries to meet their nationally determined contributions (NDCs) by 2030 . In East Africa, annual 
flows total USD 10 billion compared to an annual demand of 82 billion. Climate financing on the continent is  
more balance d between adaptation and mitigation than in other regions:  mitigation activities represented 49% 
(USD 14.6 billion) of climate finance flows compared to 39% (USD 11.4 billion) in adaptation, and 12% (USD 
3.5 billion) in activities generating dual benefits. Still, adaptation funding would need to increase six -fold to meet 
the estimated USD 62 billon annual need . In terms of sectors, funding of adaptation activities in AFOLU 
(agriculture, forestry and other land use) totalled USD 2.8 billion, or just 10%  of tota l climate finance flows . The 
private sector investments, that are urgently needed to scale adaptation solutions, represented only 14% (USD 
4.2 billion) of climate finance in Africa . This is lower than in other regions, and was invested primarily in 
mitigation projects (81%) .  
50. The barriers for rural CCA financing are high and relatively similar in all the targeted economies. General 
economic and legal barriers include insufficient financial sector regulations and debt resolution regimes 
curtailing cli mate credit creation. Lack of borrower knowledge of CCA options and skills to develop sound 
investment proposals reduce the bankable demand for CCA finance. On the supply side, lack of climate finance 
and especially CCA finance knowledge and capacity in pr ivate financial institutions; lack of access to medium 
and long -term capital by local financial institutions; and lack of appropriate risk management solutions reduce 
the private FI interest to invest in rural CCA projects. High up -front costs and risks fo r developing new business 
lines in green and particularly CCA adaptation finance further decrease this investment interest .


---
*Page 36*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 32 
 
  
39 https://aceliafrica.org/bridging -the-financing -gap-unlocking -the-impact -potential -of-agricultural -smes -in-africa/   51. The ARCAFIM interventions provide financial and non -financial solutions and offer risk cover for the Host Bank 
in the ARCAFIM regio nal facilities together with competitive long -term funding, which aim to remove the above 
type of barriers for mainstreaming CCA adaptation finance in the financial sectors of the participating countries.  
52. Considering the substantial and multi -dimensional nature of the current barriers, it is highly unlikely that CCA 
finance mainstreaming will happen in the private financial sectors in Africa without support from programmes 
such as  ARCAFIM.  At the same time the interest by the leading  commercial  bank in the region to partner with 
international financiers in the ARCAFIM initiative clearly shows that paradigm change towards more private 
sector CCA funding is possible if appropriate types of in centives are provided to the private banks to enter this 
market.   
53. Rationale and Level of Concessionality.  Higher default rates in CCA financing targeting small producers and 
rural SMEs involved in food systems, in combination with high acquisition and tra nsaction costs, serve as a 
deterrent for commercial banks and other intermediaries to engage in such lending in low -income regions of 
Africa. According to a recent study by Aceli Africa39 on agri -lending in Sub -Saharan Africa, the average agri -
lending portfolio default rate of commercial banks are estimated a t 6% and in the 4.9-7.7% range, 3-5% higher 
than commercial lending in other sectors.  The perception among commercial banks for agriculture climate 
resilience and adaptation financing is that it is even riskier, although it may prove to be exactly the contrary: 
increased climate resilience can increase the long -term ability of the borrowers to serve their loans, however 
this is yet  to be proven to the financial sector operators. Therefore, one of the goals of ARCAFIM is to gather 
comprehensive data sets for climate adaptation finance and make it available for commercial lenders in the 
target countries to make more informed choices i n the future.  
54. Given the sizable unmet annual need for adaptation financing in Africa, the marginal role currently played by 
the private sector  in financing climate adaptation activities , and the perceived risks of financing agriculture 
climate adaptation a nd resilience, and thus considered higher than standard agri -lending, the concessional 
elements of ARCAFIM are justifiable to incentivize the Host Bank to dedicate resources and effort to enter this 
field.  
55.  Regarding pricing of the loans to end clients, t he Host Bank and other PFIs in the ARCAFIM financial 
ecosystem will charge sufficient margins to ensure financial sustainability of the operation. Due to the 
concessional elements  provided by ARCAFIM to the Host Ban k, the ARCAFIM  end borrower interest rate will 
be at the low end of existing market rates in the target countries , which under normal conditions would not be 
possible to micro -level clients in rural areas. According to ARCAFIM Supply and Demand assessment, an 
increasing  number of financial institutions (including commercial banks, microfinance institutions and SACCOs) 
offer agricultural lending products,  however  the conditions for accessing the more competitive  loans  often 
exclude smallholder farmers. The pricing varies significantly in the region and between financial institutions,  
ranging from 8% - 96% per year  among the surveyed institutions. Those financial institutions that  offer lower 
interest rates to the agriculture sector, especially to small -scale farmers, can i n most cases only do so with 
support of concessional capital. See below table with summary of annual interest rates in the target region:  
Table 1 2: Interest Rate Survey Results Summary

56. With the ARCAFIM preferential lending terms and risk management solutions offered to the Host Bank , it is 
expected that (i) the participating financial institutions are incentivised to develop a CCA product portfolio, (ii) 
the rural CC vulnerable groups ca n have access to the developed CCA finance products, and (iii) the interest 
rates offered by the Host Bank  and their on -lending PFIs to end borrowers will be competitive and at the low 
end of current market -based interest rates charged this market segment in the target countries. During the 
annual ARCAFIM performance assessments, IFAD will conduct a comparative review of the  ARCAFIM interest 
rates against the  going market in each target country and provide guidelines for the ARCAFIM interest rates . 
57. GCF Grant Element for CCA Finance Innovations and Capacity Building . The ARCAFIM proposal for East 
Africa includes an element of US$ 20 million grant financing for strategic technical advisory and capacity 
building services, to ensure effective delivery of cli mate change adaptation financial services to the vulnerable 
target groups. CCA finance is an emerging field, especially for the ARCAFIM types of micro -scale investments 
through private financing channels. Therefore, significant non -financial support is req uired to foster innovation 
in the CCA finance delivery methodology including the introduction of the appropriate CCA finance taxonomy 
and the adoption of digital technology -based solutions  to encourage wider rural outreach. At the same time 
grant finance i s required to ensure effective demand for the offered CCA services  to increase end -borrower 
knowledge of CCA adaptation options and thereby to stimulate the CCA finance demand . 
58. Within the US$ 20 million of ARCAFIM non -financial services  in the East Africa region , the proposal is to include 
a US$ 1 0 million TA grant from GCF. This is 18 per  cent of the total requested GCF financing or 5 per  cent of 
the total ARCAFIM budget, which will be matched by additional grant financing by  other  international financiers 
of ARCAFIM  and cost -sharing by the PFIs (see below  Section C1 – Financing by Components). The proposed 
level of GCF grant support is highly justified due to the large unmet demand  for CCA financing among 
ARCAFIM target groups and th e need to develop new technical approaches that can be replicated elsewhere 
to substantially increase private sector participation in CCA finance in Africa . 
59. COVID -19 and War in Ukraine C ontext.  As a consequence of the COVID -19 pandemic and the measures 
prescribed globally to prevent its spread  since 2020 , GDP growth rates across all ARCAFIM target countries 
started to  drop.  Since the beginning of 2022, the Russian invasion in Ukraine and subsequent, on -going conflict 
have made the livelihoods of rural low -income people even more fragile in Eastern and southern Africa.  In such  
a situation,  in which many lenders withdrew from the rural sector they deemed risky and unprofitable, 
interventions such as those proposed by ARCAFIM are essential to facilitate and re -establish lending to 
investments by the agri -MSMEs and smallholders, thereby helping them build back better and in a sustainable 
manner. By investing US$  200 million in maki ng the agricultural sector in the East Africa region  more resilient, 
ARCAFIM will act as counter -cyclical financier to reduce the consequences not only of the current crisis but 
also future climate -related or other external shocks on the agriculture sector  and the overall economy. 
Furthermore,  ARCAFIM will contribute to rural job creation, more stable incomes and food security in the target 
countries at a time when food commodity prices are rapidly increasing in wake of the war in Ukraine .

 60. The current crisis clearly strengthens the justification of the GCF and other international financiers for their 
investments in ARCAFIM. This support has been critical in ensuring the commitment of the leading commercial 
bank in this climate change adaptat ion programme during  the time when private banks have generally been 
conservative in their engagement in new type of ventures. If successfully implemented in the current, 
demanding economic environment, the chances of major expansion and replication of the  developed CCA 
finance approach es are obvious when the overall economic conditions will again start to improve in the region.  
  
B.6. Exit strategy  
61. The exit strategies and related s ustainability of the ARCAFIM benefits and results need to  be approached at 
various levels. Below, the key sustainability and exit strategy elements of ARCAFIM are summarised.  
62. End Beneficiary Impact Sustainability . At small scale producers and MSME level, ARCAFIM will promote and 
support modernised CCA production systems that are market driven and suitable to local agro -ecological zones 
and climatic conditions. The comprehensive ARCAFIM approach to mentoring and tr aining in technical aspects 
of climate -smart farming and in relevant business skills is expected to significantly improve small -scale 
agricultur al producers’ potential to sustain the benefits from ARCAFIM interventions even when the project 
support ends. A t the farmer group and cooperative level, the ARCAFIM interventions aim at training these 
organisations to professionally conduct their business and to be more financially robust and self -managing 
partners in the environmentally friendly value chain operat ions. Being successful in this area will be a key factor 
for sustaining the ARCAFIM results and benefits in the period after the project closure , including continued and 
expanded access to financial services for working capital and investments, based on im proved skills base and 
linkages with the financial sector and markets.   A strong GESI focus, addressing both the symptoms and 
underlying causes of inequality and exclusion, will enable women and youth to engage fully in the range of 
CCA finance opportunities and thereby contribute to the sustainability of programme outcomes.  
63. Sustainability of Impact in Participating Private Sector Financial Institutions  is in the core of ARCAFIM’s 
operational sustainability and exit strategy. ARCAFIM will provide global top -end expertise to the regional Host 
Bank  and its wholesale borrower ins titutions, to ensure that the financial and non -financial services provided 
will deliver effective, sustainable and transformational adaptation finance solutions. Through its analytical work 
on CCA finance supply and demand and its related capacity and sys tems development interventions, 
ARCAFIM will significantly contribute to reducing the PFIs’ risk perception associated to this sector’s lending. 
At the same time the ARCAFIM blended credit facility with its risk management solutions will enable the Host 
Bank and its local partne r FIs to invest at scale in profitable climate compatible agriculture solutions and 
technologies and to identify new climate -smart business opportunities in this sector. With skills developed and 
experience gained , participating fina ncial institutions will be in a position to profitably continue with the 
adaptation finance operations even  when the direct support to them from ARCAFIM ends.  
64. Environmental Sustainability . The identification and promotion of the use of the best available CC adaptation 
technologies and inputs will maximize the environmental sustainability of the ARCAFIM -supported agricultural 
activities. The recommended and prioritized adaptation measures w ill ensure the protection of natural 
resources, maintain soil nutrient balance, reduce land degradation and pollution, and improve biodiversity. The 
used CCA technologies will strengthen the optimal use of natural, social, and human assets and reduce the 
negative impact of food production on the environment. This will make it possible to increase rural production 
and incomes of the ARCAFIM target group without cultivating more land or adversely affecting the environment, 
ensuring the longer -term sustainabil ity of these activities even in a situation in which the overall adverse climate 
trends in the region are likely to continue. Furthermore, a ll physical investments are carried out by private sector 
stakeholders, including MSMEs and small -scale agriculture producers, and they have full ownership of the 
installed equipment, machinery, infrastructure or land improvements. All maintenance functions, as well as 
renewal of investments as required, will be under the responsibility of the private owners.  
65. Sustainabl e Access to Financial Services . As a n additional sustainability element for the end -beneficiaries, the 
ARCAFIM CCA finance operations will be implemented in association with capable and interested partners 
from the financial sector such as the Host Bank an d loca l FIs. All financial transactions will be conducted 
through the bank accounts in financial institutions. The participating small producers and MSMEs will thus 
increasingly gain knowledge of and familiarity with the banking services and importantly , develop credit 
histories which will facilitate their ability to access bank funding in the future and in that way support the 
continued  development of their climate -smart farming and business operations .


---
*Page 39*
---

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 35 
 
 66. Exit Strategy for International Financing and ARCAFIM Replicability . The Host Bank  will repay the loans  to 
IFAD  according to the Subsidiary  Loan Agreement signed between the  Host Bank  and IFAD , including for 
successful repayments from end clients and  recoveries from defaulted clients during a defi ned period after the 
programme completion .  Subsequently, IFAD will distribute the proceeds among the international financiers in 
accordance with the loan agreements and envisaged Inter-creditor Agreement . With successful ARCAFIM 
implementation, t he capacit y of the Host Bank  will be strengthened to independently mobilize climate finance 
and manage CCA programmes, potentially in the capacity of GCF Direct Access Entit ies. 
67. The ARCAFIM implementation will create substantial replication opportunities. Following the ARCAFIM design 
and launch in East Africa, the plan is to replicate this climate finance mechanism in other regions of the 
continent including North,  South and Centr al/Wes Africa.   Further, the successful implementation of this project 
would encourage other financial institutions in the identification of new business opportunities in climate finance 
and change their risk perception of agriculture adaptation financing (see the discussion below  on paradigm 
shift potential ), using ARCAFIM’s basic concepts of blended funding, appropriate CCA taxonomies and top -
class TA .

 68. Capacity building and rural CC adaptation technology transfer are integral parts of the ARCAFIM 
programme. It will address non -financial barriers currently preventing greater CCA financing in the 
ARCAFIM target regions with three general types of capacity building and technology -related interventions. 
Firstly , it will strengthen the awareness and capacity of Participating Financial Institutions (PFIs) to offer 
rural CCA finance services  and strengthen innovative technology enabled solutions of the PFIs to r each out 
to small producers , including capacity building to address deep -rooted social and gender norms . Secondly , 
it will develop the capacities and skills of small -scale producers and rural MSMEs  to invest into technologies 
and solutions that are proven to improve their climate change resilience . Thirdly , ARCAFIM East Africa will 
provide investment resources of US$  180 million exclusively targeting CCA solutions and technologies. 
Fourthly , ARCAFIM will improve climate finance enabling environment through CCA finance policy 
dialogues and the development of a Taxonomy for CCA Rural Finance that can be imp lemented in East 
Africa and more widely in other developing regions . The details of these planned technical support and 
technology trans fer activities and implementation plans are presented above in Sections B.3 and B.4.  
69. Within the US$ 20 million of ARCAFIM non -financial services  in East Africa , the proposal is to include a 
US$ 10 million TA grant from GCF. This is 18 per cent of the total  requested GCF financing or 5 per cent of 
the total ARCAFIM budget, which will be matched by additional grant financing by other ARCAFIM financiers 
and cost -sharing by the PFIs (see Section C1 – Financing by Components). The proposed level of GCF 
grant sup port is highly justified due to the large unmet demand for CCA financing among ARCAFIM target 
groups and the need to develop CCA capacities and new technical approaches that could significantly speed 
up the expansion of private sector CCA finance activitie s in the target regions from their current low base.

 EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
D.1. Impact potential  
70. ARCAFIM will contribute to increased climate -resilient sustainable development and its outcomes are directly 
aligned with GCF’s ARA1 : Most vulnerable people and communities and ARA2:  Health, well -being, food and 
water security. The key outcomes of ARCAFIM include  (i) ARCAFIM PFIs expand and mainstream CCA finance 
in the region resulting in increased CC resilience and improved food secur ity levels of rural communities ; and 
(ii) Improved capacity of PFIs and agriculture value chain participants to adapt to risks imposed by climatic 
changes . Co-benefits from successful ARCAFIM implementation include increased rural household incomes, 
employment generation , improved biodiversity  and climate change mitigation  in the targeted communities . 
71. Small -scale producers, microenterprises and other rural p oor involved in local food systems  are the main end -
beneficiary target group of this program. Due to environmental, technical, economic, social and political factors, 
these small producers and MSMEs disproportionately experience the effects of climate chan ge and external 
shocks on their livelihoods. To maintain and improve their levels of productivity, they urgently need dedicated 
investment resources to build stronger resilience to climate change  but have been struggling so far to attract 
sufficient financing for this purpose. By making appropriate climate adaptation finance available to these target 
groups and improving the capacity of both demand and supply side actors to invest in climate adapta tion 
technologies and practices, ARCAFIM will improve the capacity of agriculture value chain participants to 
anticipate, absorb and adapt to risks imposed by climatic changes.  
72. As a result, the target groups will experience the following imp acts:  
(i) Stronger adaptive capacity and climate resilience  through better  technical capacity and ability to invest in 
climate resilient agricultural technologies and methods that reduce vulnerability to risks and future losses 
from climate change . 
(ii) Improved continuo us access to private sector CCA finance for small producers.  
(iii) Improved food security through investment in climate resilient agricultural technologies and methods which 
improve production practices and reduce risks and crop losses from climate change and contribute to 
increased crop yields.  
(iv) Co-benefits of  increased and more stable incomes through adoption of modern adaptation solutions which 
increase productivity , job preservatio n and new green job creation  for vulnerable groups through increased 
investments and economic activity in agribusinesses , biodiversity benefits  through strengthening the 
optimal use of natural, social and human assets , and climate change mitigation  through  enhanced 
agriculture technologies that also reduce emissions . 
73. Through  revolving loans during the implementation period of 12 years, the program is expected to provide 
260,000 loans worth US$ 266,000,000 across four East African countries, benefitting over 1.5 million people  
directly and indirectly . The impacts for the target group will include:   
• 208,000  households and MSMEs will adopt improved and/or new climate -resilient livelihood options  
• 268,000 households with 1,195,000 perso ns will experienc e improved food security as a benefit  
• 50% of beneficiaries are expected to be women and 30% youth  
• 208,000 ha  of natural resources will be brought under improved climate -resilient management practices . 
 
 
D.2. Paradigm shift potential  
74. A progressive but rapid shift in agricultural practices in  East will be critical as harsh climatic conditions will 
increase in many  regions  and new climatic threats for crop production and livestock keeping are projected to 
emerge. By 2030  - 2040, new and innovative agricultural systems need to be implemented in many regions 
where the climate will soon be no longer suitable for current practices. The lack of adequate and appropriate 
private sector financing for CCA investments is a serious threat  for the materialization of this transformation 
process.  Currently, there is a significant gap of  well-functioning private sector CCA finance programmes in 
ARCAFIM target regions with clear replication potential. Public CCA financing efforts are heavily grant -based, 
short -term focused and small in scale.  By making ARCAFIM a successful example and a showcase of 
commercial CCA finance, a paradigm shift  in climate chang e adaptation finance  can be achieved with the 
financiers as well as in the mindset of borrowers.


---
*Page 44*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 1.0 | PAGE 40 
   
 75. With ARCAFIM, the aim is to introduce a practical, widely implementable model for achieving  a paradigm shift 
towards systematic large -scale use of private financial resources for rural CCA investment s, the impact of which 
would go well beyond the direct impacts and time frame of the Programme . ARCAFIM’s TOC and its investment 
logic recognise that despite urgent need for increased investment volumes in low -income and lower middle -
income countries in Africa that are facing high climate stress, only very limited financing is currently allocated 
to CC adaptation investments. Particularly, financing by private sector financial institutions rarely includes loans 
for the rural adaptation projects of small producers. This has led to a very low overall level of CC adaptation 
investments in the food systems and rural value chains.   
76. The ARCAFIM interventions aim to change th is situation by introducing a CCA financing model that tackles the 
key barriers for CCA finance expansion and which at the same time is both sustainable , scalable  and replicable.  
Its interventions include blended financial resources for CCA finance investm ents to the Host Bank and to its 
local on -lending partners; appropriate risk management solutions; and targeted and focused capacity building 
and TA to both the participating financial institutions and the end -users of the CCA finance. Particularly, 
ARCAFIM will support the analytical development of the taxonomy for CCA finance operations that will support 
and guide the future implementation  of private CCA finance operations in different types of environments in 
Africa.  
77. While the direct ARCAFIM benefits w ill include the increased CC resil ience of the targeted small -scale 
producers and MSMEs, improved food and water security levels of rural communities , and increased and 
sustainable rural household incomes,  the obvious scaling -up and replication opportunities are expected to 
create a basis for a much wider longer -term impact through the  ARCAFIM -related  paradigm shift. With the 
successful ARCAFIM implementation, the capacity of the Host Bank will be strengthened to actively participate 
with its own resources in CCA finance operations on a much wider scale, and  independently mobilize climate 
finance and manage CCA programmes . In East Africa,  this learning opportunity is an openly expressed 
strategic reason for the partnering of the Equity Group with ARCAFIM, which  is expected to support the wider 
climate finance expansion strategies of th is leading commercial bank  in its region .  
78. For wider application of the ARCAFIM model o utside the direct ARCAFIM stakeholders, IFAD, the other co -
financiers , and the Host Bank  will proactively use different types of regional and international networks  to share 
knowledge and information of the ARCAFIM performance and achieved results.  When s ystematically carried 
out, the successful implementation of this programme  is expected to  encourage other African financial 
institutions  and international financiers  to re-visit their policies on climate finance and seek new rural business 
opportunities based on climate smart agricultural practices and modern CC adaptation technologies. It is 
expected that the key role in ARCAFIM of the leading commercial bank in the East Africa region (the Equity 
Group) will substantially increase this demonstration effect and encourage other financial institutions to 
increasingly participate in CC A finance operations . Furthermore, following the ARCAFIM design and launch in 
East Africa, the IFAD plan is , as indicated,  to replicate this private climate finance mechanism in other regions 
of the continent including South ern, North,  and West /Central Afri ca. 
79. Finally, to  further facilitate the materialisation  of this paradigm change, IFAD will lead the ARCAFIM -related  
efforts to improv e the  enabling environment  for CCA Finance  in Africa. IFAD would work closely with GCF -led 
network of National Designated Authorities to ensure that the developed CCA finance best practises will 
become broadly known and utilized. At policy level, the work will focus on incorporation of Climate Adaptation 
Finance Taxonomy developed by ARCAFIM into the national frameworks for the management and tracking of 
climate finance at country level.   
 
D.3. Sustainable development  
80. The proposed ARCAFIM programme is fully aligned with relevant Sustainable Development Goals  and the GCF 
approach and req uirement s on sustainable development potential in a CC adaptation programme of the 
ARCAFIM type.  Particularly important are the sustainable development potential and co -benefits of the 
programme in the areas of gender - responsive  development, economic benefits, soc ial benefits, and 
environmental co -benefits.  
81. Gender - Responsive  Development. ARCAFIM will take a deliberate, proactive, and positive approach towards 
gender equality and women ’s empowerment. To maximize the economic and social benefits at household level, 
capacity building and behaviour change communication to initiate critical reflection on deep -rooted social and 
gender norms , which perpetuate inequalities will be conducted  at all levels (from individuals, households, 
communities and groups, through to the host bank, PFIs and service providers). This will bring about a critical


---
*Page 45*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 1.0 | PAGE 41 
   
 paradigm shift towards GESI -inclusive CCA finance. . Through the network of PFIs, ARCAFIM will apply 
methods and approaches to increase financial and technical services targeting small -scale female farm 
operators and women -led agribusinesses. The programme technical advisory services will have a required 
gender balance of end beneficiaries, aiming at 50/50 ratio in service delivery , and at PFI level , include activities 
to design appropriate CCA financing products for women. The investment component target is to invest 50% 
of programme resources to women -led investments and to investments generating employmen t for women. 
Overall, ARCAFIM  will promote opportunities to address equitable access to and control over resources, 
opening greater opportunit ies for equal  socioeconomic participation , including girls' and women’s increased 
engagement in economic diversification.  
82. Economic Benefits.  Through successful Implementation of its activities, ARCAFIM is expected to result in 
major economic benefits to its rural target  group.  Immediate economic benefits will be gained at household 
level due access to finance  and markets  and with  improved technical capacity and ability to invest in increased 
production volumes and in modern production systems, which will increase the  crop yields  and lead to higher 
incomes for farmers . At the same time the v ulnerability to future losses will decrease and food insecurity due 
to climatic hazards will be avoided by adopting climate resilient agricultur al methods technologies. For the 
SMEs  in food value chains, ARCAFIM will improve incomes and sustainability with  investments to resource 
use efficiency and productivity -enhancing technologies , which will make possible the systematic expansion of 
these small enterprises serving the rural sector.  
83. Social Co -Benefits. The development and promotion of sustainable and adaptive farms and agribusinesses 
will contribute to several social benefits. Higher returns of land and labour will lead to improved psychological 
and moral well -being in the farming communities and relieve pre ssures associated with climate change -related 
hardships. Successfully implemented ARCAFIM interventions based on promoting the decent work agenda 
can also increase employment among vulnerable populations of women and youth, limit migration to towns and 
cities, and reduce conflicts over land and other limited resources. Higher and sustainable incomes levels would 
improve the opportunities to effectively use education and health services in the rural areas. Finally, 
participation in the rural savings and cred it arrangements, SACCOs and MFIs with support of programmes of 
the ARCAFIM type has proven to be one of the most effective women  and youth empowerment methods in 
Africa, often leading to substantial improvement of their economic and social position in rura l communities . 
84. Environmental Co-Benefits.  With the ARCAFIM investments, the selected, prioritised adaptation measures 
will ensure the protection of natural resources, maintain soil nutrient balance, reduce land degradation, improve 
biodiversity , and reduce pollution. At the same time the use of best available adaptation technologies and inputs 
will minimise environmental damage  as well as have a positive impact to climate change mitigation . These co -
benefits of ARCAFIM will contribute to increase d production with out cultivation of more land or affecting the 
environment. Overall, t he ARCAFIM -supported technologies and farm management solutions aim to strengthen 
the optimal use of natural, social and human assets; reduce the negative impact of food production to 
environment; and narrow the output gap.

income rural populations experiencing CC stress  in target countries. In Table 13 below, a  summary on the 
population, average incomes , and rural poverty in the four focal countries is presented, indicating low overall

86. The scale and intensity of climate risks and vulnerability to climate change within the target group are assessed 
in detail in the Country Vulnerability Assessments, summarised in Section B1 of this Funding Proposal. Based 
on these Assessments, the CCA tax onomy on eligible and bankable CCA investments for the needs of 
ARCAFIM client was developed  during the design .  
87. Considering the substantial and multi -dimensional nature of the current barriers for CCA finance, it is highly 
unlikely that CCA finance mainstreaming to the planned recipient s would happen in the private financial sectors 
of the targeted countries without support from programmes of the ARCAFIM type. The commitment of the 
leading commercial bank to manage the programme and to co -invest its own resources  into it increase s the 
chances of successfully reaching the planned recipients with appropriate services. The developed CCA 
taxonomy and substantial ARCAFIM technical support are aimed to increase the chances for implementation 
success and lat er, create a basis for  the scaling up and replication of the ARCAFIM model.  
D.5. Country ownership  
88. Climate  change adaptation is a priority for all ARCAFIM participating countries. In particular, ARCAFIM will 
contribute to targets and commitments in National Adaptation Plans (NAPs), National Climate Change Policies 
and Strategies, National Communications (NCs),  SDGs National Strategies for Disaster Risk Reduction and 
the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). Each of the target countries has reiterated the importance of 
adaptation in their revised NDCs and the agriculture sector is a priority to ensure clim ate change resilience of 
communities. For the agriculture and rural development sectors, the scope for adaptation includes protecting, 
managing and restoration of rangelands; ensuring resilient access to water supply for productive purposes; 
promoting sust ainable water harvesting and storage; scaling -up Integrated Water Resources Management 
approach and use efficiency; promoting use of renewable energy sources and energy efficient technologies; 
scaling up climate smart agriculture including agroecology; pro moting climate resilient capture fisheries; 
promoting highly adaptive and productive livestock breeds; promoting development of climate resilient crop 
varieties and expanding postharvest handling, storage, value addition and marketing, among others.  
 
89. These  priorities are part of the initial taxonomy being developed for ARCAFIM that financial institutions can 
apply in the selection of investments. Through the adaptation options being financed, ARCAFIM will contribute 
to meeting the targets and commitments in  the NDCs and national climate strategic priorities of all selected 
countries, as summarised in Table 14 below, covering the ARCAFIM target countries in East Africa region.

90. IFAD, as the accredited entity, has experience in climate change adaptation with a focus on agricu lture and 
rural development.  IFAD invests in building resilience of rural populations and promotes better targeting of 
climate finance and inclusive rural finance for small -scale producers. Through the Adaptation for Smallholder 
Agriculture Programme and rural finance investments, IFAD has developed strong capacities in targeting 
investments to small -scale rural producers and MSMEs for climate change adaptation. Through ARCAFIM, 
IFAD will collaborate in East Africa with Equity Group , which has a strong Afr ican footprint and appropriate 
climate change -related strategies and action plans. The due diligence confirms the bank’s capacities to deliver 
on the ARCAFIM objectives with the strengthening of some areas such as the reporting on adaptation benefits 
accru ing from the investments.  
 
91. To increase country -level ownership beyond the participation in the ARCAFIM proposal development and 
implementation, governance support and alignment with regional and national climate change strategies and 
polices will be instru mental. The NDAs from the participating countries have been engaged through the 
Concept Note and F ull Funding  Proposal development. The ARCAFIM concept was presented and discussed 
with each NDA to confirm the inclusion of their countries in the Programme a nd also obtain their inputs to shape 
the Programme. The presentation and discussion with the Kenya NDA was held in April 2023; for Rwanda the 
presentation and discussion was done in August and September 2022; for Tanzania the presentation and 
discussions w ere in February 2022 and for Uganda the presentation and discussion were held in October 2022. 
The NDAs confirmed the alignment of ARCAFIM with their national priorities  such as mobilization of climate 
finance  and the opportunities presented for private se ctor engagement. They reiterated the relevance of the 
ARCAFIM to identify viable climate change adaptation investments for the agriculture and rural development 
sectors.  The inputs received from the NDAs were on the potential strategies for gender, youth a nd social 
inclusion in the programme; type of technical assistance that would be required particularly by the small scale 
producers and MSMEs; strategies for building synergies with existing programmes and projects and; capacity 
development potential for n ational agencies working with climate finance.  
 
92. Alignment to and potential complementarity with GCF and IFAD financed initiatives in the target 
countries. In the ARCAFIM target countries, the project will benefit from synergies with IFAD and GCF 
investment portfolios. In particular , linkages will be bui lt with country strategies and ongoing and upcoming 
projects that support development of climate resilient production systems and value chains for small -scale 
producers.  In terms of IFAD country strategies in each of the four countries, which are developed  alongside 
Governments, t heir strategic objectives focus on promoting  sustainable production, marketing, access to 
finance, and climate adaptation and resilience , to which ARCAFIM is highly complementary .  
 
93. In terms of projects, t he synergies will be mutua l between existing GCF and IFAD projects , helping to enhance 
the impact of ARCAFIM and vice -versa. Key ways that the existing projects will complement ARCAFIM will be 
to identify pipeline investees and collaborating partners for ARCAFIM -supported financing  institutions. At the 
same time ARCAFIM can enhance the impact of existing IFAD and GCF projects by providing technical 
assistance and complementary financing to beneficiaries, in particular for the various projects aiming to develop 
value chains but in wh ich access to finance remains a barrier.  
 
94. In both directions, lessons and expertise from experience can be fed into the design and implementation of the 
ongoing and future initiatives. Of particular note in this regard is the green financing taxonomy to be developed 
through ARCAFIM that can serve as a resource for other green financial inclusion projects. Below in Table 15,

 95. Commitment and Capacity of Executing Agencies to Deliver ARCAFIM. As described in detail in Sections 
B.3 (Programme Description) and B.4 (Implementation Arrangements), the Executive Agen cy for ARCAFIM  
East Africa , Equity Group,  is a leading commercial bank in the  region . Both at the Group and the country 
subsidiary level, th is bank is fully committed to the implementation of ARCAFIM. The bank  sees the programme 
as an important and integral part of its overall environmental and climat e change strategy  and as an instrument 
to develop and expand their climate change adaptation finance operations to all levels of the key food value 
chains, including particularly the smallholder production activities. The due diligence processes conducted by 
IFAD confirmed the Equity Group’s operational and financial status and capabilities suitable for operating both 
the financial and non -financial components of ARCAFIM, as well as the bank’s capability to manage the foreign 
exchange risk related to the pr ogramme loans.  Furthermore, to ensure the successful implementation of 
ARCAFIM by the Executing Agenc y, substantial high quality TA support will be provided from ARCAFIM -related 
budgets both to the Host Bank and its on-lending local partner FIs to further develop the skills of the participating 
financial institutions to implement this large -scale CCA finance programme and to appropriately monitor and 
report on its performance and impact.    
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness  
 
96. ARCAFIM Blended Finance structure . The Blended Finance Structure is used for the ARCAFIM in order to 
mobilize local private sector financing (i.e. Host Bank ) for CCA by means of de -risking structures. The Blended 
Finance structure of ARCAFIM will consist of USD 180 million for Ea st Africa, consisting USD 45 million from 
GCF (USD 21 million for 1 st loss, and USD 24 million for mezzanine/2 nd loss), USD 45 million from IFAD or 
IFAD organized international co -financiers, and USD 90 million from Equity Group . Therefore, the expected 
mobilization ratio of GCF funding is 1:4 at the Facility level. There is further mobilization expected at the 
unde rlying portfolio if the  end-beneficiaries are able to attract further financing from local financial institutions. 
The IFAD adheres to the DFI enhanced blended finance principles  to ensure crowding -in private sector 
financing and minimum concessionality, w ithout crowding out private finance.  
 
97. Reinforcing markets (Principle 4 under the DFI blended finance principle) . The ARCAFIM financial 
structure aims to develop a market that addresses the root causes of market failure, while providing adequate 
incentive to financiers. From the Host Bank perspective, credit risks are mitigated by the credit protection 
provided by IFA D with the financial support of international financiers (partners) . Therefore, financial institutions 
are incentivized to make prudent credit decisions towards the final borrowers. At the same time, the Host Bank 
is contractually obliged to on -lend directly or indirectly to small producers and rural MSMEs for CCA 
investments in order to promote lending to that particular niche. In addition, Technical Assistance provision 
address lack of sector -specific capacity and st rengthen the potential for greater private sector participation over 
time, and eventually create and drive bankable demand in the target groups in collaboration with the Host Bank .

 E.4. GCF Outcome level: Enabling environment (IRMF core indicators 5 -8 as applicable)  
Core Indicator  Baseline context  
(description)  Rating for current 
state (baseline)  Target scenario  
(description)  How the project will 
contribute  Coverage  
Core Indicator 6: Degree to 
which GCF investments 
contribute to technology 
deployment, dissemination, 
development or transfer and 
innovation  There is limited awareness 
and lack of understanding of 
climate change adaptation 
investment opportunities, 
technologies, and benefits 
among small producers and 
MSMEs. There is limited 
knowledge on climate change 
adaptation in the private 
financial sector,  and a lack of 
incentives, which  has resulted 
in a lack of suitable lending 
products (resources) offered to 
finance climate change 
adaptation technologies and 
practices.  low PFIs (and other financial 
institutions) have incentives, 
skills, and resources t o 
incorporate CCA financial 
products as a part of their 
mainstream finance offering, 
which results in small 
producers and MSMEs 
accessing and using 
appropriate financial products 
to adopt CCA technologies 
and practices.  ARCAFIM will provide financial 
and n on-financial support to 
develop  and deploy  tailored 
financial products to scale up 
CCA finance; and will share 
lessons learned and best 
practices to encourage wider 
adoption of CCA finance in the 
region.  
 Multi -countries  
Core indicator 7: Degree to 
which GCF Investments 
contribute to market 
development/transformation at 
the sectoral, local, or national 
level While there are some 
initiatives in the region 
facilitating the development of 
a climate finance market, there 
is still limited use of private 
financial resources for rural 
CCA i nvestments and the 
market’s ability to serve small 
rural producers and MSMEs 
remains limited due to: a lack 
of information on the market 
potential, low demand, a lack 
of appropriate long -term 
financing, and an unfavourable 
investment environment with a 
lack of incentives for 
sustainable development of 
the market.  low The Host Bank is actively 
participating with their own 
resources in CCA finance 
operations on a much wider 
scale, independently 
mobilizing CCA programmes. 
There is increased demand 
and uptake amongst small 
producers and MSMEs of new 
appropriate financial products 
for climate smart agricultural 
practices and modern CC A 
technologies. The success of 
these products and sharing of 
best practices  with other  
financial institutions in the 
region  further  expand s the 
availability of  CCA finance  to 
small rural producers and 
MSMEs.  ARCAFIM will help 
mainstream CCA finance by 
testing the implement -ability of 
the model with  private entit y, 
promote competitiveness and 
most appropriate CCA finance 
imple mentation models, and 
integrate lessons learnt from 
one sub-region into effective 
messages on how to promote 
CCA finance on a wider scale 
over the African continent.  Multi -countries  
Core indicator 8: Degree to 
which GCF investments 
contribute to effective 
knowledge generation and There is limited awareness 
and knowledge within private 
financial institutions o f best 
practices in implementing and low Best practices in CCA finance 
for small rural producers and 
MSMEs is used to improve 
and expand CCA finance by ARCAFIM provides financial 
and non -financial (technical) 
support to Partner Financial 
Instit utions to improve and Multi -countries

 E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements  
98. The purpose of Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E)  for ARCAFIM is to continuously monitor and evaluate 
performance, assess whether the program is on track to deliver expected results and targets, generate data to 
improve performance, and increase learning in the field of Climate Change Adaptation (CCA) fi nance. 
ARCAFIM’s Theory of Change and Logical Framework will guide the M&E approach for the programme. The 
M&E approach outlined here will be further developed and refined during the inception phase s with the 
necessary inputs of stakeholders in the M&E pro cess.  
99. Prior  to ARCAFIM  launch , during  legal  documentation  phase,  dedicated  short  term design  resources  are 
used  for start-up preparations  including  recruit ment  of an ARCAFIM  M&E  Specialist  for the Host Bank  at the 
banking  group  level.  Such  specialist  would  provide  focused  support  to the design of a core financial reporting 
system for ARCAFIM with key indicators for the programme funds utilization and portfolio st atus, to be operated 
as a part of the main Host Bank performance reporting system. The M&E Specialist would also support the 
development of the additional, specific M&E reports on ARCAFIM operations, that (i) respond to the eligibility 
criteria and MIS and reporting requirements of the programme and (ii) can be operated as much as possible as 
a part of the standard IT -based accounting system of the Host Bank.  
100. In the Host Bank Management Information System , as a key principle, the books of accounts will have the  
loans issued from the project  funds specifically coded as ARCAFIM loans and reported a s eligible investment 
types for CCA financing. This will make possible a systematic on -line reporting and tracking of the ARCAFIM 
portfolio, as well as conducting separate annual audits on its accounting, with audit reports submitted to all 
investors in th is programme . 
101. M&E Stakeholders . While IFAD leads reporting back to the GCF, the Host Bank has a prominent role in data 
collection and analysis both for monitoring and evaluation aspects. For East Africa Region, Equity Group will 
provide  regular  performance  reports  to IFAD,  based  on a pre-defined  reporting  schedule,  indicators,  and 
formats,  to be agreed -upon  during  inception  between  IFAD  and the Host Bank.  The Equity Group already  has 
robust  data management  systems  in place  as well as dedicated  staff experienced  in reporting  requirements  of 
key international  development  financiers . In detail, the ARCAFIM M&E system will rely on coordination and 
data collection by the key stakeholders in project implementation . 
Stakeholder  Role  Responsibility  
IFAD  Accredited 
Entity  IFAD will implement and coordinate the overall M&E system; organize and lead the 
Annual Supervision Missions and Midterm and Final Evaluations; guide ARCAFIM 
stakeholders in appropriate M&E requirements and standards;  allocate resources to 
the stakeholders and service providers for the M&E tasks and deliverables; aggregate 
the M&E data and present it in overarching reports and knowledge documents; verify 
and ensure quality and validity of the M&E data and information f rom the various 
sources under ARCAFIM; and organize learning events to improve the programme 
performance and facilitate sector wide learning.

and PFIs  Executing

collection and analysis for the ARCAFIM portfolio under its management. The Host 
Bank and other PFIs will collect and report on baseline data, portfolio information, as 
well as output and select outcome indicators; participate in Annual Supervision 
Missions, Midterm and Final Evaluations; and participate in learning and adaptive 
management events. Resources are earmarked to support the preparations within the 
Host Bank and other PFIs to establish the required financial reporting systems for 
ARCAFIM wi th key indicators for the programme funds utilization and portfolio status, 
to be operated as a part of the main performance reporting system .

102. M&E Approach. The overall M&E approach for ARCAFIM is built around its Theory of Change and Log -Frame 
and builds in adaptive management. Details on the specific tools are included in the Logframe in Section E, 
summari zed below:  
• Monitoring Activities: ARCAFIM will monitor program activities on an ongoing basis, providing progress 
updates in Annual Reports and all activities will be reviewed and adaptive measures identified during Annual 
Supervision Missions (more details below).   
• Monitoring Outputs: ARC AFIM will monitor program outputs by collecting data annually from  the Host Bank, 
who will lead data collection on outputs and report annually. Progress updates will be provided in Annual 
Reports, and all outputs will be reviewed an d adaptive measures identified during Annual Supervision 
Missions.  
• Evaluating Outcomes: ARCAFIM will evaluate program me outcomes , especially related to the expected 
climate results,  at 3 years, 6 years, 9 years and 12 years of implementation. These evalua tions will include a 
Household/Business Surveys of a sample of loan recipients . At 6 years and 12 years the detailed Interim and 
Final Evaluations are conducted (more details below). A before/after comparison with contribution analysis 
will be used to anal yse program outcomes and impact, and the program will use IFAD’s scorecard 
methodology for small -scale producer resilience measurement.  
 
103. IFAD Missions. IFAD will conduct Annual Supervision Missions  according to its own policies and procedures. 
The purpose of IFAD’s Annual Supervision Missions is to assess progress made in the implementation of 
activities , identify issues and challenges affecting implementation and agree on key strategic measures to 
enhance project performance. Annual Missions deliver a report with an updated M&E logframe with public 
summaries of the reports made available on IFAD’s website. These missions are followed up every six months 
by Bi-Annual Implementation Support Missions  which provide an opportunity to follow up on key 
recommendations made in the Annual Supervision Report.   
104. Evaluations.  At years 6 and 12, the I nterim and Final  Independent  Evaluations will be planned  and managed 
by IFAD according to the requirements of the GCF ’s evaluation criteria as per the GCF Evaluation Policy, and 
according to needs of evidence based ARCAFIM management. In advance to the Evaluation Missions, the

 RISK ASSE SSMENT AND MANAGEMENT  
F.1.  Risk factors and mitigations measures  
Selected Risk Factor 1 : Weak CCA Finance Market Development   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  High Medium  
Description  
The financial sectors in the ARCAFIM target countries have not yet established CCA finance frameworks for the 
mainstream financial operations and private FIs  have  very limited CCA finance portfolios. This leads to the core 
operational risk for any CCA fina nce programme in the region: limited demand by small producers and MSMEs for 
appropriate CCA financing and limited capacity to meet the demand by offering appropriate CCA product and 
services by financial institutions. The risk often results in low disburs ement volumes of CCA schemes part icularly in 
Sub-Saharan Africa.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
To mitigate this risk, substantial TA resources are included under ARCAFIM to stimulate both the demand and supply 
side. The Host Bank  will have delivery teams and systems set up at each country to professionally implement the 
scheme. High -class TA will be provided to achieve high staff capacities in the Host Bank to develop appropriate CCA 
finance products and to train their local partne r FIs in CCA financing and management. The beneficiary training will as 
well be adequately coordinated and resourced. Further, CCA investment progress and performance data collection 
and monitoring are of key importance, and this capacity will be developed  in-house at the Host Bank . Residual risk 
after the mitigating measures is expected to be moderate to low.  
Selected Risk Factor 2: Credit  Worthiness and Non -repayment Risk  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Credit  Medium  Medium  Host Bank will conduct more comprehensive Household/Business Surveys including both quantita tive and 
qualitative data collection and analysis. The analysis will not only evaluate direct Outcomes and medium -term 
results, but also assess the programme’s contributions to a long -term sustainable paradigm shift and enabling 
environment using GCF’s three -point scale rating. The ARCAFIM unit within IFAD  will lead the evaluation 
process following IFAD’s procurement guidelines and will be supported by IFAD’s Research and Impact 
Assessment (RIA) unit. The evaluations will be supported by an Evaluation Reference Group, made up of IFAD, 
GCF and the other co -financiers of ARCAFIM. This group will review and approve the terms of reference , the 
findings and the reports. The Host Bank will participate in the evaluations as needed.  
105. Annual Reporting. The Host Bank as key Executing Entity will provide quarterly repor ts to IFAD on financial 
progress and comprehensive annual reports to IFAD against  workplans, safeguards and  the Logical Framework  
indicators . IFAD as ARCAFIM Accredited Entity will provide annual reports to GCF  on the Funded activity 
according to GCF requi rements and will share those reports with other financiers. These reports will track and 
report progress on finances, results, management, workplans, activities, environmental and social safeguards, 
gender action plans, and all IRMF -related data and requir ements. The narrative will also cover:  
• Progress against intended objectives according to the ToC, with quantitative reporting on indicators and a 
qualitative analysis on what, why and how results are being achieved and contextual changes  
• A qualitative summary of any observed contribution to paradigm shift  
• A qualitative summary of any observed progress or contextual changes in the enabling environment  
• Lessons learned, challenges, and adaptive management measures undertaken.  
 
106. Aside from Annual Reports to GCF and Annual Supervision Reports, ARCAFIM will publish additional 
knowledge products for wider public under Component 2.3 on topics that will be identified throughout the  
programme implementation and  M&E process.


---
*Page 69*
---

E 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 1.0 | PAGE 65 
   
 Description  
The credit risk of small -scale producer and rural MSME finance is widely perceived as high by both international and 
regional FIs, and many FIs therefore shy away from this market. According to a recent study by Aceli Africa46 on agri -
lending in Sub -Saharan Africa, the average agri -lending portfolio default rate of commercial banks are estimated at 
6% and in the 4.9-7.7% range, 3-5% higher than commercial lending in other sectors.  Small producers face a similar 
or higher risk compared to the agri -MSMEs. Compared to standard agricultural loans to small producers, the risks 
with CCA finance instrume nts are unknown . 
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The ARCAFIM credit risks from the Host Bank perspective are mitigated by credit protection provided by IFAD . The 
real credit risk of CCA finance is unknown at the moment with insignificant or no systematic credit default data 
available for this specific sec tor. The proposed protection is, however, deemed adequate to convince  the Host Bank 
to enter or expand to the relatively unknown private sector field of CCA finance. Residual risk after the mitigating 
measures is expected to be moderate to low.  
Selected R isk Factor 3 : Foreign Currency Risk  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Forex  Medium  Low 
Description  
ARCAFIM lending at the national level will be made in local currency while the international repayments by the Host 
Bank are made in US$ . This causes in the schemes of the ARCAFIM type a potential currency risk  which may 
adversely affect the programme disbursements, outreach and impact . 
Mitigation Measure(s)  
Under the ARCAFIM lending, the Host Bank manage s the currency risk of the US$ AR CAFIM financing through its 
internal treasury operations. The Host Bank, by design, is selected for its size, regional presence, financial strength, 
strong governance, and compliance with banking authority regulatory guidelines, including foreign currency exposure. 
In terms of loans to beneficiaries, loans from the Host Bank are expected to be in local curre ncy, and in hard currency 
only in circumstances where the borrower has the ability to hedge the foreign exchange risk, e.g., an exporter which 
earns revenues in US$. It is in the interest of the Host Bank and the borrower that the Host Bank only provides h ard 
currency loans to forex -earning enterprises  that can manage the FX risk. After these mitigation measures, the forex 
risk is con sidered  low for the successful programme implementation.  
Selected Risk Factor 4 : Money laundering and Financial Crime  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Prohibited practices  Medium  Low 
Description  
In the ARCAFIM type of international lending operation, the possibility of prohibited  practices such as money 
laundering and other financial crimes needs to be considered and seen as a danger for successful programme 
implementation. Operating in the targeted countries, with sometimes fragile control environments, increase s the 
potential for such risks.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The EDD assessment s support the implementation of IFAD’s Anti -Money Laundering and Countering the Financing 
of Terrorism Policy47 and related Procedures and reduce the risk that IFAD and the financiers of ARCAFIM conduct 
business with counterparties that are on international sanctions list s or have been involved in money laundering, 
terrorism financing or related risks . The Enhanced Financial Crime/KYC assessment fulfils IFAD’s fiduciary 
responsibility to protect i ts assets against potential misuse and ensure that its financing is not used for money 
laundering or terrorism financing .  
 
46 https://aceliafrica.org/bridging -the-financing -gap-unlocking -the-impact -potential -of-agricultural -smes -in-africa/   
47 EB2019/128/R.41/Rev.1  (https://webapps.ifad.org/members/eb/128/docs/EB -2019 -128-R-41-Rev-1.pdf)

 Selected Risk Factor  5: Over -Indebtedness of Small Agribusinesses  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Credit  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Risk of over-Indebtedness of  small producers and agribusinesses  can materialize, if in a relatively large scheme such 
as ARCAFIM, loans are provided to the low -income borrowers beyond their repayment capacity . Even if such practices 
can provide short -term relief against the adverse CC impact, the overall longer -term c onsequences to the target group 
can be negative and increase instability in the borrowing households.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
To mitigate the risk of  small  agribusinesses taking loans beyond their repayment capacity, the aim of ARCAFIM 
Output 2.2 is to strengthen the smallholders’ and MSMEs’ knowledge of viable CCA options and benefits, appropriate 
financial services, business planning capacity, investment s tart-up and financial management skills of the borrowers , 
and thereby lower the investment risk for the PFIs. Furthermore, bankability assessments will be carried out for all 
potential borrowers , to help to inform the PFIs of the real investment risks and opportunities. The capacity building 
and BDS services will be demand -driven and target well -planned, productive, and screened investments in the rural 
agricultural sectors, thereby reducing the risks of over -indebtedness of the borrowers.  After  these  mitig ating 
measures, th e over -indebtedness risk is considered to be low.  
Selected Risk Factor 6 : Macroeconomic and Governance  Risk s 
Category  Probability  Impact  
Governance  High Medium  
Description  
Maintaining stable economic growth levels during ARCAFIM implementation period is likely  to be a challenge in most 
of the target countries. Public debt levels can increase and surpass standard risk levels , global economic slowdown 
may continue to cause inflation and interest rate fluctuations, and supply chains of agriculture inputs and outputs may 
continue to be disrupted . Due to COVID -19 and environmental shocks, the economies contracted in various target 
countries and with the impacts of the war in Uk raine, the recovery is expected to take longer than was initially 
assumed, potentially affecting much of the ARCAFIM period. Furthermore, in various target countries, g overnance 
and transparency issues persist, resulting in high governance and corruption -related risk ratings.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
A key safeguard against governance and corruption -related risks is the private sector orientation of all the 
investments under ARCAFIM , as well as IFAD’s safeguards, practices and policies on zero tolerance to fraud and 
corruption48. This concerns both the private financial institutions that issue the services financed by ARCAFIM and the 
borrowers that utilise these services. At the same time , the impacts of adverse political and macro -economic 
deve lopments, such as the recent Russian attack to Ukraine, are  mitigated through the Host Bank financial stability 
and policies guiding their pricing and risk assessment. However, the external risks  are not possible  to be fully 
eliminate d in light of the inte nsity of the recent and ongoing external shocks . The residual impact of this risk is 
considered medium.  
 
 
48 https://www.ifad.org/documents/38711624/40189366/fraud_e.pdf/ab42fc11 -b213 -49af-8df3-e70af2076650?t=1519055057000
