 GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS  
G.1.  Environmental and social risk assessment   
107. The environmental and social risk category is moderate based on the screening of the potential ARCAFIM 
investments and the expected micro/ small scale and scope of these  investments . The end line investments will 
be made in non -sensitive locations  and potential adverse effects from them will be localized , mostly reversible 
and manageable with appropriate actions as proposed in the generic Environment and Social Management 
Plan (ESMP) in the ARCAFIM Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) . The ESMF  
provides guidance for managing its environmental and social (E&S) risks. The ESMF places emphasis on the 
E&S risk management processes for the Programme’s implementing entities; the Host Bank, with additional 
guidance provided to the Participating Financial Institutions (PFIs) and end beneficiaries.  
108. The ESMF aligns with the GCF E&S Screening Guidelines and E&S Policy, IFAD’s Social Environment and 
Climate Assessment Procedures (SECAP) as well as the relevant E&S  legislation and guidelines in each of 
the ARCAFIM beneficiary countries. The ESMF builds on E&S risk management processes of the Host Bank, 
ensuring that gaps identified in the E&S due diligence (ESDD) of Host Bank are addressed including tools and 
templa tes.  
109. The ESMF is based on a programme level E&S risk assessment of ARCAFIM’s financing activities and 
outlines the broad E&S risks applicable to all ARCAFIM stakeholders, such as E&S -related incidents, 
stakeholder engagement and grievances, E&S management  skills and capacity, etc. Based on this assessment 
the ESMF provides the appropriate guidance notes to mitigate these risks. The ESMF will also briefly describe 
specific E&S risks that may be applicable to end beneficiaries, depending on the specific clim ate change 
adaptation investments that will be made. Risks (identified by the ARCAFIM country risk research) may include 
environmental pollution related to increased use of agro -chemicals (fertilizers, herbicides and pesticides), 
improper waste and wastewa ter management, land degradation (resulting from use of sub -optimal agricultural 
practices) and deforestation, climate change shocks, use of child labour, restrictions of freedom of association, 
substandard terms and conditions of employment, and exacerbat ing the vulnerability of women farmers  and 
indigenous peoples .  
110. The ESMF describes the E&S risk management process to be applied by the Host Bank when selecting PFIs, 
namely an assessment of the comprehensiveness of PFI’s own E&S management systems. The ES MF also 
describes the risk management process to be applied by the Host Bank (direct lending) or PFIs (indirect 
lending) when selecting end beneficiaries, which will categorise the individual climate change adaptation 
projects according to their E&S risk a nd screen the end beneficiaries’ capacity to manage these risks. The 
recommendations for capacity enhanced are based on short questionnaires provided to the PFIs. As part of 
the ESMF requirements, the end beneficiaries, PFIs, the Host Bank and the ARCAFIM programme as a whole 
will all monitor and report on the management of E&S issues in their respective spheres of influence. These 
E&S risk management processes will be supported by risk categorisation, screening, due diligence and 
monitoring tools/templates .  
111.  
112. In line with GCF IP Policy (Article 38), an IPPF will be prepared i n order to ensure culturally appropriate 
consultations for communities self -identifying as indigenous peoples . 
 
G.2.  Gender assessment and action plan   
113. ARCAFIM’s Gender Equality & Social Inclusion Action Plan (GSIAP) is informed by the GESI assessments of 
the Host Bank and a sample of potential PFIs. ARCAFIM end beneficiaries (once known) shall be assessed 
based on a Generic GESI Self -Assessment tool.  Bo th GESI assessment tools are aligned to GCF’s Gender 
Policy and Project Planning Template Assessment Questions and IFAD’s gender mainstreaming criteria .   
114. The GSIAP outlines   an Action Plan with activities, indicators and targets, timelines, responsibilit ies, and 
costs. The GSIAP includes the results from the sample PFI Gender Assessments. As part of the ARCAFIM 
implementation, the Host Bank will provide further input and customize the action plan to their operational


---
*Page 72*
---

F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 1.0 | PAGE 68 
   
 needs and realities, particularly with  respect to the GSIAP timelines, responsibilities, and costs and the results 
from the additional PFI’s that will be engaged.  
115. Baseline data from  the Host Bank, potential PFIs and the Country -level socioeconomic analysis supported the 
development of an ARCA FIM Program Gender Equality & Social Inclusion Action Strategy. The Strategy aims 
at identifying and addressing the causes of vulnerability for women and girls and ensuring equal participation 
and benefits from the ARCAFIM funding activities, the ARCAFIM G ESI Action Strategy includes a GESI vision 
and policy statement, potential requirements and recommended action plan outputs for  the Host Bank and 
PFIs, a description of programmes, scope of application within the Host Bank, PFIs, milestones and generic 
completion targets for Host Banks, PFIs, and a monitoring strategy with KPIs, roles and responsibilities.  
116. Early GESI actions include conducting gender and social norms diagnostic studies at country and local levels 
to better understand the context in which AR CAFIM will be operating. Gender, youth and disability lenses will 
be applied understanding social norms, CCA technologies and practices, and financial products and services. 
These insights will provide the foundation for stimulating all stakeholders (from programme staff, host agency, 
PFIs and service providers through to community members and leaders) engage in critical reflection on norms 
that perpetuate inequalities and stimulate positive behaviour change in the inclusion -climate -finance nexus.  
 
117. As a result of capacity building for critical reflection and behaviour change on social inclusion issues, PFIs will 
view women, youth and people with disabilities as potential eligible borrowers of CCA finance. Under sub -
component 2.1, PFIs will address su pply-side barriers to CCA finance by seeking innovative ways to establish 
credit worthiness in the absence of traditional collateral, providing relevant CCA products and services, and 
identifying digital and non -digital routes to access services.  
 
118. At the c ommunity, household and individual levels, the shift in gender norms and attitudes will create an 
enabling environment in which women, youth and people with disabilities will be able to make independent 
decisions to borrow money and adopt CCA technologies and practices. Under sub -component 2.2, the demand 
for CCA finance will be strengthened by developing their climate adaptive knowledge and skills, financial 
literacy, entrepreneurship and business skills.  
 
119. Activities under output  2.3 will further strengthen the enabling environment for inclusive climate finance at 
policy level.   
 
120. The outcomes for women  and youth as a result of accessing CCA finance will be securing climate change -
resilient livelihoods with the benefits of food an d water security (with reduced workloads of household tasks); 
gains in voice and decision -making over income and the use of assets and outputs, including income 
generated; improved business and employment opportunities; and reductions in harmful practices and 
domestic violence. The principal institutional gains will be the ability of the host bank  and PFIs to expand the 
outreach of CCA finance to individuals that have traditionally been excluded, and a more conducive policy 
environment.  
 
121. These outcomes wil l feed into ARCAFIM outcome 1.3 (increased CC resilience), outcome 1.4 (improved food 
and water security) and the co -benefits 1 (increased incomes) and 2 (employment generation).  
 
G.3.  Financial management and procurement  
122. Disbursement Arrangement. During the availability period of the first 5 years, the Host Bank (i.e. Equity Bank 
(Kenya ) Limited ) will make a disbursement request to IFAD based on the disbursement projection every six [6] 
months under the Subsidiary  Loan Agreement. Equity Bank (Kenya ) Limited  will on -lend the amount received 
from IFAD to  other  participating  country subsidiaries  of EGH , namely Equity  Bank Uganda  Limited , Equity Bank 
Rwanda  PLC, and E quity Bank (Tanzania ) Limited , in accordance with the disbursement projection in each 
country subsidiary through sub -loan agreements. T he Host Bank and the other participating  country 
subsidiaries will be able to make a disbursement for the loans qualifying the ARCAFIM eligibility crit eria and 
CCA taxonomy (the “Qualifying Loans”) from the fund that they have received from IFAD, and Equity Group’s  
own contribution equal to the fund  from IFAD . Once the Qualifying Loan is disbursed, the Host Bank should 
submit the inclusion request to IFA D to notify IFAD of a list of the Qualifying Loan s, so that IFAD can monitor 
the status of the  portfolio  and disbursement to end -borrowers under the  Facility .

 123. Procurement. Under the ARCAFIM Technical Assistance (TA) programme for technical support to the Host 
Bank (Equity Bank Kenya) and to the intermediary PFIs, the Host Bank will coordinate the service delivery, 
utilizing either their own internal service units (i.e. Equity Group Foundation) with ARCAFIM support or 
recruiting external technical service p roviders. Where external TA is required, the Host Bank’s Procurement 
Policy will apply. The Accredited Entity (AE) has reviewed the Host Bank’s Procurement Policy and found to 
be adeq uate and equivalent to the AE’s procurement rules, policies, and procedure  to the extent and scope of 
its accreditation , as it: (a) promotes best practices, transparency and professionalism in all acquisition 
processes within the Host Bank fraternity; (b) ensures that suppliers of goods and services are subjected to a 
competitive tendering process in order to achieve quality, price competitiveness and reliability; (c) ensures that 
all expenditure is in accordance with prior approved budget and procurement plan; and (d) ensures compliance 
with applicable regulations and l egislations. Procurement will be carried out by the Host Bank and procurement 
plan will be agreed between the AE and Host Bank. For the procurement oversight, the AE will provide prior 
review on all the major procurement as identified in the below threshol d, and conduct supervision every 6 
months to review the procurement processes conducted by the EE.  IFAD will put in place to ensure that the 
procurement carried out by the EE’s is compliant with EE’s procurement guidelines that are equivalent to the 
AE’s p rocurement guidelines .     
124. Prohibited practices.  The Subsidiary Agreement has the clauses that the EE has the obligation for the 
reporting of irregularities or prohibited practices, if any, during the implementation period. In addition, the 
prohibited practices are included in the IFAD Policy covenants in the Subsidiary Agreement.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information.  
☐ No confidential information:  The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may 
be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.  
☒ With confidential information:  The accredited entity declares that the  funding proposal, including its annexes, may 
not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited 
entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:  
• full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with 
an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the 
accredited enti ty’s disclosure policy, and  
• redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.  
The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential 
information.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel ’s assessment of 
FP220  
Proposal name:  Africa Rural Climate Adaptation Finance Mechanism (ARCAFIM) 
for East Africa region  
Accredited entity:  International Fund for Agricultural Development  
Programme countries : Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda  and the  United Republic of Tanzania  
I. Summary  
1. The Africa Rural Climate Adaptation Finance Mechanism ( ARCAFIM ) for East Africa 
region aims to scale up private sector agriculture and climate adaptation finance in Eastern 
Africa ( specifically, Kenya, Rwanda , Uganda,  and the United Republic of Tanzania ) through an 
on-lending program me that supports smallholder farme rs and rural micro, small and medium 
enterprises ( MSME s) involved in food systems.  The climate goal of the programme is the wider 
use of commercial resources for Climate Change Adaptation (CCA ) in the region, enhancing the 
well -being of rural communities through climate -resilient livelihoods.  
Targeted programme 
size  USD 200 million (loan: USD 180 million; grants: USD 20 million)  
Loans (USD)  Grants (USD)  
GCF 45 m illion   GCF 10 m illion  
IFAD 45 m illion  (to be raised)  
IFAD (N ording Development Fund)  
15 million  
IFAD (G overnment of Finland)  10 
million  
Unfunded: 20 million  IFAD 10 m illion  (including 5 
million  from the Government of 
Denmark)  
Equity Group 90  million   
Total: 180 m illion   Total: 20 m illion


---
*Page 86*
---

 
GCF/B. 37/02/Add. 11 
Page 82 
 
 
Proposed GCF 
involvement  A total commitment of USD 55 million, comprising USD 45 million in 
subordinated loans and USD 10 million in grants.  
Recipient of GCF 
assistance  The International Fund for Agricultural Development  (IFAD ) will be the lender 
of record of the ARCAFIM funds and will on-lend the GCF funds to the executing 
entity, Equity Bank (Kenya ). Equity Bank will, in turn, on -lend facilities to its 
subsidiaries in Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania.  Equity 
Bank will th en on-lend the funds to the end borrowers in the four  target 
countries, directly  (70%) through its own subsidiaries and  indirectly  (30%)  
through financial institutions such as microfinance institutions (MFIs), savings 
and credit cooperatives (SACCOs), and FinTech companies . These will on -lend 
the funds for the eligible activities under the program me. The e nd borrowers 
are smallholders and rural MSME s. 
The IFAD ARCAFIM loans will also be matched 1:1 by Equity Bank , ensuring 
scale and alignment of interest for the programme.  
II. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
2.1 Impact potential       Scale:  Medium–High  
2. Increased weather unpredictability and frequency of extreme weather events will 
continue to have  an impact on the operations of the smallholders and MSMEs in the target 
countries , as they do not have the capacity to adapt and respond to climate change. New and 
innovative technology and practices are needed for the smallholders and MSMEs , as current 
practices will no longer be suitable.  
3. Agriculture investments in Africa, regardless of climate change, have always been 
viewed as problematic due to the structu re of the sector  consisting mainly of smallholder 
farmers . However, the perceived risk of the smallholder agriculture sector in Africa has been 
exacerbated by  the unpredictability of  climate change.  In the context of changing climate and its 
variability, i nvestments in climate -resilient and low -emission agriculture are perceived as high 
risk, especially by the local banking sector. There is currently a limited financial product 
available for the agriculture sector and existing products involve  high interest rates, collateral 
requirements,  and short repayment periods.  
4. The programme addresses the financial and non -financial barriers that prevent more 
CCA  financing in Eastern Africa. The programme will provide CCA  loans to smallholders and 
rural MSMEs t hrough a leading commercial bank in the region. CCA  loan products , which will 
aim to pass the concessionality to the end  borrowers , will be financed as follows :  
(a) 40 per cent of the loan programme will be distributed  by the host bank (Equity Bank) by 
providing direct micro -loan s to small holder farmers  
(b) 30 per cent of the loan programme will be distributed  by the host bank and subsidiaries 
by providing direct loans to small and medium -sized enterprises ; and  
(c) 30 per cent of the loan programme will be distributed  by providing wholesale loans to 
MFIs, SACCOs which will on -lend for climate adaptation as per eligibility criteria.  
5. As a result, the programme would strengthen the resilience of the target beneficiaries in 
the nasc ent CCA financial sector and thereby support sustainable agriculture, food and water 
security, resilient livelihoods and improved sustainable land use.


---
*Page 87*
---

 
GCF/B. 37/02/Add. 11 
Page 83 
 
 
6. More directly, the program me aims to enhance CCA  finance access for smallholders and 
rural MSMEs involv ed in the food system , impact ing 1,160,000 direct beneficiaries and 330,000 
indirect beneficiaries , in the following ways:  
(a) It is expected to provide new loans to 260,000 smallholders and 500 MSMEs worth USD 
710 million through revolving loans.  
(b) It will stre ngthen awareness and improve the capacity of 20 participating financial 
institutions to provide rural CCA  finance services.  
(c) It will i mprove the capacity and awareness of the smallholders and rural MSMEs  by 
raising  financial literacy and their knowledge of CCA  financial products and services.  
(d) A taxonomy will be used as a key governance tool to identify eligible and bankable CCA  
investments.  The taxonomy prepared by IFAD is an impressive foundational tool which , 
if successful , can be widely replicated in the  areas of the  banking sector aiming to enter 
adaptation finance . However,  IFAD must ensure that it be adopted as part of Equity 
Bank’s due diligence process, and that it does not become merely another check -box tool 
used by the loan officers.  
7. The main risk to the programme achieving its desired impact is the executive entity 
aiming to recycle loans when they are paid back by the end  beneficiaries.  From a perspective of 
scale and its impact, this makes sense , as this design utili zes the same capital multiple times 
over the lifetime of the programme, so that it reaches many more people.  However, the trade -off 
of recyclable capital is that , ultimate ly, loans may need to be short -term and there may be  undue 
pressur e on the end beneficiaries  to pay back over  shorter periods of time that they are able to.  
These beneficiaries are particularly  vulnerable to unpredictable climate -related events , such as  
droughts, which could delay, minimize,  or ruin yields, making the short tenors of loan 
repayments risky.  This is a risk which is currently not sufficiently addressed in the design of the 
programme.  
8. CCA  loans may enable the end beneficiaries to be more productive and resilient,  but they 
could also negatively impact the financial resilienc e of the se beneficiaries and , in the process , 
reconfirm to  the financial sector that this segment is not bankable, which would undermine the 
paradigm shift potential.   
9. Other factors potentially undermining the impact  include : 
(a) Equity Bank being unable to place loans due to  a lack of an investible pipeline (driven , 
among other things , insufficiently incentivized loan officers but also due to the fact  that 
Equit y Bank has a strong network in the target sector but  still a nascent track record in 
the other subsidiaries );  
(b) Limited u tilization of the loan if Equity Bank does not pass on  the concessionality of the 
GCF/IFAD loan (subsidi zed pricing plus additional first loss) to loan clients , and   
(c) The dependence on  IFAD raising the balance of the funds .  
2.2 Paradigm shift potential      Scale:  Medium  
10. Scale:  The creation of financial products in the banking system tailored to small holders 
and MSM Es in the agriculture value chain, both to improve their efficiency and to increase their 
resilience to climate change.  The programme has correctly identified interventions such as : 
(a) Improving the  management information system and technical support to enable  a 
participating financial institution to pilot digital technologies such as geographic -based 
information about the agricultural models and expected  yields ; 
(b) Developing digital payments and mobile banking financial services ; and


---
*Page 88*
---

GCF/B. 37/02/Add. 11 
Page 84 
(c) Implementing data analytics trainings to make data -driven decision credit assessment
and risk management of the loans.
In addition, IFAD has developed a systemic and analytical approach to CCA financing by 
developing a first -of-its-kind adaptation investment taxonomy . If successful, this tool would  
provide  an appropriate  conceptual framework for scaling investments in this sector  via a 
systematic, analytical approach that can be  adopted by other financial institutions . 
11. Profitability: Given the small size of the loans, the ability to use digital technologies
successfully could showcase how banks and other financial institutions could reach this
segment profitably and at scale.
(a) Identifying and piloting adaptation finance for the agricul ture sector could bring the
nascent financing form grants to commercial viability, further strengthening the options
for supporting some of the most vulnerable segments of the population in East Africa.
12. Co-financing from a regional bank:  IFAD ARCAFIM loan s being  matched 1:1 by Equity
Bank (the executing entity ) and a regional commercial institution already showcases the
potential for CCA  financing.
13. One key point to flag  is that the programme is limited to one institution:  Equity Bank.
It is unclear how this will enable transformation of the entire industry. A stronger paradigm
shift would have been to  apply  at least the technical assistance component of the  programme to
other banks over time.
14. Finally, there also is no convincing theory of how this program me will, over time , enable
replication with less subsidy and risk mitigation.
2.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High  
15. Small rural  producers are responsible for an estimate d average of 80  per cent  of food
production in Sub-Saharan Africa , while MSMEs involved in food systems provide services ,
valuable jobs,  and income opportunities to rural low -income populations.  These two segments
disproportionally experie nce the effects of climate change and external shocks on their
livelihoods.  To maintain and improve their levels of productivity, they urgently need dedicated
investment resources to build greater resilience to climate change but have been struggling to
attract sufficient financing.  Hence, the ARCAFIM programme – if it can deliver sufficient and
cost -effective financing  – would achieve high co -benefits:
16. Social and environmental potential co -benefits  include p romot ing food security ,
improv ing quality of land through sustainable land management practices  and h igher returns of
land and labour, which will positively affect the livelihoods of farmers .
17. Economic potential benefit s include i ncreased crop yields , resulting  in higher income for
farmers  and i ncreased resilience of the agriculture sector .
18. Gender  & Age/ related co -benefits : The programme will target 50 per cent programme
resources to women -led investments and generate employment for women . It will also target  30
per cent of programme resour ces to youth.  Increased financial and technical services will target
female small -scale farm operators and women -led agribusinesses.


---
*Page 89*
---

GCF/B. 37/02/Add. 11 
Page 85 
2.4 Needs of the recipient  Scale:  High  
19. The ARCAFIM target countries under Phase 1 in East Africa region are Kenya, Rwanda
Uganda,  and the United Republic of Tanzania , selected for the following reasons :
(a) They are either low-income countries or lower middle -income countries ;
(b) All are under severe climate change stress, which is higher than  average in developing
countries , as stated by the  Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change  (IPCC ) report ,
Climate Change  2022:  Impacts, Adaptation and Vulnerability1;
(c) There is a substantial  market of small -scale agriculture production with high potential
for rural CCA financing ; and
(d) The Equity Bank has significant operations and suitable delivery networks to reach the
intended ARCAFIM target groups with CCA finance products.
20. As mentioned a bove, the main concern is how the knowledge gained by  any success of
CCA financial products in agriculture  will be shared with the whole industry – rather than
limited to only one institution and its related network of MFIs and SACCOs.  The independent
Technical Advisory Pa nel ( TAP )  is reassured  by the fact that IFAD’s innovative CCA taxonomy
for agricultural value chains tool will be widely shared with the market  (as confirmed by IFAD
in the written questions and answers).
21. The scale and intensity of climate ris ks, and the  vulnerability to climate change within
the target group , are explained in the funding proposal and the country vulnerability
assessments . More than 70 per cent of the population still live in rural areas , where population
growth continues,  and people rely heavily on climate -sensitive rain -fed agriculture. Projections
indicate that the reduced crop productivity is expected to increase poverty and food insecurity2.
The resources needed for adaptation continue to increase and it is evident that th e involvement
of the private sector is crucial. The programme aims to provide long -term CCA  capital in this
nascent sector of low -income or lower middle -income countries.
22. Small -scale farms account up to 90 per  cent of crop production in the region and the
main source of employment3. By providing CCA  loans to this sector, the programme will address
the economic and social needs for the smallholders and rural MSMEs. The financial proposal
emphasi zes the lack of alternative sources of funding for this sector  and correctly identifies how
the current funding market does not serve target market, except for grants.
2.5 Country ownership  Scale:  High  
23. The programme would directly contribute to the implementation of the commitments in
nationally determined contributions  (NDCs) national adaptation plans, national climate change
strategies and policies of the four  target countries.
1 IPCC. 2022. Climate Change 2022: Impacts, Adaptation and Vulnerability . Available at

24. The adaptation component  of the target countries ’ NDCs requests focus on the following
objectives4:
(a) Enhanced adaptive capacity and climate resilience of the agricultural sector ;
(b) Promotion and scal ing up of climate -smart agriculture interventions to increase
productivity in an en vironmentally sustainable way ;
(c) Development and wide sharing of a knowledge system ; and
(d) Mobilization of financial resources from foreign and domestic capital markets.
25. The programme will have a commitment of co -financing and implementation from a
leading co mmercial bank in the region, Equity Bank, subsidiary of Equity Bank Holdings
Limited.  Equity Bank Kenya will on -lend the loan to Equity Bank Uganda, Equity Bank Rwanda,
and Equity Bank Tanzania through sub -loan agreements. Equity Group Holdings is the larg est
banking group in the region and has a strategic focus to increase its rural and agricultural
portfolio, a track record of micro lending in the agriculture sector, a strong balance sheet to
match the international capital investments , and an ability to hedge fund in USD and disburse in
local currencies. The programme will ensure the bank will scale up its overall environmental
and climate change strategy, strengthen ing its capacity to develop, assess and implement
climate adaptation financial products.
2.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale:  Medium  
26. The programme will provide CCA  loans to smallholders and rural MSMEs , and expects
1,160,000 direct beneficiaries, which represents 0.7 per cent of the population , and 330,000
indirect benefici aries , which represents 0.2 per cent of total population.
27. The co -financing ratio is 1:2.6, and the programme aims to leverage USD 55 million of
co-financing from international institutions and USD 90 million from a regional commercial
bank.
28. The financial s ustainability of the programme is highly dependent on GCF participation.
The programme includes a  strategy to phase out GCF support and operate on a commercial
basis in the long term.
29. The programme will also provide training and on -lend ing to four  equity group
subsidiaries and 20 participating financial institutions, which would ensure that CCA loans
become mainstreamed in the financial  ecosystem.
30. Ecosystem building : There is a satisfactory selection of a regional African executing
entity with strong loca l branches in all four countries.  In the East Africa region, the Equity Group
Holdings  operates in ARCAFIM target countries , having a wide rural branch network supported
by a large number of banking agents. In total, the Equity Group Holdings has over 330 branch
offices and over 53,000 agents in the ARCAFIM target countries. With this significant outreach ,
and its history as an active lender to small -scale farmers, Equity Group Holdings is well
equipped to provide CCA loans direct ly to smallholders and rural MSMEs. Therefore, direct
loans to end borrowers are expected to have a large share of the ARCAFIM portfolio in East
Africa.
31. Generally, there is a risk that concessionary credit lines to specific banks may distort
markets and create dominant  or monop olistic institutions . This happens when  the concession is
4 Kenya . 2020. Updated Nationally Determined Contribution ; Republic of Rwanda . 2020. Updated Nationally 
Determined Contribution ; The United Republic of Tanzania . 2021. Nationally Determined Contribution ; The Republic

captured by one institution which comes to dominate a market and may have market 
distortionary effects.  This risk is apparent in the present program me.  
32. The risk of market distortion may be  mitigate d in this programme if there are
alternative channels  to Equity Bank  that  effectively reach the ARCAFIM target group with CCA
financing services . The program me targets 30  per cent  of the loans volume to be issued through
other lenders such as rural SACCOs and MFIs, which operate with large rural clienteles in this
region .
33. In order to support a market -based and sustainable interbank lending market in the
region, there is a strong rationale for Equity Bank to lend through MFIs and SACCOs. The
commercial bank s in the region have a significantly higher liquidity position compared to
SACCOs and MFIs, which are less able to mobili ze savings and international capital.  On the other
end of the spectrum, local commercial banks have less appetite to invest into the gr owing
agriculture market – compared to SACCOs and MFIs – but many have a better ability to access
capital and have high liquidity.  Despite this unbalance in capital accumulation in the financial
market, SACCOs and MFIs have limited opportunities to lend fr om the commercial banks to
increase their investments among rural clients. Therefore, strengthening the channel between
Equity Bank ( which can serve as  an example for other commercial banks in the future) and the
smaller local financial intermediaries woul d be key to achieve a sustainable market -structure
for climate adaptation investment  that  would not need to rely on donor -supported or
government -led funds but would function through private -sector market interests.
34. Given that strong rationale, the independent TAP found  it surprising that a minimum 30
per cent  overall  portfolio  target for on-lending  through other channels ( which  are not Equity
Bank) is not predetermined  in the subsidiary loan agreements and recommend it is included in
the relevant legal documents between IFAD and Equity Bank.
35. Strong executing entity with regional outreach in the target countries and target
sectors:  Based on IFAD’s extensive rural finance experience in Africa and reviews of financial
markets in the targeted countries , as well as interviews with several candidate banks, Equity
Group was selected as the host bank for the executing the ARCAFIM programme in East Africa.
The criteria for identifying the host bank identification included  a status of a leading commercial
bank in the targeted countries, solid institutional and financial conditions, a strong interest in
agriculture and rural finance operations as reflected in the size of portfolio or foreseen
strategies to scale up lending to the sector , a corporate objective to develop and scale  up the
bank’s climate finance and particularly climate adaptation finance operations, a regional branch
or agency network to operate in at least four or five  countries relevant to ARCAFIM, a strong
express ed interest to manage and co -finance the ARCAFIM programme, and established
governance, social and environmental management structures.
36. The regional approach, based on a strong regional executing entity , will allow the
programme to achieve economies of scale  and efficiency, and  demonstrate a  significant effect by
leveraging the strengths of a large -scale regional partner organi zation with a high level of
environmental, social and governance integration in its operations, a strong balance she et,
appropriate operational capacities, and an ability to hedge fund in US D and offer loans to low -
income borrowers in local currencies. The phased approach will also enable learning by testing
the implementation of the model with strong partner organi zations in East Africa and
integrating lessons learned into effective messages on how to promote CCA finance on a wider
scale over the African continent.
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
37. The IFAD A RCAFIM  is a well -designed programm e which aims to lower barriers to CCA
finance for agricultural and rural beneficiaries in East Africa.  The independent TAP commends
the programme for tackling both the supply of adaptation credit (especially the CCA  finance
taxonomy for the food system) an d the demand by the end beneficiaries  for such credit .

38. The independent TAP also  commend s the partnership with a regional bank , Equity
Group , as an executing entity, which already has wide -reaching networks in the region, along
with its  large contribution to the funding of the programme at USD 90 m illion . In addition, if the
programme is well executed, it may have long -term, market -making potential in enabling large
commercial banks to on -lend to smaller and less well capitali zed micro -SME finance and SACCO
institutions.
39. Therefore, the independent TAP endorses  this funding proposal , subject to the
condition s below :
40. Condition 1: Subsequent fundraising condition : IFAD states that for the balance of USD
45 million  that it needs to raise -- in addition to the USD 45 m illion  funding from GCF – it is in
negotiations with the Nordic Development Fund and the Government of Finland. It is not
guaranteed that these funds will materiali ze. In order to ensure the programme’s ability to
leverage GCF capital to crowd in additional capital in CCA financing, the independent TAP would
add a condition that GCF funding be disbursed pro rata to additional funding raised by IFAD.
41. Condition 2: Passing on the concessionality to end beneficiaries.
(a) Under the terms of the proposed loan agreement between Equity Bank and IFAD (acting
as trustee for the GCF), and as currently indicated in the Operations Manual (Annex 21),
Equity Bank will have to submi t inclusion notices to motivate successive disbursements.
Under the current terms of the Operations Manual, Equity Bank would not have to
provide evidence that it passes on at least a portion of the concessionality (consisting of
the below -market interest rate combined  with the first loss risk mitigation) of the
IFAD/GCF loan to its clients  and beneficiaries (e.g. in terms of tenor and/or interest
charged). (The Operational Manual only stipulates that “[…] each Qualifying Loan must
be provided at the lowest  end of market interest rate range as compared to similar risk
sharing products provided by similarly mandated financial institutions, however, not
below an interest rate viable for the market in order to avoid potential market
distortion .”) The Operations  Manual, and ultimately the loan agreement between IFAD
and Equity Bank, should contain a provision making it obligatory for Equity Bank to
provide, as part of the inclusion notice, evidence that the concessional terms of the
IFAD/GCF facility are passed o n to its loan clients. Such evidence could include
calculations of the amount Equity Bank would have charged its loan clients if it had
funded these loans from its own balance sheet, or other objectively verifiable market
benchmarks.
(b) IFAD should also fores ee implementation of an annual audit to determine whether
Equity Bank has passed on concessional terms to its loan clients.  These audits would
involve a comprehensive review of the Equity Bank loan portfolios, interest rates
charged and compliance with the  conditions set by IFAD.
42. The independent TAP would  also add the following three  recommendations, which
would enable the programme to achieve its stated climate , economic  and social co -benefits:
43. Recommendation 1 : Minimum lending through different local int ermediaries:  Given
the high risk of market distortion, where existing rural SACCOs and MFIs have limited access to
finance, it is important to maintain that at least 30  per cent  of the loans to end beneficiaries are
disbursed through these intermediaries.  In other words, the independent TAP suggests  that a
maximum of 70  per cent  of the loans be channelled through the host bank or its subsidiaries.
44. Recommendation 2: Incentivizing loan officers : (Concessional) lines of credit are a
tried and tested method to drive investment in climate action through local financial
intermediaries. However, experience shows that many lines of credit, and especially those that
are designed to achieve ambitious promotional objectives, are not drawn. The commitment fee
that is envisioned (0 .5 per cent  annually on undrawn amounts) provides Equity Bank with a
strong incentive to utili ze the line of credit provided by IFAD. However, the key will be to


---
*Page 93*
---

 
GCF/B. 37/02/Add. 11 
Page 89 
 
 
properly incentivize loan officers in the respective Equity Bank country operations to originate 
a portfolio of new projects that would qualify under the terms of the program me. There is 
significant theoretical demand , but that will have to be translated into a pipeline of investible 
opportunities for Equity Bank. Equity Bank s hould be required to submit, prior to first 
disbursement, a more comprehensive plan of how it will incentivize and support its loan officers 
to originate this new target portfolio.  
45. Recommendation 3: Monitoring gender equality and social inclusion indicator s: 
Among the 260,000 loans (worth US D 266,000,000) expected to be disbursed across the four 
East African countries, 50  per cent  of beneficiaries are expected to be women and 30  per cent  
youth. For the maximum 70  per cent  of the loans to be disbursed throug h Equity Bank directly 
or through subsidiaries, the independent TAP recommends  that IFAD track s whether 
beneficiaries are  50 per cent  women and 30  per cent  youth (to be defined) in each of the four 
countries, and report to GCF on whether the targets have b een met, and if not, why not.  If such 
targets are consistently not met over  a period agreed with the GCF Secretariat, IFAD is to 
investigate and report back on why the targets have not been met .

 ARCAFIM GENDER EQUALITY AND SOCIAL INCLUSION STRATEGY  
INTRODUCTION  
The overall development goal of ARCAFIM is “ Livelihoods in rural communities across the target countries rendered 
climate resilient through increased CCA investments, CCA capacity, employment creation and rural incomes”. The key 
ARCAFIM objectives are: (i) increased climate change  resilience of smal l-scale producers and rural micro -, small - and 
medium -sized enterprises (MSMEs)  active in the local food systems through CCA investments across agricultural  
value chains; and (ii) improved capacity of agriculture value chain participants to adapt to risks imposed by climatic 
changes. An underlying ambition of the programme is to achieve a paradigm shift towards systematic large -scale 
use of private financial resources for rural CCA investments.  
The programme has set a target to invest 50% of programme resou rces for women -led investments and to 
investments generating employment for women (and 30% for youth), and for advisory services to achieve a gender 
balance among the end beneficiaries in service delivery.  
This annex sets out the main parameters of the ge nder and social inclusion (GESI) action plan for ARCAFIM. Part 1 
provides a brief GESI analysis of smallholders, MSMEs and financial institutions in the context of climate change 
adaptation (CCA) and financial inclusion. Particular attention is paid to the  importance of identifying and addressing 
social norms which often act as key barriers to the financial inclusion of women, youth and people with disabilities.  
Part 2 provides an overview of the GESI action plan setting out the main actions and targets. Th ese will be further 
developed during early implementation.  
PART 1: GESI ANALYSIS  
Whilst modest gains are being made by East African rural women in improving their livelihoods, gender inequalities 
persist in many areas, such as political representation, access to education and training, access to quality and 
affordable healthcare, emplo yment, sexual and gender -based violence, and harmful cultural practices.1 Despite the 
presence of progressive legal and policy frameworks promoting gender equality, their implementation is weak. In 
2021, Kenya, Republic of Tanzania and Uganda lie in the l owest quartile of countries ranked on the UNDP’s Gender 
Inequality Index , which reflects gender -based inequalities in health, empowerment and economic dimensions.2 
Contributing factors include entrenched discriminatory social norms , which are reflected in  the OECD’s Social 
Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI) and in the African Gender Index.3 Rwanda has the lowest levels of inequality and 
discriminatory social norms in the East Africa region.  
Climate change is not only one of the greatest environmental and development challenges facing the world today, 
but also  one that will have critical impa cts on human rights and inequalities, including gender inequality , where  
women and girls suffer disproportionately from the effects of climate change due to a variety of social, cultural,  and 
economic factors.  Rising temperatures, irregular rainfall patte rns and seasonal uncertainty will severely disrupt all 
aspects of rural livelihoods through widespread crop failure/loss, food insecurity and malnutrition, outbreaks of 
human and animal diseases, dislocation of human populations, and destruction of propert y and infrastructure. As 
livelihoods and incomes become more precarious, the viability of households and communities will be challenged. 
There is an ever -growing body of evidence that identifies the critical role women must  play in climate change 
mitigatio n and adaptation - women are innovators, leaders, entrepreneurs, workers  and customers in climate 
 
1 https://moderndiplomacy.eu/2021/02/20/east -africa -the-status -of-women -remains -unequal -at-all-levels -of-society/  
2 https://hdr.undp.org/data -center/thematic -composite -indices/gender -inequality -index#/indicies/GII  
3 OECD. 2022. SIGI Policy Highlights  for Ea st Africa  From Data to Policy Action: Tackling Gender -Based Discrimination in Social 
Institutions. OECD Development Centre https://read.oecd -ilibrary.org/view/?ref=1153_1153894 -wse22t2a31&title=SIGI -Policy -
Highlights -for-East-Africa

*Page 98*
---

Page 94 
 
   
 related fields. Whether at a global, national,  or local level, within a political, business  or social context, many are 
already leading the fight in the clima te crisis .4  
As global temperatures climb, widespread shifts in weather systems occur, making events like droughts, hurricanes,  
erratic rainfall,  and floods more intense and unpredictable . Agriculture is the most important employment sector 
for women in low and lower -middle income countries during these unpredictable weather events . Although  women , 
youth and indigenous people s are disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change and associated 
severe weather events , they remain underrepresented, both in key decision -making positions and as users/end 
beneficiaries of concessional finance for climate adaptation.5  
Women and girls are impacted disproportionately from the effects of climate change due to factors such as 
entrenched discriminatory social and cultural norms, a lack of entitlements to land and other credit assets, and 
unequal access to land, water, and prod uctive assets, compounded by limited mobility and decision -making power 
in many contexts. Their mortality from climate -related disasters is higher than that of men, and climate -induced 
floods and droughts exacerbate women’s poverty and unpaid domestic and care work burdens. W omen are 
impacted in their roles in smallholder cash and subsistence crop and livestock systems, including upstream and 
downstream MSMEs in the food value chains, to livelihoods dependent on natural resources (including fisheries, 
fores try and non -timber forest products). The burden of household tasks , which typically fall to women and girls - 
such as collecting water and fuel, or securing nutritious food – become even more onerous. As lives become more 
stressful, the incidence of gender -based violence and other conflicts is likely to increase.  
Yet in many African countries, women are recognized as agents of change who make valuable contributions to 
climate change mitigation and adaptation efforts. They are building climate resilience and enhancing the livelihoods 
and well -being of their families by inv esting in climate -smart agriculture and sustainable energy solutions for home 
and work. They are also finding innovative ways to adapt to climate change impacts on their communities and 
environments,6 for example by switching to drought -resistant seeds, e mploying low impact or organic soil 
management techniques, or leading community -based reforestation and restoration efforts.7 
Gender  
The ARCAFIM is a timely innovation to extend loans for rural adaptation to small producers in food systems and rural 
value  chains. Without specific initiatives to strengthen inclusion, the ability of rural women to develop resilient 
livelihoods through the opportunities presented by CCA finance will be constrained by a range of gender -based 
inequalities.8 For example:

Page 95 
 
   
 • As smallholder producers9, women tend to utilize  smaller plots of less productive land than men and have 
fewer resources including labour, improved technologies and inputs. They focus on the subsistence 
production of crops and livestock with limited acces s to information, advisory services and engagement 
with markets. With insecure tenure over land and other natural resources, they may be reluctant to 
commit to CCA measures and unable to access finance.  
• As entrepreneurs running micro - and small -scale enter prises10, women typically engage in low value 
addition activities, utilizing  low-cost and labour -intensive technologies. The majority will be active at the 
lowest end of the scale of MSMEs (with under 10 employees and less than US$ 100,000 revenue).  
• Larger enterprises working along the food system value chains will recruit women as employees  where 
they may experience gender gaps in pay, terms and conditions of employment, opportunities for 
advancement, and practical facilities at the workplace.  
• Although many women are active members of VLSAs and SACCOs, they save and borrow small  sums of 
money.11 They often have a weak voice in intra -household household decision -making regarding the use 
of resources, including the income they earn. In many contexts, authorization from a male is required for 
women to open a bank account, obtain loa ns, enter into a contract or register a business.  
• Women’s ability to invest in CCA technologies and practices , either as smallholders or entrepreneurs, is 
compromised by many factors including a lack of awareness of opportunities, low mobile phone 
ownershi p, limited financial literacy and a lack of business skills to develop sound CCA investment 
proposals. With insecure land tenure, a lack of an identity document, an absence of credit history and no 
formal bank account, women are unable to meet standard col lateral requirements. They generally have 
an aversion to risk (especially regarding relatively new investments such as CCA) and prefer investments 
yielding benefits in the short -term. As a result, women often end up being offered very small short -term 
loan s, which invariably serve only as working capital, whereas larger medium -scale and longer -term loans 
are needed for women entrepreneurs to move from micro -enterprise status to nascent SME status. 
Without access to credit, women must settle for suboptimal l ivelihood choices, forgoing opportunities to 
boost their productivity, enhance their  income, and improve their climate resilience and ability to cope 
with a variety of shocks.  
• Financial institutions  are often hesitant to regard women smallholders and small  entrepreneurs as ‘safe’ 
borrowers. This is despite evidence showing that women -owned small businesses have the lowest 
proportion of non -performing loans, and that women and women -owned businesses tend to be more 
prudent borrowers than their male counterpa rts. The range of financial products and services often 
overlook the specific needs of these groups. Indeed, gender -blind products may inadvertently reinforce 
gender inequalities.  
• Women and women -led organizations have limited representation and engagemen t in decision -making 
processes  relating to climate change adaptation and mitigation, rendering their views and priorities 
invisible.  
 
9 For ARCAFIM the definition of “small producer” is based on th e criteria: (i) engaged in agriculture activity in crops, forestry, 
livestock, aquaculture or fisheries, (ii) main household income from the agriculture activity, (iii) majority of  agriculture work 
carried out by family members, (iv) the ARCAFIM supported small loans are appropriate for the household agriculture 
investments.  
10 In ARCAFIM, the eligible microenterprises and agribusiness -SMEs are profit -oriented enterprises that are involved in the 
agricultural value chain either directly or by providing enabl ing services to value chain actors. Following the IFC classification, a 
microenterprise has less than 10 employees and revenue less than $100,000; s small enterprise has less than 50 employees and 
revenue less than $3 million; and a medium enterprise has l ess than 300 employees and revenue less than $15 million.  
11 IFAD. 2022. In Kenya, for example, women typically receive less than 10 per cent of formal credit to smallholders and only 1 
per cent of formal credit to agriculture, and instead rely on informal  sources.

Page 96 
 
   
 All these barriers reflect a range of social norms , which perpetuate negative gender inequalities and hinder  women 
from eq ually participating in and benefiting from development interventions, including their ability to borrow CCA 
money . Gender norms can determine to a great extent the level to which women’s financial inclusion is possible (see 
box 1). However, it  is important  to recognize that women are a heterogenous group (by age, wealth, socio -economic 
status, marital status) and this has significant implications for their resource endowments - including labour - and 
their freedom to make independent decisions. In turn, thi s influences their information -seeking behaviour, ability to 
attend training, develop new skills, take out loans, adopt new practices, and make decisions regarding income 
earned. Among those often most disadvantaged are women heading their own households a s a result of never 
marrying , divorce or widowhood; they typically account for around 25 -30% of all households in the four participating 
countries.    
Box 1: Examples of gender norms and their impact on financial inclusion  
Negative g ender norms : 
• Women should not have an independent bank account  
• Women should not own their own mobile phone  
• Women should not make independent decisions regarding the use of their income  
• Women should not take a loan without their husband’s permission  
• Women should be responsible for covering basic household expenses  
• Women should focus their time and energy on household duties  
• Women should not travel to town alone  
Norm holders  include traditional influencers, like husbands, parents, in -laws, community and traditional 
leaders and peers, and also non -traditional influencers like marriage counsellors  
Rewards  for following the norms include  women  being regarded as a role model, having the guarantee of a 
strong marriage, and being respected and praised by the community.   
Sanctions  for women  who do not comply with the norms can take the form of being called negative names, 
divorce, suffering violence, and being disowned by one's own parents.  
Norms are context specific and can change over time . For example, norms may be more r elaxed for 
wives in polygamous marriages  or when men are not able to provide for their family  or as a result of 
men and women participat ing in women’s rights education .  
 
The significance of addressing the root causes of women’s financial exclusion has been recognized increasingly in 
recent years.12 Unless negative norms and behaviours are addressed, women still face challenges in accessing formal 
financial products even when customized financial products and services are available. Similar ly, with the heightened 
focus on adaptation to climate change, the need to recognize and address the uneven distribution of vulnerability 
to and gender -differentiated impact of climate change is critical.13 Climate  services can risk reinforcing the gender -

 based inequalities if they fail to understand and effectively target the differentiated needs of both women and 
men.14,15  
Hence it will be essential for ARCAFIM to identify and address both the underlying causes of gender inequality and 
their symptoms, if women are to engage fully with CCA finance opportunities. This will have implications for the 
success of the programme in developing a more resilient agricultural sector and rural economy  in the short term, as 
well as contributing to the sustainability of the longer -term goal to develop climate change resilient livelihoods and 
communities.  
Youth  
Populations in East Africa are  youthful, with approximately 20% aged 15 – 24 years old.16 Farming and self -
employment in the agri -food sector are the dominant categories of youth employment in rural areas.17 In regions 
where there are few employment opportunities, entrepreneurship presents a real opportunity to better their 
livelihoods. 18 However, although the rural youth may be perceived as ‘drivers of economic development’, they can 
face many challenges from inappropriate education systems and inadequate skills, limited training opportunities, a 
lack of access to assets (including land), a lack of access to goods and services, and financial exclusion (they are often 
not seen as bankable). In turn, this can lead to unemployment, landlessness, financial exclusion, o utmigration, and 
child labour in agriculture.19 
Youth is a heterogeneous group (in terms of gender, age, ethnicity, cultural specificities, educational level, 
employment status, skills and capacities) and of a transitory nature in time. Young women face a double challenge 
of being young and being female, due to gender -biased norms, a higher chance of being married and pregnant at an 
early age, limited physical mobility, and more household responsibilities. Since youth represent a significant 
proportion of t he population, it is essential that priorities and opportunities cater for their different livelihood 
options.  
People with disabilities  
People with disabilities typically account for around 2 -5% of the East African population.20 They are overrepresented 
among the poor, unemployed, face stigma and experience marginalization in multiple dimensions of life.21 Climate

 change and external shocks tend to exacerbate these inequalities. Multiple and entrenched barriers, which are 
embedded in institutions, norms, at titudes and physical spaces, constrain their ability to fulfil their fundamental 
rights including access to health care and other vital services.  
Persons with disabilities, especially young persons with disabilities, often live on the margins of their community. 
Families and communities may assume that they are unable to engage in mainstream activities such as attending 
school which subsequently results in a lack of skills; low confidence, expectations and achievement; and limited  
workplace opportunitie s. Many disabled women and children face a heightened risk of domestic and sexual violence. 
However, opportunities do exist to strengthen the participation of persons with disabilities in local food systems and 
pro-poor value chains.  
PART II: GENDER AND S OCIAL INCLUSION ACTION PLAN  
The programme has set a target to invest 50% of programme resources for women -led investments and 
to investments generating employment for women, and for the advisory services to achieve a gender 
balance among the end beneficiar ies in service delivery. In addition, 30% of beneficiaries are to be the 
youth.  
The objective of the  ARCAFIM GESI action plan is to ensure that women, youth and people with disabilities 
have equal opportunity to access and benefit from CCA financing as men  and to secure climate -resilient 
livelihoods. The action plan has four principal audiences:  
- end-users  of CCA finances and ARCAFIM programme services : smallholders, MSMEs and their 
communities , including women, youth and people with disabilities ; 
- financial service providers : Participating Financial Institutions (PFIs) which will be offering CCA 
financial services to the small producer and MSME markets, including host bank branches and 
agent networks, micro -finance institutions (MFIs), savings and credit coop eratives (SACCOs) and 
Fintechs ;  
- other service providers : such as advisory and extension staff, input suppliers, agribusiness es, 
vale chain actors; and  
- programme  management : ARCAFIM Facility Coordination Unit at IFAD, PMU at the host bank 
HQ and ARCAFIM fo cal points at country subsidiaries.  
 
Purpose  
The purpose of the ARCAFIM program me is to make rural climate change adaptation finance work for Africa’s future:  
• To catalyse and scale up private sector climate change adaptation financing targeting smallholders and rural 
and rural MSMEs in the agricultural sector in Africa.  
• To address  the environmental, technical, economic, social and political factors faced by smallholder 
producers and rural MSMEs to adapt and build stronger resilience to climate change and thereby bring 
about growth and opportunity.  
 
Theory of change, assumptions and ris ks


---
*Page 103*
---

Page 99 
 
   
 The GESI action plan is an integral part of the overall ARCAFIM. The programme will use the twin -track approach to 
mainstream GESI considerations into ARCAFIM operations, complemented by targeted interventions to overcome 
any specific barriers as necess ary. This approach will enable programme opportunities to be expanded to women, 
youth and persons with disabilities, while also addressing the main gaps and barriers they face to inclusion.  
The GESI theory of change is founded on the need to identify and address social norms that perpetuate GESI 
inequalities and exacerbate barriers on both the supply and demand sides of CCA financial inclusion.  GESI activities 
are present in programme component 2 (which addresses the non -financial barriers to CCA financing) and 
programme implementation arrangements.  
Early programme implementation arrangements will ensure there is sufficient capacity and skills  to deliver and track 
GESI performance through the recruitment of specialists, selection of implementin g partners, strengthening capacity 
of those responsible for programme delivery, and the monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) system.  
Early GESI actions  include conducting gender and social norms  diagnostic studies  at country and local levels to 
better  understand the context in which ARCAFIM will be operating. Gender, youth and disability lenses will be applied 
understanding social norms, CCA technologies and practices, and financial products and services. These insights will 
provide the foundation for stimulating all stakeholders (from programme staff, host agency, PFIs and service providers 
through to community members and leaders) engage in critical reflection on norms that perpetuate inequalities 
and stimulate positive behaviour change  in the inclusi on-climate -finance nexus.  
As a result of capacity building for critical reflection and behaviour change on social inclusion issues , PFIs will view 
women, youth and people with disabilities as potential eligible borrowers of CCA finance. Under sub -component 2.1, 
PFIs will address supply -side barriers  to CCA finance by seeking innovative ways to establish credit worthiness in the 
absence  of traditional collateral, providing relevant CCA products and services, and identifying digital and non -digital 
routes to access services.  
At the community, household and individual levels, the shift in gender norms and attitudes will create an enabling 
environment in which women, youth and people with disabilities will be able to make independent decisions to 
borrow money and adopt CCA technologies and practices . Under sub -component 2.2, the demand for CCA finance  
will be strengthened by developing their  climate adaptive knowledge and skills, financial literacy, entrepreneurship 
and business skills.  
Activities under sub -component 2.3 will further strengthen the enabling environment for inclusive climate finance at 
the policy level.   
The outcomes  for women  and youth as a result of accessing CCA finance will be securing climate change -resilient 
livelihoods with the benefits of food and water security ( including  reduced workloads associated with household 
tasks); improved business and employment opp ortunities  and incomes ; gains in voice and decision -making over the 
use of assets and outputs, including income generated; and reductions in harmful practices and domestic violence. 
The principal institutional gains will be the ability of the Host Bank and PFIs to expand the outr each of CCA finance 
to individuals that have traditionally been excluded, and a more conducive policy environment.  
These outcomes will feed into ARCAFIM outcome 3 (increased CC resilience), outcome 4 (improved food and water 
security) and the co -benefits 1 (increased incomes) and 2 (employment generation).  
The key assumptions  that underpin the GESI pathway moving from actions to outputs and outcomes include: 
stakeholders are willing to engage in the process of critical reflection to understand their unconscious biases and 
adapt their behaviours accordingly; the PMU and impleme nting partners embrace the GESI agenda and ensure it is 
reflected in resource allocation and tracking performance; there are sufficient skilled implementing partners and


---
*Page 104*
---

Page 100 
 
   
 service providers to deliver high quality GESI activities; and policy makers are willi ng to take measures to improve 
the policy frameworks to strengthen financial inclusion.  Upstream and downstream linkages in the food system are 
reasonably buoyant and small and medium enterprises provide opportunities for employment.  
The r isks of not add ressing social inclusion issues in CCA finance include:  
• Negative social norms would continue to disempower women, youth and people with disabilities, limiting 
their livelihood opportunities and perpetuating harmful practices including violence.  
• A significant proportion of the population would not have the ability, knowledge, skills or confidence to 
access and use CCA finance to ensure their livelihoods to become climate change secure. In turn, this 
would threaten the stability of rural communities and may trigger outmigration.  
• PFIs would miss the opportunity to expand their client base.  
 
Sub-component 2.1: Awareness raising and capacity development of PFIs for CCA finance  
Under sub -component 2.1, the action plan will ensure that GESI considerations are mainstreamed into the supply 
side of CCA finance work of the PFIs and other service providers (such as advisory services, value chain actors).  
Capacity building for critical  reflection and behaviour change among PFIs and other service providers :  
This activity will follow on from the findings of gender and social norms diagnostic studies which will be conducted 
in each country during early implementation (see implementation arrangements for further details). Using 
participatory approaches to facili tate dialogue, trust, ownership, visioning and behaviour change at various levels , 
frontline staff and management from PFIs, BDS and other service providers will critically reflect on deep -rooted social 
and gender norms and attitudes which have an adverse effect on financial and other service provision to women, 
youth and people with disabilities. This will help stimulate positive behaviour change and lay the foundation for PFI 
staff to recognise the capacity of women and others to save and borrow money fro m the formal sector, and ensure 
financial products and services are relevant to their needs.  
 
Technical support for PFIs (host bank and local intermediaries) :  
• The CCA finance strategy (including CCA financial advisory capacities) and rural outreach and social 
inclusion strategies  will specifically address how the CCA products and services will developed and 
disseminated to women, youth and people with disabilities. Methods for community  engagement and 
outreach  will be inclusive and gender responsive . Women field staff may make it easier to engage with rural 
women.  
• CCA lending products and services  (including climate risk insurance) will be tailored to the needs, resources 
and capacities  of women and youth who are small producers or micro/small entrepreneurs in the food 
system.22 The range of products will cover production, post -harvest and value addition activities for both 
household consumption and the market undertaken by smallholders and micro/small businesses. They will 
also include energy -saving and climate smart technologies to make all aspects of rural livelihoods more 
climate resilient and reduce the workload of household tasks (such as biomass production, improved stoves, 
solar l anterns, water harvesting and storage systems, micro -irrigation for home gardens, potable water, 
basic food processing).  
 
22 Financial products could be co -developed between the FPIs a nd potential clients from different backgrounds to ensure their 
relevance. For further details, see Mayoux, L and IFAD. 2017. Financial Action Learning System (FALS) An integrated 
methodology for client financial empowerment and responsible financial servi ce provision, Concept Note 
https://gamechangenetwork.org/wp -content/uploads/2017/02/FALS_IFADConceptNote_Marchweb.pdf


---
*Page 105*
---

Page 101 
 
   
 • The taxonomy framework  for end -borrower investment eligibility will be reviewed regularly for relevance 
and effectiveness from a GESI perspective. The framework will adopt a holistic livelihood approach and will 
include technologies and practices that make both the productive and domestic aspects of rural livelihoods 
more climate change resilient. Adjustments  will be made as necessary to make the framework more 
inclusive.  
• Innovative ways will be used to establish creditworthiness and assess risks  among borrowers that would 
enable resource poor households (including those enterprises headed by women and youth)  to gain access 
to finance. Alternative forms of collateral could include group security or non -land assets.  
• Repayment terms  could be adjusted to meet the capacity of borrowers and enable them to adjust to 
unexpected shocks, with longer grace periods and r epayment periods, and options for pausing repayment 
schedules without severe penalties.  
• Insurance products  will be tailored to reduce the risk of borrowing and protect agricultural investments 
against climate risk. They will be made more accessible to rura l women, youth and people with disabilities 
by insurance providers adopting new approaches, such as flexible premium payments and bundling with 
other services.  
• Build the capacity of MFIs and SACCOs  to support women, youth and people with disabilities in su stainable 
livelihood development and agribusiness , as well as members and leaders of their organizations.  
Identifying and piloting technology -enabled innovative solutions : 
• Digital financial technologies  will be used for cost -effective transactions, credit assessment and risk 
management. Account will be taken of gender - and youth -differentiated access to digital services, through 
device financing, agent networks, group ownership of tablets for accessing c limate and extension 
information (for example, by SACCOs or farmer field schools), e -vouchers etc.).  
• Advisory packages, market information, weather and climate information  will be integrated into the 
mobile banking financial service interface. The content  will be adapted to be relevant to the different needs 
of the end -users, including women, youth and people with disabilities, and disseminated in local languages.  
• Alternatives to digital services  will be developed for those with limited access to such technologies, such 
as community radios, information boards, public announcements on market days, village agents, mobile 
services etc.  
• Conduct market studies  to identify market opportunities for commodities of interest to women, youth and 
people with disabilities and support the growth of their enterprises . 
Sub-component 2.2: CCA investment support services to small producers and rural MSMEs  
Under sub -componen t 2.2, the action plan will ensure that GESI considerations are mainstreamed into the provision 
of targeted and practical technical assistance to stimulate demand for CCA finance.  
Capacity building for critical reflection and behaviour change at the commu nity level : This activity will follow on 
from the findings of gender and social norms diagnostic studies , which will be conducted in each country during 
early implementation (see implementation arrangements for further details). Participatory approaches (see box 2) 
will be used in the community to facilitate dialogue, trust, ownership, visioning and behaviour  change with 
individuals, households, groups, leaders and upstream and downstream value chain actors. They will critically reflect 
on deep -rooted social and gender norms and attitudes in order to challenge power dynamics and bring about a 
paradigm shift to wards gender equality and social inclusion, specifically around financial inclusion and adapting

 livelihoods to climate change. Some activities will explicitly engage with norm holders, men and boys to address the 
concepts of masculinity, gender and stigma . 
Box 2: Gender Action Learning System (GALS)  
GALS and household methodologies will be instrumental in enabling gender transformation amongst 
smallholders in groups and in farmer groups to empower women and men towards financial inclusion, more 
balanced wo rkloads, equitable decision making and benefits sharing. GALS tools will enable households to 
discuss the importance of climate -change adaptation and address the gender and social norms which could 
hinder access to financial products and the adoption of CC A technologies and practices in both productive 
work and household tasks.  
With the exception of the Republic of Tanzania, the participating countries have service providers with 
extensive experience of the GALS.

Small -scale agricultural producer CCA investment promoti on and financial literacy services:  
• Ensure the membership and leadership of the 16,000 rural producer groups to be trained include 50% 
women and 30% youth (of whom 50% are young women).  
• Promote inclusive leadership  in governance structures such as communi ty organizations, cooperatives, 
farmer groups, VLSAs, SACCOs and MFIs through leadership training, mentoring and developing their role 
as change agents.  
• Through participatory engagement with the community, ensure CCA products and services meet the 
priority needs and resources of different groups within the food system, including women, youth and 
people with disabilities. Understand the differentiated impac ts of climate change on rural livelihoods  and 
demand for CCA options. For example, women may prefer less risky but more labour -intensive climate 
smart technologies if they can allocate their own labour and accrue the benefits for themselves.23 They 
might p refer to invest in crops that contribute to household food and nutrition needs or new crop varieties 
or supplement traditional revenue with diversified production activities.24 In addition, they will be 
interested in technologies that will make the domesti c aspects of rural livelihoods more climate change 
resilient , such as water collection/harvesting, storage and use; fuel collection and use; food processing and 
preparation; and energy .  Consequently, they will have different needs for weather and climate 
information, training and technology options based on their access to productive assets, social and gender 
norms, and household division of labour.   
• Conduct awareness campaigns  about CCA technologies and practices to reach the widest rural audience s 
through a variety  of media, drama, demonstration plots, study visits and digital technologies. Include 
women’s groups and local information -sharing mechanisms as communication channels.

Sub-component 2.3: CCA rural finance policy dialogues  
IFAD will be leading discussions on creating an enabling environment for climate finance with the participation of 
ARACFIM financiers, the host bank, PFIs and value chain stakeholders. Links will be established for experience sharing 
with private and publi c sector rural finance platforms and other green finance initiatives in the region.

Page 104 
 
   
 This policy dialogue presents a unique opportunity to engage government and policy makers with GESI challenges 
and opportunities linked to inclusive CCA finance. Engagemen t will be b roaden ed to encourage collaboration and 
exchange between government, NGOs and civil society on the nexus between GESI, climate change and financial 
services. Key areas to address include28: 
• Any gaps in the implementation of women’s land rights . Often gender equality in access to and control over 
land is established at a constitutional level with relevant policy, legal and institutional reforms, but 
implementation remains weak at sub -national levels as a result of a lack of knowledge, inability to  access 
relevant information, and contradictions with customary laws. Actions may be required to increase 
understanding about the legal framework and strengthen implementation at the sub -national levels and 
below.  
• Women’s representation in advocacy, policy , and decision -making roles and processes . In 2019, the UNFCCC 
recognized that the ‘full, meaningful and equal participation and leadership of women in in national - and 
local -level climate policy and action is vital for achieving long -term climate goals”. However, women are 
often under -represented on such bodies; likewise for the youth and people with disabilities. Their 
participation can be increased by capacity strengthening programmes in confidence building and effective 
public speaking, the use of quota s, and ensuring meeting times and venues are suitable. It may also be 
necessary to engage with the traditional leadership on addressing any negative behavioural norms.   
 
Implementation arrangements  
The implementing structures for the GESI action plan through the host bank, Equity Bank Group, and the PFIs are 
presented in Appendices 1 and 2.  
The activities of Equity Bank Group and its Tier 2 Financial Intermediaries will be assessed based on their expected 
alignment with national and international gender frameworks such as the Bloomberg Gender -Equality Index, 2X 
Collaborative’s 2X Green toolkit, the Women Empowerment Principles (WEPs), the Gender Equality Mainstreaming 
(GEM) Framework, and ‘A Fund Manager’s Guide to Gender -Smart In vesting’ by IFC and CDC, and climate 
frameworks such as GFANZ, TCFD, the CDSB Framework, Climate Action 100+, and the Just Transition criteria from 
the Impact Investing Institute.  
Programme management.  Actions to strengthen the GESI dimensions of programme management:  
• Support for  GESI Action Plan implementation will be provided through the Technical Assistance, which 
includes capacity building at different levels including for the Host Bank, PFIs and the end beneficiaries.  
• Recruit a GESI specialist  for the PMU  at the Host B ank with responsibility to oversee: (i) the gender and 
social norms diagnostic studies followed by the development of a detailed GESI action plan; (ii) capacity 
strengthening and technical support to PMU staff and agents; (iii) the recruitment of experienc ed service 
providers support gender  mainstreaming and capacity building  at the field level; (iv) liaison with the MEL 
team; and (v) ongoing technical support during implementation.  
• Include a specific commitment to gender -responsive financial inclusion in t he terms of reference  of the 
programme manager, all staff in PMU and ARCAFIM focal points. This will be backed by capacity strengthening  
on identifying and addressing gender issues. Annual performance reviews will track staff performance on 
delivering GEWE  and GESI goals.

 • Ensure gender considerations are mainstreamed into all programme components . 
• Select implementing partners  (PFIs and other service providers) with a proven commitment to poverty
