• Seek opportunities to work with the range of regional and national  organizations supporting the growth of

Gender and social norms diagnostic studies and detailed design of the GESI strategy. In order to increase the 
financial inclusion of women, youth and people with disabilities and facilitate their economic empowerm ent, 
diagnostic studies30 will be undertaken to understand how gender and social norms influence their financial 
inclusion31 (or exclusion) and their ability to adapt to climate change.  
Based on this understanding, activities to guide critical reflection will be delivered to seek positive behaviour change 
at multiple levels – from the individual, household and community levels to PFIs, service providers and policymakers. 
These will be coupled with intentional efforts to address key demand -and supply -side b arriers which block people’s 
ability to participate fully in CCA financial inclusion initiatives.  
The studies will be context specific and conducted in each participating country. They will complement the standard 
GESI analysis which examines the symptoms of gender inequality, youth, disability and poverty issues.32 The findings

31 CGAP and FinEquity, 2022 in https://www.rfilc.org/events/tackling -the-root -causes -of-women -s-financial -exclusion/  
32 A GESI  analysis typically examines inequalities in the areas of: access and control over resources and benefits; skills; 
workloads; decision -making and leadership; household dynamics; and well -being. It describes relevant national policies, 
strategies and actors; identifies the different roles, interests and priorities of women and men; and identifies the most 
important livelihood prob lems and opportunities faced by the community, as experienced by women , men , youth and people 
with disabilities . It also take s account of the differentiated and intersectional challenges facing different groups (for example, by 
sex, age, marital status, so cio-economic status, health, disability).


---
*Page 110*
---

Page 106 
 
   
 will help shape the detailed GESI strategy to ensure that the design of CCA technologies and practices, financial 
services and delivery channels will lead to more effective  interventions to increase CCA financial inclusion.  
Capacity building and behaviour change communication for PMU and key implementing partners. Following the 
gender and social norms diagnostic studies, capacity development sessions will be undertaken with programme 
staff, government, partners and service providers (in addition to those noted under component 2.1) to initiate critical 
reflection on deep -rooted social and gender norms and attitudes, and their linkages with behaviours, in order to 
bring about a  paradigm shift towards women and CCA financial inclusion.33 
 
Monitoring, evaluation and learning. To strengthen the GESI dimensions of MEL:  
• Ensure all data in the logframe are disaggregated  by sex, age and disability, where appropriate (including targets 
and data on youth34). 
• Train staff in the collection, analysis and interpretation  of disaggregated data at various levels: from the 
communities, PFIs and other service providers, to ARCAFIM focal points at country subsidiaries, PMU at host 
bank HQ and ARCAFIM Facility Coordination Unit at IFAD. Use findings from data analysis to inform  programme 
management and activities.  
• Track performance indicators , including by sex, age, location of the borrower, loan purpose, loan disbursements 
and outstanding balances, as well as repayment performance.  
• Establish and monitor mechanisms for tracking progress on outreach targets  between the different levels of 
programme management and service providers.  
• Monitor and analyse  beneficiary outreach , paying particular attention to ensuring the average value of loans to 
women and youth are commensurate with t hose given to men for similar operations in the food system35, with 
recommendations for remedial actions if required to strengthen outreach.  
• Assess progress on GEWE and GESI  through use of the project level index for the Women’s Empowerment in 
Agriculture Index (Pro -WEAI36) or IFAD empowerment index. Monitor changes in social norms, gender roles and 
power dynamics.  
• Undertake studies on good practices  leading to gender -responsive CCA financial inclusion outcomes and 
impacts and organize knowledge management and sharing events  to disseminate findings.  
• Ensure GESI issues and financial inclusion actions are addressed during supervision missions and mid -term 
review . Make provision for additional TA if it is recognised that the programme is failing to deliver on GESI 
targets during the mid -term review.  
 
33 For example, Care’s Social Analysis and Action methodology facilitates dialogue, enabling critical thinking and reflection 
about how social conditions perpetuate food and nutrition insecurity and development chal lenges.  https://www.care.org/our -
work/health/strengthening -healthcare/social -analysis -and-action -saa/  
34 The youth target of 30% of beneficiaries needs to be disaggregated by sex; that is, 50% of the youth target is young women.  
35 It is important to ensure that: (i) the targets for women and youth are met through their substantive engagement in 
programme activities, such as entrepreneurs, not solely as minor beneficiaries, such as employees in an agribusiness; (ii) 
women and youth have a proportionate share of the higher value loans and are not disproportionally clustered at the lower 
end; and (iii) the funds available for MSMEs are not totally consumed by the larger enterprises.  
36 Pro-WEAI is a composite index, which is based on 1 2 indicators of women’s empowerment in agriculture: autonomy in 
income, self -efficacy, attitudes about domestic violence, input in productive decisions, ownership of land and other assets, 
access to and decisions on credit, control over use of income, work  balance, visiting important locations, group membership, 
membership in influential groups, and respect among household members.

ARCAFIM GENDER AND SOCIAL INCLUSION ACTION STRATEGY    
Activities  Indicators & Targets  Timeline  Responsibilities  Costs  
 Impact Statement: The ARCAFIM Program will seek to deploy innovative financial products along with Technical 
Assistance to scale up CCA finance through the local private financial sector in selected East African (EA) countries 
to address the unmet needs of rural producers i nvolved in MSME food systems. ARCAFIM will mobilize US$ 270 
million in CCA investments and $ 30 million in TA to enable vulnerable communities, including women and girls, to 
identify, mitigate and adapt or increase their resilience to the negative impacts of climate change. Concurrently, 
ARCAFIM will seek to create opportunities to enhance livelihoods, particularly for women and youth. In ensuring 
equitable access to the benefits of the project, including employment and income generating activities, ARCAFIM  
will identify the potential impact and technical assistance where possible, to support disenfranchised groups, 
including women, to access climate resiliency tools and knowledge products to facilitate climate -smart 
innovation. This will be done through the  stakeholder engagement articulated in Annex 7 of the ARCAFIM funding 
proposal and the capacity building provided under the Technical Assistance. ARCAFIM will enable local businesses, 
including women -owned and/or led enterprises to adapt their existing met hods, expand local markets and 
demand, as well as attract further investment, especially for yet to be identified adaptation products and services.   
Outcome Statement: ARCAFIM will secure climate change -resilient livelihoods with the benefits of food and 
water security (including reduced workloads associated with household tasks); improved business and 
employment opportunities and incomes; gains in voice and decision -making over the use of assets and outputs, 
including income generated; and reductions in h armful practices and domestic violence.  ARCAFIM will report 
sex-disaggregated program level impacts to the extent possible and ensure that end beneficiary vulnerable 
groups are protected against harmful practices such as child labour and exploitation, in o rder to meet both 
national and International Labour Organization legal requirements. ARCAFIM will also achieve the following 
gender -responsive outcomes:  
1. Sustainable communities: ARCAFIM shall play a critical role in providing an avenue to collectively targ et 
and empower women/youth/IPs, build on their agency and operationalize this GESI Action strategy, 
through strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk -reduction processes as well as providing 
access to women, youth and IP associations and village g roups in East Africa. The organizations will in 
turn provide access to training, collaborative efforts on tackling different aspects of climate adaptation 
such as land planning, agro -forestry, soil conservation practices and energy saving stoves and techno logy. 
A more detailed list of organizations can be accessed in Annex 4.   
2. More balanced control over the four Factors of Production: ARCAFIM shall play a crucial role in promoting 
a balance of control between men and women program end beneficiaries over th e factors of production, 
thereby facilitating their participation in decision -making processes.  
3. Mitigate the disproportionate impact of climate change on vulnerable groups (women and young girls):


---
*Page 112*
---

Page 108 
 
   
 
Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most v ulnerable people and communities in East 
Africa via opportunities benefiting women and girls, leads to their enhanced access to resources and 
economic engagement. Examples of these opportunities include access to financial resources, including 
credit, to i ncrease their economic power, and promotion of equal pay for equal work.  
4. Increase the climate resilience and adaptation capacity for vulnerable communities, including women, 
girls and indigenous communities in EA: via tools to support them in the adoption  of distributed, data -
based, and efficient water technologies, and more resilient farming practices, in order to enhance access 
to reliable and safe water supply, despite climate shocks and stresses.    
5. Mainstreaming gender in concessional finance for climate change adaptation and related technical 
advice: Apply a gender and climate lens across the investment process and ARCAFIM implementing 
activities of Equity Bank Group and the Participating Financial Intermediaries (e.g. KWFT, Faulu, Rafiki, 
TADB), via Gender and Climate Policies, as well as a Gender -Smart Climate Financing Toolkit to be used by 
the bank to disburse direct and indirect concessional finance for climate adaptation and gender -smart 
techni cal advice to smallholders and rural micro -, small - and medium -sized enterprises (MSMEs) in the 
agricultural sector in East Africa.  
 
The overall budget required for the implementation of the GSIAP is estimated as USD 960,000. This amount is 
mostly included  in the TA capacity building budget with the remainder in the Monitoring and Evaluation budget of 
ARCAFIM.  
Activities:  Indicators & Targets:  Timeline:  Responsibilities:  Costs (USD)

ANNE X 3: Organisations Support ing Women Entrepreneurs in East Africa  
REGIONAL  
Organisation                        Description  
Eastern Africa 
Farmers 
Federation 
(EAFF)  EAFF is a corporate members -based regional farmers’ organization founded in 2001. It is 
registered in Kenya under the Societies Act, with country chapters in each of its member 
countries which includes all the ARCAFIM’s focus countries in East Africa i.e., Kenya, 
Tanzania, Uganda and Rwanda. EAFF’s establishment was triggered by the development 
imperatives of the late 1990s and early 2000, especially the need to strengthen mechanisms 
for regional collective acti on to provide an opportunity for farmer organizations to engage 
at the regional level. One of the organization’s core mandates includes active engagement 
of women and youth in agricultural development regionally.  
Africa Women 
Agribusiness 
Network 
(AWAN) Afrika  AWAN Afrika is a non -profit organization limited by guarantee. Its network comprises 
individual members’ businesses including producers, processor s, aggregators, export 
companies, and input suppliers among others across the continent and globally. The 
organization provides women -owned and youth -owned agribusinesses with an E -Hub, 
which is a repository of information on agriculture along value chains  and supply chains. 
AWAN Afrika Under 30 champions involves youth of either gender engaged in agribusiness.  
UN Women  
 UN Women supports regional institutions, governments, and other stakehold ers in setting 
up and applying a comprehensive and dynamic set of global norms, policies and standards 
on gender equality and empowerment of women.  
Through policy and technical advisory services and regional engagement, the Regional 
Office partners with re gional bodies to support women’s participation in intergovernmental 
policy processes such as the regional preparations for the Commission on the Status of 
Women - CSW, the Rio+20 follow up, the Beijing+20 review, and the Post 2015 
development agenda. These  processes are linked to national level gender equality and 
women empowerment advocacy.  
ANDE GLOBAL  Through the Accelerating Women’s Climate Entrepreneurs Progra m, ANDE global hopes to 
build the gender -lens investing ecosystem for women climate entrepreneurs to grow 
scalable climate -related businesses in Sub -Saharan Africa.  
 
KENYA  
Organisation                        Description  
Association of 
Women in 
Agriculture - AWAK  was conceived on the precept of scal ing up agribusinesses from conventional 
subsistence farming to advanced climate smart agriculture, urban food security, value 
addition and advocacy in the quest of achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)


---
*Page 132*
---

Page 128 
 
   
 
Kenya AWAK  both at household and national levels.  
Women Farmers 
Association of 
Kenya (WoFaAK)  WoFaAK  is the national umbrella organization of women farmers in Kenya. WoFaAK is a non -
political, non -government and non -religious member -based organization. The association 
provides the women farmers of Kenya with a platform to articulate issues affecting them in 
agriculture. The goal of WoFaAK is to address issues that impede women farmers from 
undertaking both profitable and sustainable agricultural enterprises with a view to improve 
their livelihoods.  
Agriculture 
Sector Network 
(ASNET)  Agriculture Sector Network (ASNET) is the umbrella body representing the collective voice of 
the agriculture sector in Kenya. ASNET serves as the agriculture sector board for the  Kenya 
Private Sector Alliance (KEPSA).  
ASNET’s key role is to coordinate agriculture sector actors in Kenya through various 
mechanisms to engage in policy advocacy and value chain development that promote 
increased productivity, competitiveness and attracts investments into the agriculture sect or.  
ASNET functions through a network of agencies and organisations such as AAK Grow, 
Agricultural Employers Association, Agricultural Society of Kenya, Association of Kenya Feeds 
Manufacturers (AKEFEMA), Avocado Society of Kenya, Cereal Growers Associati on (CGA), 
Cereal Millers Association (CMA), Commercial Aquaculture Society of Kenya (CASK), East 
African Tea Trade Association, Elgon Kenya Ltd, Kenya Association of Manufacturers (KAM), 
Kenya Camel Association, Kenya Flower Council (KFC), Kenya National F armers' Federation, 
Kenya Tea Growers Association, Kenya Camel Association (KCA), Lake Naivasha Growers 
Group, Micro Enterprises Support Programme Trust (MESPT), National Coffee Platform, 
National Potatoes Council of Kenya, Seed Trade Association of Kenya and the Ustadi 
Foundation.  
The Kenya 
National 
Farmers’ 
Federation 
(KENAFF)  The Kenya National Farmers’ Federation (KENAFF) is the apex, umbrella farmers’ 
organization in Kenya; also known as the National Farmers’ Organization (NFO). It was 
founded in 1946 to represent, articulate, promote, and protect the interests of Kenyan 
through lobby, advocacy, policy action and farmer empowerment. The federation also 
deploys a wide range of member servic es:  
Capacity building on organizational development for farmer groups/associations/enterprises.  
Technical advisory and market facilitation.  
Facilitating linkages between farmers and credit and finance institutions.  
KENAFF’s membership comprises smallhol der farmers organized in Producer Business 
Groups (PBGs); County Farmers Associations (CFAs); apex Commodity Associations (CAs) 
Cooperative Societies; Farmers’ Unions; farmer -owned business entities; and, large -scale 
producers. The current membership is ap proximately 1.6 million farm families spread across 
all the 47 counties and the eleven (11) KENAFF regions.


---
*Page 133*
---

Page 129 
 
   
 
 
RWANDA  
Organisation                        Description  
Rwanda 
Women’s 
Network  Rwanda Women’s Network was founded in 1995 to end gender inequality in Rwanda by 
empowering women to be positive transformation agents in their homes, communities, and 
the nation at large. One of the organization’s key p illars is socio -economic transformation 
which focuses on enhancing access to knowledge and building financial skills as keys to the 
mobilization of gender roles and economic growth in Rwanda. The organization’s programs 
provide financial education and inve st in the development of women led local small 
businesses.  
Rwanda Youth 
in Agribusiness 
Forum (RYAF)  RYAF is a platform established to bring together different youth organizations, individual 
youth farmers and entrepreneurs in Agriculture Sector working in one or many of the 
following sub -sectors: crop production, lives tock, agro -processing, inputs and other Agro -
services (extension, marketing, food packaging, farm mechanization, seed multiplication etc.) 
as well as ICT for Agriculture in Rwanda. The platform aims to change the old mind -set 
among the youth vis -à-vis the agricultural sector in Rwanda, while orienting youth to reach 
out to other farming groups to raise awareness on the practice of business -oriented 
agriculture.  
Youth 
Engagement in 
Agriculture 
Network 
(YEAN)  YEAN is a Youth -Led Agriculture social enterprise created to empower farmers and 
agripreneurs through capacity building, foster innovation, optimization of information 
availability, quality agriculture input access, efficient crop, and animal production 
technologies deployment towards contributing to the SDG Goal 2: Ending hunger, achieving 
food security, improving nutrition, and promoting sustainable agriculture through engaging 
youth as agents of change.  
 
TANZANIA  
Organisation                        Description  
Tanzania Youth 
in Agribusiness 
Association 
(TYAA)  TYAA is an umbrella association formally registered in 2022 whose membership base 
constitute of valued youth led enterprises, registered firms and initiatives in agribusiness and 
farm related value chains including cooperatives, services providers, professionals, 
processors, exporters and related agropreneurs.  
IITA Tanzania 
Youth 
Agripreneurs 
(ITYA)  ITYA was formed in March 2014 to change the mindset and attitudes of young people 
towards agriculture and empowering them to be more productive by setting up and running 
successful agriculture businesses and becoming job creators. The group is engaged in 
different agribusinesses, including: packing of high quality cassava flour and selling under the 
brand ‘Mpishi Mkuu’, (the great cook; www.mpishimkuu.com ), production of tomatoes


---
*Page 134*
---

Page 130 
 
   
 
under a greenhouse and drip irrigation, supply of clean cassava planting mater ial, processing 
of soybean to products such as milk, yogurt, and tofu, providing weed control services to 
farmers using appropriate and safe herbicides, and the use of modern techniques (hermetic 
bags) for storage of maize and training farmers on making of  high nutrient -dense foods from 
locally grown crops.  
 
UGANDA  
Organisation                        Description  
Support for 
Women in 
Agriculture and 
Environment 
(SWAGEN)  SWAGEN is an indigenous organization founded and owned by grassroots women in 
Uganda. The organization came into existence in 1998 out of a felt need to go beyond 
engaging women at the National level due to the prolifera tion of women focused NGOs at 
the national level. Its mission is to create strong women groups capable of active 
participation in and meaningful contribution to mainstream national development.  
SWAGEN advocates for a transition to agroecology as the food p roduction model in Africa 
as opposed to industrial agriculture. Agroecology is a people -centred system of sustainable 
agriculture combining indigenous knowledge with cutting edge science, making the best 
use of nature to create healthy communities. SWAGEN supports small -scale food producers 
to build a sustainable, resilient, diverse, healthy, productive, and culturally appropriate 
food system for Africa.  
Association of 
Uganda 
Professional 
Women in 
Agriculture and 
Environment 
(AUPWAE)   AUPWAE is an association of professional women qualified and with vast experience in the 
fields of Agriculture, Forestry, Fisheries, Food Science and Technology, Environmental 
Science and Veterinary Medicine. Founded in 1992, it currently boasts of a membership of 
300 professional women working in both private and public sectors in all regions of the 
country. One of their key objectives is to enhance the ability of rural women to acquire and 
utilize a ppropriate knowledge, technology and skills for improved food security, income 
generation and environment conservation. The organization promotes training on 
entrepreneurship skills and business planning, value chain development and 
implementation, institu tional capacity development, resource mobilization and in improved 
agricultural and environment conservation technologies.  
Rural Women 
Empowerment 
Network 
(ROWONET)  
 Rural Women Empowerment Network (ROWONET) advances equity for women and girls 
throughout rural Uganda through research, advocacy, capacity building, and networking. A 
membership organization comprising 179 women -led organizations across Uganda that 
represent the communities RUWONET serves, all members share a common vision: 
equality  and socio -economic justice for women and girls. Some of the key focus areas by 
ROWONET include promoting climate smart agriculture, enhancing access to clean water 
drinking water and promoting the use of renewable energy technologies in the rural areas.  
Uganda 
Women's Trust  UWT links the lack of economic empowerment among women to the current development 
challenges such as gender -based violence; education (quality, access, dropout rates, 
retention), corruption, maternal mortality among others. UWT has thr ee approaches to


---
*Page 135*
---

Page 131 
 
   
 
women’s economic empowerment:  
Building capacity of the women in agriculture for both household food security and 
household income, providing inputs and linking them to relevant services like agriculture 
training centres, and marketing grou ps.   
Building entrepreneurship skills among the women and encouraging the women to work in 
groups to save and be loanable.  
Enhancing adult learning and skills development so that the women can negotiate, 
participate and make informed decisions in all aspe cts of their social, political and 
economic lives.  
Organization for 
Rural 
Development 
(ORUDE)  ORUDE is a grassroots NGO in south -eastern Uganda. Formed in 1997, the organization had 
been mobilizing and training rural women's economic groups and preparing  them to absorb 
credit. It then helped the groups to open savings accounts with microfinance institutions 
(MFIs) from which they hoped their members could access credit.  
Youth Go Green  Youth Go Green is a youth -led umbrella organization of youth engaged in climate change, 
resilience, green growth, environment protection, SDGs, and youth empowerment 
activities in Uganda & African countries. The organiza tion's mission is to empower and 
engage young people in pursuance of low carbon socio economic development pathway. 
Some of its key thematic areas relevant to the project include climate change adaptation 
and mitigation, biodiversity conservation, green gr owth and youth skilling and 
empowerment.  
The Young 
Farmers’ 
Federation of 
Uganda (UNYFA)  UNYFA is an umbrella body for young farmers in Uganda, established in 2016, and launched 
in June 201 7, as a duly incorporated member -based organization, with 54 members 
registered as District Young Farmers’ Associations (DYFAs), farmer youth groups and school 
agricultural clubs, with a total of over 35,000 individual young farmers across the country. 
The organization is driven by the desire to have a holistically transformed youth in 
agriculture for a sustainable economy: its target group is of youth between 12 to 39 years 
of age who are rural and/or urban agro -based and young farmers in and out of school / 
Institutions. UNYFA provides platforms for these youths where they can express and 
acquire tailor -made training to enhance their livelihoods.  
Young Farmers 
Champions 
Network 
(YOFCHAN)  YOFCHAN is a network of young Agri -entrepreneurs (14 - 40 years) working together to 
shape the future of agribusiness. It was formed to support young farmers with the 
necessary entrepreneurial skills to run agri -business enterprises, incubate their agribusiness 
ideas into viable businesses, provide affordable fin ances and link them to sustainable 
markets for them to embrace farming as a profitable business.
